# westata — complete API reference

_westata version: 0.3.17 · Generated by `docs/render_api_reference.py`_

Every public name in `westata`, with the Stata command it corresponds to, a description, and a runnable example with the output it actually returns.

Signatures and example outputs are read from the code itself, so they match the version you are reading.

The examples run against a 200-row frame called `base`, whose columns are `y x1 x2 x3 yb ycnt ycens lo hi y1 y2 firm year ypanel treat ytreat sel yselect`; `ws`, `np` and `pd` are in scope.

## Robust standard errors: Stata's `robust` versus `HC0`/`HC1`

> **Read this before comparing a robust standard error against Stata.** The same Stata option
> name maps to a DIFFERENT small-sample factor depending on the command, and mixing them up
> produces standard errors that look plausible and are wrong in the third digit.

### The mapping

| Stata | corresponds to | why |
|---|---|---|
| `regress ..., vce(robust)` | **`cov_type="HC1"`** | Stata's `regress` multiplies by `n/(n-k)` |
| `regress ..., vce(robust) small` | not accepted by Stata | `small` is ivregress-only |
| `ivregress ..., vce(robust)` | **`cov_type="HC0"`** | Stata's `ivregress` does NOT apply the `n/(n-k)` factor unless `small` is given |
| `ivregress ..., vce(robust) small` | `cov_type="HC1"` | the factor Stata applies with `small` |
| `sureg ..., cov_type="robust"` | the literal label `"robust"` | its sandwich comes from `_reg3robust`, a separate implementation, not HC1 |

### Panel estimators: `vce(robust)` and `vce(cluster id)` coincide

A third pattern, and the one most likely to surprise: for `xtreg ..., fe` Stata's `vce(robust)` and
`vce(cluster id)` produce the SAME standard error, because the fixed effects have already been
differenced out and the robust VCE is therefore clustered by construction. Measured on a 40x10
balanced panel:

```
xtreg y x1, fe                      0.05440432377516
xtreg y x1, fe vce(robust)          0.05229223550829
xtreg y x1, fe vce(cluster sid)     0.05229223550829   <- identical to robust
```

`PanelOLS` matches all three, and reports `cov_type="robust"` for both `robust` and `HC1` because
they ARE the same estimator here -- HC0/HC1 do not name a separate correction in this family. Do not
read `cov_type="robust"` on a panel result as "HC1"; read it as "the panel-robust VCE, which for
`fe` equals clustering on the entity".

### The measurement behind the table

On `grilic.dta`, standard error of `s`:

```
reg lnw s expr, vce(robust)                     0.00638056877997
    westata .fit(cov_type="HC1")               0.00638056877997   <- matches
    westata .fit(cov_type="HC0")               0.00636792980662   <- does not

ivregress 2sls lnw expr (s = rns mrt), vce(robust)   0.04061066342746
    westata .fit(cov_type="HC0")                     0.04061066342742   <- matches
    westata .fit(cov_type="HC1")                     0.04069126687443   <- does not
```

Note the two families pick OPPOSITE flavours for the same spelling. `regress` is HC1; `ivregress`
is HC0.

### Writing `cov_type="robust"` instead of a number

Both flavours are also reachable by the Stata spelling, resolved per command family so the caller
does not have to remember the table:

```python
ws.OLS.from_formula("lnw ~ s + expr", df).fit(cov_type="robust").cov_type      # 'HC1'
ws.IV2SLS.from_formula("lnw ~ expr + [s ~ rns + mrt]", df).fit(
    cov_type="robust"
).cov_type                                                                    # 'HC0'
```

The result stores the CANONICAL name, not the alias you typed -- echoing `"robust"` back would
reproduce exactly the confusion this section exists to prevent.

### What is refused, and why

`cov_type="HC2"` and `"HC3"` raise for IV estimators: Stata's `ivregress` has no leverage-corrected
robust VCE, so reporting an HC0 number under an HC2 label would be a fabrication rather than an
approximation. `e(df_r)` stores `N` for the same class of reason -- Stata leaves it missing unless
`small` is given, and a stored missing cannot be represented in the statsmodels surface.

## Linear models and IV

### `OLS`

**Stata:** `regress y x1 x2`

Ordinary least squares -- Stata's `regress`. Prints coefficients, standard errors, t statistics, p-values and confidence intervals, plus the ANOVA and fit blocks (`N`, `F`, `R²`, adjusted `R²`, root MSE). This is the reference implementation for the linear family: the small-sample correction, the `df_r` convention and the sum-of-squares block follow `[R] regress`, and the estimators that wrap a backend reuse those conventions rather than re-deriving them.

**Signature**

```python
ws.OLS(endog: 'Any', exog: 'Any', *, weights: 'Any' = None, weight_type: 'str | None' = None, param_names: 'Sequence[str] | None' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`OLS.from_formula`**

```python
OLS.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, weights: 'Any' = None, weight_type: 'str | None' = None, subset: 'Any' = None) -> "'OLS'"
```

**`OLS.fit`**

```python
OLS.fit(self, cov_type: 'str | None' = None, cov_kwds: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, ssc: 'SSC | None' = None, level: 'float | None' = None) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text

      Source |       SS           df       MS      Number of obs   =       200
-------------+----------------------------------   F(2, 197)       =     91.00
       Model |  200.183699         2  100.091849   Prob > F        =    0.0000
    Residual |  216.683448       197  1.09991598   R-squared       =    0.4802
-------------+----------------------------------   Adj R-squared   =    0.4749
       Total |  416.867146       199  2.09480978   Root MSE        =    1.0488

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
           y | Coefficient  Std. err.      t    P>|t|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
          x1 |   .8406445   .0724711    11.60   0.000     .6977258    .9835633
          x2 |   -.512087   .0748223    -6.84   0.000    -.6596425   -.3645315
       _cons |   1.005003   .0744285    13.50   0.000     .8582236    1.151781
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
```

**Notes:** `regress` stores no total sum of squares, so there is no `e(tss)`: the `SS_total` row is reconstructed as `mss + rss`. That is exact in a model with a constant, because the decomposition is additive there.

### `FEOLS`

**Stata:** `reghdfe y x1 x2, absorb(firm year)`

Fixed-effects OLS with high-dimensional fixed effects -- Stata's `reghdfe`. The fixed-effect block is written after a `|` in the formula (`'y ~ x1 + x2 | firm + year'`), and absorption is PyFixest's alternating projections. `cov_type` accepts `nonrobust`, `HC0`, `HC1` and `cluster` only: `HC2`/`HC3` need the leverage of the full dummy design, which the backend does not expose for absorbed models, so they are refused rather than approximated. It predicts: `xb` (the default) is the linear predictor PLUS the absorbed effect, `d` is the absorbed effect alone, and `residuals`/`dresiduals` follow from those, per `areg_p.ado:147-158` (the same estimator with one absorbed dimension).

**Signature**

```python
ws.FEOLS(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, weights: 'Any' = None, weight_type: 'str | None' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`FEOLS.from_formula`**

```python
FEOLS.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, weights: 'Any' = None, weight_type: 'str | None' = None, subset: 'Any' = None) -> "'FEOLS'"
```

**`FEOLS.fit`**

```python
FEOLS.fit(self, cov_type: 'str | None' = None, cov_kwds: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, ssc: 'SSC | None' = None, level: 'float | None' = None) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.FEOLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2 | firm + year', base).fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text
(MWFE estimator converged in 1 iterations)

HDFE Linear regression                            Number of obs     =        200
Absorbing 2 HDFE groups                           F(   2,     154)  =      64.05
                                                  Prob > F          =     0.0000
                                                  R-squared         =     0.6125
                                                  Adj R-squared     =     0.4992
                                                  Within R-sq.      =     0.4541
                                                  Root MSE          =     1.0242

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
           y | Coefficient  Std. err.      t    P>|t|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
          x1 |   .7872055   .0809428     9.73   0.000     .6273039    .9471072
          x2 |  -.4812685   .0857135    -5.61   0.000    -.6505945   -.3119426

Absorbed degrees of freedom:
-----------------------------------------------------+
 Absorbed FE | Categories  - Redundant  = Num. Coefs |
-------------+---------------------------------------|
        firm |         45           1         44     |
        year |         45           1         44     |
-----------------------------------------------------+
```

**Notes:** `e(rank)` counts every absorbed fixed-effect parameter, so it is larger than `len(results.params)`, while `e(df_m)` counts only reported slopes. With `weight_type='pweight'` and no explicit `cov_type`, `fit` selects HC1 -- Stata's `pweight` implies `vce(robust)`. `predict` registers no types for `reghdfe`, so predictions are not available through it. Predicting is refused when MORE THAN ONE dimension is absorbed: the multi-way effect is identified only up to a normalisation, so the refusal names that reason rather than pretending the estimator cannot predict at all.

### `PanelOLS`

**Stata:** `xtreg ypanel x1 x2, fe`

Fixed-effects panel regression -- Stata's `xtreg, fe`. `fit(entity_effects=True)` is the default. The formula implies a constant exactly as in `OLS`, so `e(b)` includes `Intercept` even though the within transformation absorbs it; write `'ypanel ~ x1 + x2 - 1'` to follow Stata's `noconstant`. Rows with missing values are dropped listwise before estimation, so `e(N)` can be smaller than `len(data)`, and the summary prints the `sigma_u`/`sigma_e`/`rho` block and the F test that all `u_i` are zero.

**Signature**

```python
ws.PanelOLS(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, entity: 'str', time: 'str', weights: 'Any' = None, weight_type: 'str | None' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`PanelOLS.from_formula`**

```python
PanelOLS.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, entity: 'str', time: 'str', subset: 'Any' = None, weights: 'Any' = None, weight_type: 'str | None' = None) -> "'PanelOLS'"
```

**`PanelOLS.fit`**

```python
PanelOLS.fit(self, cov_type: 'str | None' = None, cov_kwds: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, ssc: 'SSC | None' = None, *, entity_effects: 'bool' = True, time_effects: 'bool' = False) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.PanelOLS.from_formula('ypanel ~ x1 + x2', base, entity='firm', time='year').fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text

Fixed-effects (within) regression               Number of obs     =        200
Group variable: firm                            Number of groups  =         40

R-squared:                                      Obs per group:
     Within  = 0.3670                                         min =          5
     Between = 0.2358                                         avg =        5.0
     Overall = 0.2956                                         max =          5

                                                F(2, 158)         =      45.79
corr(u_i, Xb) = 0.0844                          Prob > F          =     0.0000

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      ypanel | Coefficient  Std. err.      t    P>|t|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
          x1 |   .7088762   .0749043     9.46   0.000     .5609333    .8568191
          x2 |  -.1092899   .0816583    -1.34   0.183    -.2705726    .0519929
       _cons |   .8444225   .0703358    12.01   0.000     .7055029    .9833422
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
     sigma_u |  .90040934
     sigma_e |  .99008668
         rho |  .45267062   (fraction of variance due to u_i)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
F test that all u_i=0: F(39, 158) = 4.10                     Prob > F = 0.0000
```

**Notes:** One deliberate deviation, documented on the class: under `cov_type='cluster'`, westata stores `e(df_m)` as the number of slopes and `e(rank)` as `len(e(b))`, where clustered Stata stores both one lower (the class docstring records the StataNow 19.5 measurements). `e(F)`, `e(df_b)` and `e(df_r)` do agree -- only those two labels differ, and copying Stata's would print an F label that contradicts the p-value beside it.

### `RandomEffects`

**Stata:** `xtreg ypanel x1 x2, re`

Random-effects GLS -- Stata's `xtreg, re`. Variance components come from the backend's default Swamy-Arora estimator; the optional small-sample refinement is deliberately left off so the estimator is exactly that documented default. The summary reports `sigma_u`, `sigma_e`, `rho` and the correlation of `u_i` with the linear index, with a Wald chi-square test in the header rather than the F that `xtreg, fe` prints; `e(theta)` holds the quasi-demeaning factor the fit applied.

**Signature**

```python
ws.RandomEffects(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, entity: 'str', time: 'str', weights: 'Any' = None, weight_type: 'str | None' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`RandomEffects.from_formula`**

```python
RandomEffects.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, entity: 'str', time: 'str', subset: 'Any' = None, weights: 'Any' = None, weight_type: 'str | None' = None) -> "'PanelOLS'"
```

**`RandomEffects.fit`**

```python
RandomEffects.fit(self, cov_type: 'str | None' = None, cov_kwds: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, ssc: 'SSC | None' = None, *, entity_effects: 'bool' = False, time_effects: 'bool' = False) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.RandomEffects.from_formula('ypanel ~ x1 + x2', base, entity='firm', time='year').fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text

Random-effects GLS regression                   Number of obs     =        200
Group variable: firm                            Number of groups  =         40

R-squared:                                      Obs per group:
     Within  = 0.3669                                         min =          5
     Between = 0.2363                                         avg =        5.0
     Overall = 0.2957                                         max =          5

                                                Wald chi2(2)      =     101.64
corr(u_i, X) = 0 (assumed)                      Prob > chi2       =     0.0000

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      ypanel | Coefficient  Std. err.      z    P>|z|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
          x1 |   .7277599    .073114     9.95   0.000     .5844592    .8710606
          x2 |   -.119745   .0784768    -1.53   0.127    -.2735566    .0340667
       _cons |   .8440041   .1428722     5.91   0.000     .5639798    1.124028
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
     sigma_u |  .78751004
     sigma_e |  .99008668
         rho |  .38750008   (fraction of variance due to u_i)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
```

### `BetweenOLS`

**Stata:** `xtreg ypanel x1 x2, be`

Between estimator -- Stata's `xtreg, be`. Runs one OLS on entity means, so a 200-row panel with 40 entities is estimated on 40 observations: `e(N)` is the number of group means actually consumed and `e(df_r) = n_entities - K`. `e()` stores the three panel R-squareds (`r2_b`, `r2_o`, `r2_w`), but there is no `sigma_u`/`sigma_e`/`rho` block: a regression on means has no within variation to decompose.

**Signature**

```python
ws.BetweenOLS(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, entity: 'str', time: 'str', weights: 'Any' = None, weight_type: 'str | None' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`BetweenOLS.from_formula`**

```python
BetweenOLS.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, entity: 'str', time: 'str', subset: 'Any' = None, weights: 'Any' = None, weight_type: 'str | None' = None) -> "'PanelOLS'"
```

**`BetweenOLS.fit`**

```python
BetweenOLS.fit(self, cov_type: 'str | None' = None, cov_kwds: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, ssc: 'SSC | None' = None, *, entity_effects: 'bool' = False, time_effects: 'bool' = False) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.BetweenOLS.from_formula('ypanel ~ x1 + x2', base, entity='firm', time='year').fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text

Between regression (regression on group means)  Number of obs     =        200
Group variable: firm                            Number of groups  =         40

R-squared:                                      Obs per group:
     Within  = 0.3654                                         min =          5
     Between = 0.2375                                         avg =        5.0
     Overall = 0.2954                                         max =          5

                                                F(2,37)           =       5.76
sd(u_i + avg(e_i.)) = .9034525                  Prob > F          =     0.0066

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      ypanel | Coefficient  Std. err.      t    P>|t|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
          x1 |   1.128085   .3429968     3.29   0.002     .4331071    1.823062
          x2 |  -.2568295   .2873123    -0.89   0.377    -.8389796    .3253206
       _cons |   .8297597   .1453072     5.71   0.000     .5353393     1.12418
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
```

### `FirstDifferenceOLS`

**Stata:** `xtreg ypanel x1 x2, fd`

First-difference estimator -- Stata's `xtreg, fd`. Every period is differenced against the immediately preceding one (`t` against `t - 1`, so a missing period is not bridged) and the model is fitted without a constant; the patsy intercept is therefore removed before the design reaches the backend, which rejects a constant in a first-difference regression. On a 40-entity, 5-period panel that leaves 160 usable observations, which is what `e(N)` reports.

**Signature**

```python
ws.FirstDifferenceOLS(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, entity: 'str', time: 'str', weights: 'Any' = None, weight_type: 'str | None' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`FirstDifferenceOLS.from_formula`**

```python
FirstDifferenceOLS.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, entity: 'str', time: 'str', subset: 'Any' = None, weights: 'Any' = None, weight_type: 'str | None' = None) -> "'PanelOLS'"
```

**`FirstDifferenceOLS.fit`**

```python
FirstDifferenceOLS.fit(self, cov_type: 'str | None' = None, cov_kwds: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, ssc: 'SSC | None' = None, *, entity_effects: 'bool' = False, time_effects: 'bool' = False) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.FirstDifferenceOLS.from_formula('ypanel ~ x1 + x2', base, entity='firm', time='year').fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text

Group variable: .                                 Number of groups =      40
                                                                Obs per group:
                                                                    min =       5
                                                                    avg =       5
                                                                    max =       5
Log likelihood = -274.0773697                        Prob > chi2      =  0.0000

Number of obs         =        160
F(2, 158)             =      40.91
Prob > F              =     0.0000
R-squared             =     0.3412
Adj R-squared         =     0.3370
Root MSE              =     1.3503

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      ypanel | Coefficient  Std. err.      t    P>|t|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
          x1 |   .6218778    .069845     8.90   0.000     .4839276     .759828
          x2 |  -.1015749   .0754635    -1.35   0.180    -.2506223    .0474726
```

**Notes:** The formula implies a constant the way every patsy formula does, and it is dropped silently: there is no option to keep one, and `e(b)` therefore holds slopes only.

### `IV2SLS`

**Stata:** `ivregress 2sls ytreat x1 (treat = x3)`

Two-stage least squares -- Stata's `ivregress 2sls`. The formula uses linearmodels' grammar, `'ytreat ~ x1 + [treat ~ x3]'`; Stata's `(endog = instrument)` spelling is rejected, because the grammar is delegated to the backend instead of reimplemented. Exogenous regressors, endogenous regressors and excluded instruments are named separately, and `e(b)` is reported in Stata's order -- endogenous first, included exogenous after, `_cons` last -- even though the design matrix is built the other way round.

**Signature**

```python
ws.IV2SLS(endog: 'Any', exog: 'Any', endog_instr: 'Any', instruments: 'Any', *, param_names: 'Sequence[str] | None' = None, weights: 'Any' = None, weight_type: 'str | None' = None, **kwargs: 'Any') -> 'None'
```

**`IV2SLS.from_formula`**

```python
IV2SLS.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', **kwargs: 'Any') -> "'IV2SLS'"
```

**`IV2SLS.fit`**

```python
IV2SLS.fit(self, cov_type: 'str | None' = None, cov_kwds: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, ssc: 'SSC | None' = None, level: 'float | None' = None) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.IV2SLS.from_formula('ytreat ~ x1 + [treat ~ x3]', base).fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text

Instrumental-variables 2SLS regression            Number of obs   =        200
                                                  Wald chi2(2)    =     135.14
                                                  Prob > chi2     =     0.0000
                                                  R-squared       =     0.5247
                                                  Root MSE        =     .98083

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      ytreat | Coefficient  Std. err.      z    P>|z|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
       treat |   1.444141   .2839408     5.09   0.000     .8876271    2.000654
          x1 |   .7721013   .0689206    11.20   0.000     .6370194    .9071831
       _cons |   .9463986   .1952162     4.85   0.000     .5637819    1.329015
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Endogenous: treat
Exogenous:  x1 x3
```

**Notes:** `HC0` is Stata's plain `vce(robust)` and `HC1` is `vce(robust) small` -- the opposite of `regress`, where `vce(robust)` is HC1. `HC2`/`HC3` are rejected, since `ivregress` has no leverage-corrected robust VCE to reproduce. `e(df_r)` stores `N`, because Stata leaves it missing unless `small` is given and a stored missing cannot be represented.

### `IVLIML`

**Stata:** `ivregress liml ytreat x1 (treat = x3)`

Limited-information maximum likelihood -- Stata's `ivregress liml`. Solves the k-class problem with the LIML `kappa`, reported as `e(kappa)`. Under exact identification that `kappa` is 1 and the estimates equal 2SLS; when the model is over-identified it departs from 1 and the estimates differ. Both were measured on the fixture frame: exactly identified, `e(kappa)` prints 1.0 and the coefficients coincide with `IV2SLS`; adding a second instrument gives `kappa` about 1.0183 and visibly different coefficients.

**Signature**

```python
ws.IVLIML(endog: 'Any', exog: 'Any', endog_instr: 'Any', instruments: 'Any', *, param_names: 'Sequence[str] | None' = None, weights: 'Any' = None, weight_type: 'str | None' = None, **kwargs: 'Any') -> 'None'
```

**`IVLIML.from_formula`**

```python
IVLIML.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', **kwargs: 'Any') -> "'IV2SLS'"
```

**`IVLIML.fit`**

```python
IVLIML.fit(self, cov_type: 'str | None' = None, cov_kwds: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, ssc: 'SSC | None' = None, level: 'float | None' = None) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.IVLIML.from_formula('ytreat ~ x1 + [treat ~ x3 + x2]', base).fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text

Instrumental-variables LIML regression            Number of obs   =        200
                                                  Wald chi2(2)    =     128.73
                                                  Prob > chi2     =     0.0000
                                                  R-squared       =     0.5189
                                                  Root MSE        =     .98683

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      ytreat | Coefficient  Std. err.      z    P>|z|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
       treat |   1.290524   .2830631     4.56   0.000     .7357303    1.845317
          x1 |   .7653347    .069321    11.04   0.000      .629468    .9012013
       _cons |   1.045101   .1948412     5.36   0.000     .6632198    1.426983
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Endogenous: treat
Exogenous:  x1 x3 x2
```

**Notes:** `cov_type='HC2'`/`'HC3'` are rejected for the same reason as in `IV2SLS`: there is no leverage-corrected robust VCE in `ivregress` to reproduce.

### `IVGMM`

**Stata:** `ivregress gmm ytreat x1 (treat = x3)`

Generalized method of moments -- Stata's `ivregress gmm`. `ivregress gmm` ties *two* things to `vce()`, not one: the covariance of `e(b)` and the GMM weight matrix `W`, which comes from the same moment covariance. So westata picks the backend weight matrix from `cov_type` as well. When the model is exactly identified that changes only the standard errors; when it is over-identified the *point estimates* move too, which is what Stata does and why GMM departs from 2SLS there. Measured on the fixture frame with two instruments: exactly identified, `cov_type='cluster'` leaves the coefficients untouched; with the second instrument added, clustering moves them by about 1.1e-2.

**Signature**

```python
ws.IVGMM(endog: 'Any', exog: 'Any', endog_instr: 'Any', instruments: 'Any', *, param_names: 'Sequence[str] | None' = None, weights: 'Any' = None, weight_type: 'str | None' = None, **kwargs: 'Any') -> 'None'
```

**`IVGMM.from_formula`**

```python
IVGMM.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', **kwargs: 'Any') -> "'IV2SLS'"
```

**`IVGMM.fit`**

```python
IVGMM.fit(self, cov_type: 'str | None' = None, cov_kwds: 'Any' = None, ssc: 'Any' = None, **kwargs: 'Any') -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.IVGMM.from_formula('ytreat ~ x1 + [treat ~ x3]', base).fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text

Instrumental-variables GMM regression             Number of obs   =        200
                                                  Wald chi2(2)    =     176.20
                                                  Prob > chi2     =     0.0000
                                                  R-squared       =     0.5247
                                                  Root MSE        =     .98083
GMM weight matrix: Robust

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      ytreat | Coefficient  Std. err.      z    P>|z|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
       treat |   1.444141   .2801603     5.15   0.000     .8950365    1.993245
          x1 |   .7721013   .0603991    12.78   0.000     .6537212    .8904813
       _cons |   .9463986   .1922365     4.92   0.000     .5696219    1.323175
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Endogenous: treat
Exogenous:  x1 x3
```

**Notes:** The covariance is the backend's GMM weight-matrix sandwich, which cannot be expressed through the OLS-shaped `vce.compute_cov` path, so `cov_kwds` is forwarded to the backend verbatim rather than interpreted here.

### `XtIvreg`

**Stata:** `xtivreg ypanel x1 (x2 = x3), fe`

Panel instrumental variables -- Stata's `xtivreg`, with `method='fe'` (within 2SLS) or `method='re'` (G2SLS). The formula uses Stata's spelling, `'ypanel ~ x1 + (x2 = x3)'`, with the parenthesised instrument block split off textually so factor expansions and interactions keep working inside each piece. For `fe` the within transform is applied to the dependent variable, the exogenous regressors, the endogenous regressors *and the instruments* -- an undemeaned instrument still carries the entity effect, so the projection would re-absorb part of the effect the transform was meant to remove. The constant is recovered afterwards from the entity means.

**Signature**

```python
ws.XtIvreg(endog: 'Any', exog: 'Any', endog_regressors: 'Any', instruments: 'Any', *, exog_names: 'Sequence[str]', endog_names: 'Sequence[str]', instrument_names: 'Sequence[str]', codes: 'np.ndarray', sizes: 'np.ndarray', method: 'str' = 'fe', model_data: 'pd.DataFrame | None' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`XtIvreg.from_formula`**

```python
XtIvreg.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, entity: 'str', time: 'str | None' = None, method: 'str' = 'fe', subset: 'Any' = None) -> "'XtIvreg'"
```

**`XtIvreg.fit`**

```python
XtIvreg.fit(self, cov_type: 'str' = 'nonrobust', cov_kwds: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, ssc: 'Any' = None, *, maxiter: 'int' = 100, strict: 'bool' = False) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.XtIvreg.from_formula('ypanel ~ x1 + (x2 = x3)', base, entity='firm', time='year').fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text

Fixed-effects (within) IV regression            Number of obs     =        200
Group variable: firm                            Number of groups  =         40

R-squared:                                      Obs per group:
     Within  =      .                                         min =          5
     Between = 0.0029                                         avg =        5.0
     Overall = 0.0000                                         max =          5

                                                Wald chi2(2)      =       4.33
corr(u_i, Xb) = -0.0059                         Prob > chi2       =     0.1149

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      ypanel | Coefficient  Std. err.      z    P>|z|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
          x1 |   .7464423   .4321449     1.73   0.084    -.1005461    1.593431
          x2 |   4.463368   32.15818     0.14   0.890     -58.5655    67.49223
       _cons |   .5517225   2.083237     0.26   0.791    -3.531347    4.634792
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Endogenous: x2
Exogenous:  x1 x3
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
     sigma_u |  1.4971249
     sigma_e |  4.4921699
         rho |  .09996814   (fraction of variance due to u_i)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
 F test that all u_i=0: F(39,158)  =     0.20                Prob > F = 1.0000
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
```

**Notes:** Only `fe` and `re` are implemented. Stata's `be`, `fd` and Baltagi's `ec2sls` raise `UnsupportedError` rather than falling back to a nearby estimator, because the transformations differ, not just the display.

### `XtGls`

**Stata:** `xtgls y x1 x2, panels(heteroskedastic)`

Panel GLS -- Stata's `xtgls`. `panels` accepts `iid`, `heteroskedastic` (the default) and `correlated`; `corr` accepts `independent` (the default) and `ar1`, which applies a Prais-Winsten transform with one common autocorrelation coefficient before the same GLS step. This is two-step feasible GLS, not pooled OLS: panel-specific variances reweight each panel, so the coefficients move whenever those variances correlate with the regressors. Measured on the fixture frame, `panels(heteroskedastic)` gives `x1 = .8398055` against `.8406445` from `OLS` on the same data.

**Signature**

```python
ws.XtGls(endog: 'Any', exog: 'Any', codes: 'np.ndarray', sizes: 'np.ndarray', *, param_names: 'Sequence[str]', panels: 'str' = 'heteroskedastic', corr: 'str' = 'independent', nmk: 'bool' = False, model_data: 'pd.DataFrame | None' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`XtGls.from_formula`**

```python
XtGls.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, entity: 'str', time: 'str | None' = None, panels: 'str' = 'heteroskedastic', corr: 'str' = 'independent', nmk: 'bool' = False, subset: 'Any' = None) -> "'XtGls'"
```

**`XtGls.fit`**

```python
XtGls.fit(self, cov_type: 'str' = 'nonrobust', cov_kwds: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, ssc: 'Any' = None, *, maxiter: 'int' = 100, strict: 'bool' = False) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.XtGls.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base, entity='firm', time='year').fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text
Cross-sectional time-series FGLS regression

Coefficients:  generalized least squares
Panels:        heteroskedastic
Correlation:   no autocorrelation

Estimated covariances      =        40          Number of obs     =        200
Estimated autocorrelations =         0          Number of groups  =         40
Estimated coefficients     =         3          Time periods      =          5
                                                Wald chi2(2)      =     309.67
                                                Prob > chi2       =     0.0000

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
           y | Coefficient  Std. err.      z    P>|z|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
          x1 |   .8398055   .0564532    14.88   0.000     .7291592    .9504518
          x2 |  -.5251067   .0582604    -9.01   0.000    -.6392949   -.4109186
       _cons |   .9897201   .0582355    17.00   0.000     .8755807     1.10386
```

**Notes:** `panels(iid) corr(independent) nmk` is the combination that reproduces `regress`, because the only thing missing there is the `N/(N-k)` factor that `nmk` supplies. `corr(psar1)` (a separate coefficient per panel) and robust/cluster VCEs raise instead of returning the common-rho or conventional numbers under the wrong label. `igls` is mentioned in the module docstring but `fit` has no such parameter, so passing it fails as a `TypeError`, not a westata error.

### `XtPcse`

**Stata:** `xtpcse y x1 x2, corr(independent)`

Panel-corrected standard errors -- Stata's `xtpcse` (Beck-Katz). OLS point estimates with the panel-corrected VCE and no small-sample factor applied; `selection` is `casewise` (Stata's default, estimating the disturbance covariance from the periods every panel shares) or `pairwise` (from the periods each pair of panels shares). The summary prints the panel design block -- panels, estimated covariances, autocorrelations and coefficients -- and reports a Wald chi-square rather than an F.

**Signature**

```python
ws.XtPcse(endog: 'Any', exog: 'Any', codes: 'np.ndarray', sizes: 'np.ndarray', *, param_names: 'Sequence[str]', correlation: 'str' = 'independent', selection: 'str' = 'casewise', periods: 'np.ndarray | None' = None, model_data: 'pd.DataFrame | None' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`XtPcse.from_formula`**

```python
XtPcse.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, entity: 'str', time: 'str | None' = None, correlation: 'str' = 'independent', selection: 'str' = 'casewise', subset: 'Any' = None) -> "'XtPcse'"
```

**`XtPcse.fit`**

```python
XtPcse.fit(self, cov_type: 'str' = 'pcse', cov_kwds: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, ssc: 'Any' = None, *, maxiter: 'int' = 100, strict: 'bool' = False) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.XtPcse.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base, entity='firm', time='year').fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text
Linear regression, correlated panels corrected standard errors (PCSEs)

Group variable:   firm                          Number of obs     =        200
Time variable:    year                          Number of groups  =         40
Panels:           correlated (balanced)         Obs per group:
Autocorrelation:  no autocorrelation                          min =          5
                                                              avg =          5
                                                              max =          5
Estimated covariances      =       820          R-squared         =     0.4802
Estimated autocorrelations =         0          Wald chi2(2)      =     195.35
Estimated coefficients     =         3          Prob > chi2       =     0.0000

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
             |           Panel-corrected
           y | Coefficient  std. err.      z    P>|z|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
          x1 |   .8406445   .0649097    12.95   0.000     .7134238    .9678653
          x2 |   -.512087   .0873288    -5.86   0.000    -.6832483   -.3409257
       _cons |   1.005003    .107612     9.34   0.000     .7940868    1.215918
```

**Notes:** `time=` may be omitted on a balanced panel, where periods are read positionally from row order. On an unbalanced panel it is required: without it `ConsistencyError` is raised and the argument is named, because pairing rows by position would compute something that is not a covariance. `correlation='ar1'`/`'psar1'`, which replace OLS by Prais-Winsten, raise `UnsupportedError`.

### `XtAbond`

**Stata:** `xtabond ypanel x1, lags(1)`

Arellano-Bond / Blundell-Bond dynamic panel GMM -- Stata's `xtabond` family. One-step GMM on the first-differenced equation; `lags` sets how many lags of the dependent variable enter as regressors, and `cov_type` is `'gmm'` (the default) or `'robust'`. Only balanced panels are supported. The instrument count is reported both ways: `e(instruments)`/`e(zrank)` is Stata's "Number of instruments" (one more than the difference-side columns, because Stata also declares a level-equation moment the difference estimator cannot use), while `e(instruments_difference)` is the number of columns actually used.

**Signature**

```python
ws.XtAbond(endog: 'Any', exog: 'Any', groups: 'Any', periods: 'Any', *, lags: 'int' = 1, param_names: 'Sequence[str] | None' = None, exog_names: 'Sequence[str] | None' = None, system: 'bool' = False, model_data: 'pd.DataFrame | None' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`XtAbond.from_formula`**

```python
XtAbond.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, entity: 'str', time: 'str', lags: 'int' = 1, system: 'bool' = False, subset: 'Any' = None) -> "'XtAbond'"
```

**`XtAbond.fit`**

```python
XtAbond.fit(self, cov_type: 'str | None' = None, cov_kwds: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, ssc: 'Any' = None, *, maxiter: 'int' = 100, strict: 'bool' = False) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.XtAbond.from_formula('ypanel ~ x1', base, entity='firm', time='year').fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text
Arellano–Bond dynamic panel-data estimation     Number of obs    =     120
Group variable: firm                            Number of groups =      40
Time variable: year
                                                   Obs per group:
                                                           min =       3
                                                           avg =     3.0
                                                           max =       3
Number of instruments =      8                  Wald chi2(2)     =   30.43
                                                Prob > chi2      =  0.0000
One-step results


------------------------------------------------------------------------------
             | Coefficient  Std. err.      z    P>|z|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
        L1.y |   .2296989   .1226223     1.87   0.061    -.0106364    .4700341
          x1 |   .6069829   .1107299     5.48   0.000     .3899563    .8240095
```

**Notes:** Sargan, Hansen, AR(1) and AR(2) are not reported at all rather than reported approximately: their exact constructions are not fully specified in the manual, and a wrong overidentification statistic looks more authoritative than its absence. `system=True` only labels the result as system GMM -- the level moments are not estimated, so the coefficients are still the difference estimator's.

### `Eivreg`

**Stata:** `eivreg y x1 x2, reliab(x1 .9)`

Errors-in-variables regression -- Stata's `eivreg`. `reliab` is required and maps regressor names to assumed reliabilities; without it the call raises, since uncorrected attenuation would silently be OLS under another name. The estimator subtracts the measurement-error variance from the normalised cross-product (`A = X'X/n - S`), which undoes the attenuation instead of reweighting, and the standard errors come from the M-estimator sandwich including the derivative of `S` with respect to the coefficients -- dropping that term leaves the standard errors wrong even though the coefficients are unchanged.

**Signature**

```python
ws.Eivreg(endog: 'Any', exog: 'Any', reliab: 'Mapping[str, float]', *, param_names: 'Sequence[str]', model_data: 'pd.DataFrame | None' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`Eivreg.from_formula`**

```python
Eivreg.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, reliab: 'Mapping[str, float] | None' = None, subset: 'Any' = None) -> "'Eivreg'"
```

**`Eivreg.fit`**

```python
Eivreg.fit(self, cov_type: 'str | None' = 'nonrobust', cov_kwds: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, ssc: 'Any' = None) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.Eivreg.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base, reliab={'x1': 0.9}).fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text
Errors-in-variables regression


     Assumed
----------------------------                    Number of obs     =        200
                                                F(2, 197)         =      97.02
                                                Prob > F          =     0.0000

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
             | Coefficient  Std. err.      t    P>|t|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
          x1 |    .934051    .078524    11.90   0.000     .7791954    1.088907
          x2 |  -.5117214   .0713415    -7.17   0.000    -.6524125   -.3710303
       _cons |   .9996233   .0743546    13.44   0.000     .8529901    1.146257
```

**Notes:** Only the unadjusted VCE is available: `cov_type='robust'` raises `UnsupportedError`. A `reliab` entry naming something that is not a regressor also raises, and the message lists the regressors it did find.

### `Sureg`

**Stata:** `sureg (y1 x1) (y2 x2)`

Seemingly unrelated regression -- Stata's `sureg`. It takes a *sequence* of two-sided formulas, `from_formula(['y1 ~ x1', 'y2 ~ x2'], data)`, not one string. Coefficients are prefixed with the equation label (`y1:x1`, `y2:_cons`) because a stacked coefficient vector would otherwise contain duplicate names. The cross-equation covariance is `Sigma_ij = e_i'e_j / n` -- the `n` divisor, not `n - k`, is what the standard errors depend on -- and the system is solved blockwise rather than by materialising the full Kronecker matrix.

**Signature**

```python
ws.Sureg(designs: 'Sequence[np.ndarray]', responses: 'Sequence[np.ndarray]', *, equation_names: 'Sequence[str]', param_names: 'Sequence[Sequence[str]]', model_data: 'pd.DataFrame | None' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`Sureg.from_formula`**

```python
Sureg.from_formula(equations: 'Sequence[str]', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, names: 'Sequence[str] | None' = None, subset: 'Any' = None) -> "'Sureg'"
```

**`Sureg.fit`**

```python
Sureg.fit(self, cov_type: 'str | None' = 'nonrobust', cov_kwds: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, ssc: 'Any' = None, *, iterate: 'bool' = False) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.Sureg.from_formula(['y1 ~ x1', 'y2 ~ x2'], base).fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text
Seemingly unrelated regression
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
Equation             Obs   Params         RMSE  "R-squared"      chi2   P>chi2
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
y1                   200        1    1.0169773      0.2718      73.77   0.0000
y2                   200        1     .9704172      0.1092      24.73   0.0000
------------------------------------------------------------------------------

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
             | Coefficient  Std. err.      z    P>|z|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
y1           |
          x1 |   .6028085   .0701866     8.59   0.000     .4652454    .7403717
       _cons |   .9131627    .072023    12.68   0.000     .7720002    1.054325
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
y2           |
          x2 |  -.3438779   .0691467    -4.97   0.000    -.4794029   -.2083529
       _cons |   1.958213   .0687593    28.48   0.000     1.823447    2.092978
```

**Notes:** `iterate=True` (Stata's `isure`) is refused, because the convergence rule is not precisely documented and a wrong rule changes every standard error on the final iteration. `cov_type='robust'` follows the `reg3` robust sandwich and does change the reported standard errors, but `e(vce)` still reads `'unadjusted'` -- read the result's `cov_type` attribute instead if you need to know which VCE you got. `cov_type='cluster'` is refused, and all equations must use the same observations: a mismatch raises rather than fitting on unequal samples.

### `Mvreg`

**Stata:** `mvreg y1 y2 = x1 x2`

Multivariate regression -- Stata's `mvreg`, spelled `'y1 y2 = x1 x2'` with the outcomes left of `=` and a shared regressor list right of it. Every equation uses every regressor, so the coefficients are exactly per-equation OLS; only the VCE uses the cross-equation residual covariance, `V = Sigma (x) (X'X)^-1` with `Sigma_ij = u_i'u_j / (n - k)`. Note the `n - k` divisor here against `n` for `sureg`: with different regressors in the equations the two commands therefore disagree on identical data, and that is a specification difference, not an inconsistency.

**Signature**

```python
ws.Mvreg(designs: 'Sequence[np.ndarray]', responses: 'Sequence[np.ndarray]', *, outcome_names: 'Sequence[str]', param_names: 'Sequence[str]', model_data: 'pd.DataFrame | None' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`Mvreg.from_formula`**

```python
Mvreg.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, subset: 'Any' = None) -> "'Mvreg'"
```

**`Mvreg.fit`**

```python
Mvreg.fit(self, cov_type: 'str | None' = 'nonrobust', cov_kwds: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, ssc: 'Any' = None) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.Mvreg.from_formula('y1 y2 = x1 x2', base).fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text
Equation             Obs   Parms        RMSE    "R-sq"          F      P>F
--------------------------------------------------------------------------
y1                   200       3   1.0243928    0.2722    36.8382   0.0000
y2                   200       3    .9740065    0.1160    12.9271   0.0000
--------------------------------------------------------------------------

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
             | Coefficient  Std. err.      t    P>|t|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
y1           |
          x1 |   .6070447   .0707867     8.58   0.000     .4674478    .7466416
          x2 |  -.0243666   .0730832    -0.33   0.739    -.1684925    .1197593
       _cons |    .914468   .0726986    12.58   0.000     .7711007    1.057835
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
y2           |
          x1 |  -.0832083   .0673049    -1.24   0.218    -.2159389    .0495224
          x2 |  -.3430494   .0694885    -4.94   0.000    -.4800863   -.2060126
       _cons |   1.962931   .0691228    28.40   0.000     1.826615    2.099247
```

**Notes:** Only the unadjusted VCE is implemented; `cov_type='robust'` raises `UnsupportedError`, because only the unadjusted VCE is verified against Stata for this command.

### `RobustRegression`

**Stata:** `rreg y x1 x2`

Robust regression -- Stata's `rreg`, in Stata's statement order: fit OLS, drop observations with Cook's D above 1 and refit, iterate Huber weights, iterate biweight weights, then run one unweighted regression of the adjusted response. `tune` (default 7) scales the biweight constant `c_b = 4.685 * tune / 7`, and `tolerance` defaults to 0.01 -- Stata's own stopping rule. That default matters: iterating tighter lands on a different point, so `tolerance=1e-9` reproduces fully-converged estimates instead of Stata's.

**Signature**

```python
ws.RobustRegression(endog: 'Any', exog: 'Any', *, tune: 'float' = 7.0, param_names: 'list[str] | None' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`RobustRegression.from_formula`**

```python
RobustRegression.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, tune: 'float' = 7.0, subset: 'Any' = None) -> "'RobustRegression'"
```

**`RobustRegression.fit`**

```python
RobustRegression.fit(self, cov_type: 'str | None' = None, cov_kwds: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, ssc: 'Any' = None, *, maxiter: 'int' = 1000, tolerance: 'float' = 0.01, strict: 'bool' = False) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.RobustRegression.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text
Robust regression

Robust regression                               Number of obs     =        200
                                                F(  2,       197) =      81.25
                                                Prob > F          =     0.0000

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
           y | Coefficient  Std. err.      t    P>|t|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
          x1 |   .8381007   .0763045    10.98   0.000     .6876222    .9885792
          x2 |  -.5063725   .0787801    -6.43   0.000    -.6617331   -.3510119
       _cons |   1.019782   .0783655    13.01   0.000     .8652395    1.174325
```

**Notes:** The reported `mss`/`rss`/`mse`/`rmse`/`r2`/`F` describe the unweighted regression of the adjusted response -- the regression Stata actually posts -- not weighted sums of squares built from the final biweights, which are a different quantity.

### `QuantileRegression`

**Stata:** `qreg y x1 x2, quantile(.5)`

Quantile regression -- Stata's `qreg`. Minimises the check function at `quantile`, solved by IRLS; because the objective is piecewise linear, several parameter vectors can attain the same minimum and the solver returns one of them. Standard errors use Stata's `fitted` sparsity estimator -- the regression is re-fitted at `quantile ± h` with the Hall-Sheather bandwidth -- not a kernel density of the residuals, which would move every standard error while leaving the coefficients alone. Two reporting details are easy to misread: `e(sum_adev)`/`e(sum_rdev)` are check-function sums rather than sums of absolute values, and `e(q_v)`, printed as "(about q)", is the `ceil(n*tau)`-th order statistic of the outcome, not an interpolated quantile.

**Signature**

```python
ws.QuantileRegression(endog: 'Any', exog: 'Any', *, quantile: 'float' = 0.5, param_names: 'list[str] | None' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`QuantileRegression.from_formula`**

```python
QuantileRegression.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, quantile: 'float' = 0.5, subset: 'Any' = None) -> "'QuantileRegression'"
```

**`QuantileRegression.fit`**

```python
QuantileRegression.fit(self, cov_type: 'str | None' = 'iid', cov_kwds: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, ssc: 'Any' = None, *, reps: 'int' = 0, maxiter: 'int' = 2000, strict: 'bool' = False) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.QuantileRegression.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base, quantile=0.5).fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text

Median regression                                   Number of obs =        200
  Raw sum of deviations 112.7471 (about 1.0899456)
  Min sum of deviations   84.134                    Pseudo R2     =     0.2538

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
           y | Coefficient  Std. err.      t    P>|t|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
          x1 |   .8769292   .0996525     8.80   0.000     .6804067    1.073452
          x2 |  -.4154447   .1028856    -4.04   0.000    -.6183432   -.2125463
       _cons |   1.082302    .102344    10.58   0.000     .8804712    1.284132
```

**Notes:** `e(marginsnotok)` is `"stdp stddp residuals"`, so `predict` offers `xb`, `stdp` and `residuals` only; `stddp` is an `sqreg`-only type and raises after `qreg`.

### `Bsqreg`

**Stata:** `bsqreg y x1 x2, quantile(.5)`

Bootstrapped quantile regression -- Stata's `bsqreg`. The point estimates are the plain `qreg` ones and only the standard errors come from the bootstrap, which resamples rows and refits. `reps` defaults to 200, and `seed` makes the draw reproducible; with no seed a fresh one is drawn from the system entropy, mirroring Stata's use of the session's current `c(seed)` rather than a fixed constant -- but the drawn value is not stored on the result, so an unseeded fit cannot be reproduced afterwards. Structurally this is a one-quantile `Sqreg`, which is the equivalence `[R] qreg` states, so the two can never disagree about the resampling scheme.

**Signature**

```python
ws.Bsqreg(endog: 'Any', exog: 'Any', *, quantile: 'float' = 0.5, param_names: 'list[str] | None' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`Bsqreg.from_formula`**

```python
Bsqreg.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, quantile: 'float' = 0.5, subset: 'Any' = None) -> "'Bsqreg'"
```

**`Bsqreg.fit`**

```python
Bsqreg.fit(self, cov_type: 'str | None' = None, cov_kwds: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, ssc: 'Any' = None, *, reps: 'int' = 200, seed: 'int | None' = None, strict: 'bool' = False) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.Bsqreg.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base, quantile=0.5).fit(reps=50, seed=20261031).summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text

Median regression, bootstrap(50) SEs                Number of obs =         200
  bootstrap(50) SEs                                   .5 Pseudo R2=      0.2538
              |               Bootstrap

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
             | Coefficient  Std. err.      t    P>|t|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
          x1 |   .8769292   .1145663     7.65   0.000     .6509953    1.102863
          x2 |  -.4154447    .135737    -3.06   0.003    -.6831289   -.1477606
       _cons |   1.082302   .1080426    10.02   0.000     .8692332     1.29537
```

**Notes:** The bootstrap seed is recorded in `e(seed)`, so even an UNSEEDED fit can be replayed: pass `seed=result.e['seed']` back to `fit()` and the replicates repeat exactly. Stata gives the same guarantee through `c(seed)` and `set seed`. Row order also carries between replicates, as it does in Stata's `bsampl_w`/`qreg_c` loop, so each replicate does not start from the original data order.

### `Sqreg`

**Stata:** `sqreg y x1 x2, quantiles(.25 .5 .75)`

Simultaneous-quantile regression -- Stata's `sqreg`. Fits several quantiles at once and bootstraps the whole parameter vector: one resample is drawn per replicate and every quantile is fitted on it, which is what makes the covariance simultaneous -- the cross-quantile blocks then measure how the quantiles move together under the same resampling, which independent draws would destroy. Parameter names carry the quantile prefix (`q0.25:x1`). `predict(kind='stddp')` gives the standard error of the difference in linear predictions between the first two quantiles, using the cross-equation covariance rather than adding the two variances.

**Signature**

```python
ws.Sqreg(endog: 'Any', exog: 'Any', *, quantiles: 'Sequence[float]' = (0.25, 0.5, 0.75), param_names: 'list[str] | None' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`Sqreg.from_formula`**

```python
Sqreg.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, quantiles: 'Sequence[float]' = (0.25, 0.5, 0.75), subset: 'Any' = None) -> "'Sqreg'"
```

**`Sqreg.fit`**

```python
Sqreg.fit(self, cov_type: 'str | None' = None, cov_kwds: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, ssc: 'Any' = None, *, reps: 'int' = 200, seed: 'int | None' = None, strict: 'bool' = False) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.Sqreg.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base, quantiles=(0.25, 0.75)).fit(reps=50, seed=20261031).summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text

Simultaneous quantile regression                    Number of obs =         200
  bootstrap(50) SEs                                  .25 Pseudo R2=      0.2766
                                                     .75 Pseudo R2=      0.2865
              |               Bootstrap

---------------------------------------------------------------------------------
                | Coefficient  Std. err.      t    P>|t|     [95% conf. interval]
----------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
q25             |
          _cons |   .2844253   .1116347     2.55   0.012     .0642728    .5045777
             x1 |   .8812208   .0923716     9.54   0.000     .6990567    1.063385
             x2 |  -.5777819   .0853988    -6.77   0.000    -.7461952   -.4093686
----------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
q75             |
          _cons |   1.687185   .0782618    21.56   0.000     1.532847    1.841524
             x1 |   .8443399   .0861656     9.80   0.000     .6744144    1.014265
             x2 |  -.4856428   .0828392    -5.86   0.000    -.6490082   -.3222775
```

**Notes:** `stddp` needs an `sqreg` fit with at least two quantiles and both equations using the same regressors, and raises otherwise. The default `reps=200` makes a three-quantile fit take roughly twenty seconds on 200 rows, so the example passes a smaller `reps` and two quantiles. The seed is recorded in `e(seed)`, so an unseeded fit can be replayed by passing it back to `fit(seed=...)`.

### `NeweyWestResult`

**Stata:** `newey y x1 x2, lag(2)`

The object `newey` returns: `regress`'s point estimates behind `newey`'s narrower result surface. Two differences from a plain `StataResults` are visible to callers. `e()` holds only `newey`'s own scalars and macros, because `newey` posts a fresh `e()` rather than extending `regress`'s, so `r2`, `r2_a`, `rmse`, `sigma`, `rss`, `mss` and `ll` are absent. And `predict` accepts only `xb`, `index`, `stdp` and `residuals`. Constructing it directly takes a fitted result and a `lag`; normally one comes back from `ws.newey(formula, data, lag=...)`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.NeweyWestResult(results: 'Any', *, lag: 'int') -> 'None'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.newey('y ~ x1 + x2', base, lag=2).summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text

Regression with Newey–West standard errors      Number of obs     =        200
Maximum lag = 2                                 F(  2,       197) =     107.69
                                                Prob > F          =     0.0000

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
             |             Newey–West
           y | Coefficient  std. err.      t    P>|t|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
          x1 |   .8406445   .0662943    12.68   0.000     .7099069    .9713822
          x2 |   -.512087   .0707643    -7.24   0.000    -.6516398   -.3725342
       _cons |   1.005003   .0768392    13.08   0.000     .8534696    1.156535
```

**Notes:** Requesting any other prediction type raises with `newey_p.ado`'s restriction quoted, rather than silently forwarding `regress`'s fifteen types.

### `Nl`

**Stata:** `nl y = {b0} * exp({b1} * x1)`

Nonlinear least squares -- Stata's `nl`. The formula is Stata's `y = <expression>` shape with parameters in braces, as in `'y = {b0} + {b1} * x1'`, and the coefficient table shows them with Stata's `/` prefix (`/b0`). Starting values come from `start=` or inline as `{b0=4}`; when neither is given a coarse multiplicative grid search seeds the optimiser, which matters because a parameter living orders of magnitude away from 1 is otherwise unreachable. `has_constant` is inferred from where the parameters sit in the expression and decides whether the total sum of squares is centred and one model degree of freedom dropped.

**Signature**

```python
ws.Nl(endog: 'Any', frame: 'pd.DataFrame', parameter_names: 'Sequence[str]', predictor: 'Callable[[Mapping[str, float], pd.DataFrame], np.ndarray]', *, has_constant: 'bool' = False, start: 'Mapping[str, float] | None' = None, formula: 'str' = '', model_data: 'pd.DataFrame | None' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`Nl.from_formula`**

```python
Nl.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, start: 'Mapping[str, float] | None' = None, subset: 'Any' = None) -> "'Nl'"
```

**`Nl.fit`**

```python
Nl.fit(self, cov_type: 'str | None' = None, cov_kwds: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, ssc: 'Any' = None, *, maxiter: 'int' = 200, strict: 'bool' = True) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.Nl.from_formula('y = {b0} + {b1} * x1', base).fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text

      Source |      SS            df       MS
-------------+----------------------------------   Number of obs   =       200
       Model |  148.66262         1  148.662619    F(1, 198)       =    109.75
    Residual |  268.20453       198  1.35456832    Prob > F        =    0.0000
-------------+----------------------------------   R-squared       =    0.3566
       Total |  416.86715       199  2.09480978    Adj R-squared   =    0.3534
                                                   Res. dev.       =    626.26

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
             | Coefficient  Std. err.      t    P>|t|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
         /b0 |   .9723568   .0824264    11.80   0.000     .8098105    1.134903
         /b1 |    .842525   .0804234    10.48   0.000     .6839286    1.001121
```

**Notes:** `fit(strict=True)` is the default and raises `ConvergenceError` when the optimiser does not converge, instead of returning an unconverged fit. The class retains no regressor matrix (`design_matrix` is `None`) and `predict` registers no types for `nl`, so prediction is not available through it even with an explicit `exog=`.


## Discrete choice, counts and limited responses

### `Logit`

**Stata:** `logit yb x1 x2`

Logistic regression by maximum likelihood -- Stata's `logit`. Reports an LR chi-square model test, an intercept-only log-likelihood and a pseudo R-squared in the header, then coefficients on the log-odds scale with z statistics. `e(aic)` is stored and equals `-2*e(ll) + 2*e(rank)`, which is what `estat_ic` prints. `predict` accepts `kind='xb'`, `'stdp'`, `'pr'`, `'residuals'` and `'score'`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.Logit(endog: 'Any', exog: 'Any', *, param_names: 'list[str] | None' = None, freq_weights: 'Any' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`Logit.from_formula`**

```python
Logit.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, subset: 'Any' = None, freq_weights: 'str | None' = None) -> "'_DiscreteT'"
```

**`Logit.fit`**

```python
Logit.fit(self, cov_type: 'str | None' = None, cov_kwds: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, ssc: 'Any' = None, *, maxiter: 'int' = 500, tol: 'float' = 1e-10, strict: 'bool' = False) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.Logit.from_formula('yb ~ x1 + x2', base).fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text

Iteration 0:  Log likelihood = -137.98875  

Logistic regression                                     Number of obs =    200
                                                        LR chi2(2)    =  13.90
                                                        Prob > chi2   = 0.0010
Log likelihood = -131.03744                             Pseudo R2     = 0.0504

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
          yb | Coefficient  Std. err.      z    P>|z|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
          x1 |   .5292965   .1527099     3.47   0.001     .2299905    .8286024
          x2 |  -.1323303   .1488764    -0.89   0.374    -.4241227    .1594621
       _cons |   .1522173   .1474054     1.03   0.302    -.1366919    .4411265
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
```

**Notes:** The prediction type is `score` (singular), the equation-level score; Stata reserves the plural `scores` for multi-equation models, so `predict(kind='scores')` raises instead of returning one column. Frequency weights are supported via `from_formula(..., freq_weights='w')` and are applied by row expansion, which is what Stata's `fweight` means here.

### `Probit`

**Stata:** `probit yb x1 x2`

Probit regression by maximum likelihood -- Stata's `probit`. The header block and the inference table have the same shape as `Logit`'s, but the link is the standard normal CDF, so coefficients are in latent-standard-deviation units and are smaller than the logit ones on the same data. `predict` offers `'xb'`, `'stdp'`, `'pr'`, `'residuals'` and `'score'`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.Probit(endog: 'Any', exog: 'Any', *, param_names: 'list[str] | None' = None, freq_weights: 'Any' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`Probit.from_formula`**

```python
Probit.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, subset: 'Any' = None, freq_weights: 'str | None' = None) -> "'_DiscreteT'"
```

**`Probit.fit`**

```python
Probit.fit(self, cov_type: 'str | None' = None, cov_kwds: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, ssc: 'Any' = None, *, maxiter: 'int' = 500, tol: 'float' = 1e-10, strict: 'bool' = False) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.Probit.from_formula('yb ~ x1 + x2', base).fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text

Iteration 0:  Log likelihood = -137.98875  

Probit regression                                       Number of obs =    200
                                                        LR chi2(2)    =  13.97
                                                        Prob > chi2   = 0.0009
Log likelihood = -131.00316                             Pseudo R2     = 0.0506

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
          yb | Coefficient  Std. err.      z    P>|z|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
          x1 |   .3293536   .0928298     3.55   0.000     .1474106    .5112966
          x2 |  -.0799735   .0916574    -0.87   0.383    -.2596188    .0996717
       _cons |   .0947076   .0908939     1.04   0.297    -.0834411    .2728564
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
```

**Notes:** `score` is singular, as for `logit`; the plural spelling is Stata's multi-equation form and is refused. As with `logit`, frequency weights are applied by row expansion.

### `OrderedLogit`

**Stata:** `ologit yord x1 x2`

Ordered logistic regression -- Stata's `ologit`. The ordered outcome's distinct values become the categories (`e(k_cat)` counts them) and the table reports slopes plus `e(k_cat) - 1` cut points labelled `/cut1`, `/cut2`, and so on, the same labels Stata prints. `predict(kind='pr')` returns the probability of one category; pass `outcome=` to choose which (it defaults to the first), and the probabilities over all categories sum to one.

**Signature**

```python
ws.OrderedLogit(endog: 'Any', exog: 'Any', *, param_names: 'list[str] | None' = None, freq_weights: 'Any' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`OrderedLogit.from_formula`**

```python
OrderedLogit.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, subset: 'Any' = None, freq_weights: 'str | None' = None) -> "'_DiscreteT'"
```

**`OrderedLogit.fit`**

```python
OrderedLogit.fit(self, cov_type: 'str | None' = None, cov_kwds: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, ssc: 'Any' = None, *, maxiter: 'int' = 500, tol: 'float' = 1e-10, strict: 'bool' = False) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
d = base.assign(yord=base.ycnt.clip(0, 2))
ws.OrderedLogit.from_formula('yord ~ x1 + x2', d).fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text

Ordered logistic regression                             Number of obs =    200
                                                        LR chi2(2)    =  24.81
                                                        Prob > chi2   = 0.0000
Log likelihood = -202.12949                             Pseudo R2     = 0.0578

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
        yord | Coefficient  Std. err.      z    P>|z|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
          x1 |   .6788396   .1431435     4.74   0.000     .3982834    .9593957
          x2 |  -.0554988   .1342388    -0.41   0.679     -.318602    .2076043
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
       /cut1 |  -1.147585   .1700937                     -1.480962    -.814207
       /cut2 |   .2706843   .1493337                     -.0220043     .563373
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
```

**Notes:** The model assumes the outcome is categorical: passing a continuous variable makes every distinct value a category, so on a continuous response `e(k_cat)` equals the sample size and `k_cat - 1` cut points are estimated alongside the slopes. `predict` offers no `'score'` type -- only `'xb'`, `'stdp'`, `'pr'` and `'residuals'` -- because the score would be one variable per equation.

### `OrderedProbit`

**Stata:** `oprobit yord x1 x2`

Ordered probit regression -- Stata's `oprobit`. The same ordered-category machinery as `OrderedLogit` with the normal distribution in place of the logistic one, so it reports slopes and `e(k_cat) - 1` cut points and, on the same data, a comparable LR test and log-likelihood but systematically smaller coefficients (the logistic scale needs a factor of about 1.6 relative to the normal one). `predict(kind='pr')` takes the same optional `outcome=` argument.

**Signature**

```python
ws.OrderedProbit(endog: 'Any', exog: 'Any', *, param_names: 'list[str] | None' = None, freq_weights: 'Any' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`OrderedProbit.from_formula`**

```python
OrderedProbit.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, subset: 'Any' = None, freq_weights: 'str | None' = None) -> "'_DiscreteT'"
```

**`OrderedProbit.fit`**

```python
OrderedProbit.fit(self, cov_type: 'str | None' = None, cov_kwds: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, ssc: 'Any' = None, *, maxiter: 'int' = 500, tol: 'float' = 1e-10, strict: 'bool' = False) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
d = base.assign(yord=base.ycnt.clip(0, 2))
ws.OrderedProbit.from_formula('yord ~ x1 + x2', d).fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text

Ordered probit regression                               Number of obs =    200
                                                        LR chi2(2)    =  24.01
                                                        Prob > chi2   = 0.0000
Log likelihood = -202.52596                             Pseudo R2     = 0.0560

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
        yord | Coefficient  Std. err.      z    P>|z|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
          x1 |   .4063142   .0853207     4.76   0.000     .2390887    .5735398
          x2 |  -.0356842   .0825221    -0.43   0.665    -.1974245     .126056
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
       /cut1 |  -.6815503   .0986671                     -.8749343   -.4881663
       /cut2 |   .1743743   .0919787                     -.0059006    .3546491
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
```

**Notes:** The outcome must be discrete for the fit to be meaningful: a continuous column produces one category per distinct value and `k_cat - 1` cut points, which is an unidentifiable model. No `'score'` prediction type is registered; the available kinds are `'xb'`, `'stdp'`, `'pr'` and `'residuals'`.

### `Poisson`

**Stata:** `poisson ycnt x1 x2`

Poisson regression by maximum likelihood -- Stata's `poisson`. Coefficients are on the log-mean scale; the header prints an LR chi-square test, `e(ll_0)` and a pseudo R-squared. `predict(kind='n')` and `kind='ir'` both give the predicted mean (on this data they are numerically identical), while `kind='pr'` needs a count: `predict(kind='pr', n=2)` gives `Pr(y = 2)`, and calling `'pr'` without it raises rather than assuming a value.

**Signature**

```python
ws.Poisson(endog: 'Any', exog: 'Any', *, param_names: 'list[str] | None' = None, freq_weights: 'Any' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`Poisson.from_formula`**

```python
Poisson.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, subset: 'Any' = None, freq_weights: 'str | None' = None) -> "'_DiscreteT'"
```

**`Poisson.fit`**

```python
Poisson.fit(self, cov_type: 'str | None' = None, cov_kwds: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, ssc: 'Any' = None, *, maxiter: 'int' = 500, tol: 'float' = 1e-10, strict: 'bool' = False) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.Poisson.from_formula('ycnt ~ x1 + x2', base).fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text

Poisson regression                                      Number of obs =    200
                                                        LR chi2(2)    =  35.40
                                                        Prob > chi2   = 0.0000
Log likelihood = -299.6568                              Pseudo R2     = 0.0558

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
        ycnt | Coefficient  Std. err.      z    P>|z|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
          x1 |   .3319199    .055595     5.97   0.000     .2229556    .4408841
          x2 |   .0025616   .0575378     0.04   0.964    -.1102105    .1153336
       _cons |   .3313258   .0621307     5.33   0.000     .2095519    .4530998
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
```

**Notes:** `'pr'` is Stata's `pr(n)`/`pr(a, b)` family, not a single probability, so it takes `n=`. Frequency weights are supported and are applied by row expansion.

### `NegativeBinomial`

**Stata:** `nbreg yod x1 x2`

Negative binomial regression, NB2 -- Stata's default `nbreg`. The dispersion is statsmodels' `alpha` with `Var = mu + alpha*mu^2`, the same parameterisation as Stata's default, estimated on the log scale and reported as `/lnalpha` with `e(alpha)` as the exponentiated value. The optimiser is BFGS rather than Newton because the Newton step diverges from the Poisson start once `alpha` is in the parameter vector. `e(aic)` is stored and agrees with `estat_ic`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.NegativeBinomial(endog: 'Any', exog: 'Any', *, param_names: 'list[str] | None' = None, freq_weights: 'Any' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`NegativeBinomial.from_formula`**

```python
NegativeBinomial.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, subset: 'Any' = None, freq_weights: 'str | None' = None) -> "'_DiscreteT'"
```

**`NegativeBinomial.fit`**

```python
NegativeBinomial.fit(self, cov_type: 'str | None' = None, cov_kwds: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, ssc: 'Any' = None, *, maxiter: 'int' = 500, tol: 'float' = 1e-10, strict: 'bool' = False) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
d = base.assign(yod=base.ycnt ** 2)
ws.NegativeBinomial.from_formula('yod ~ x1 + x2', d).fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text

Negative binomial regression                            Number of obs =    200
                                                        LR chi2(1)    =  33.33
Dispersion: mean                                        Prob > chi2   = 0.0000
Log likelihood = -472.86915                             Pseudo R2     = 0.0340

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
         yod | Coefficient  Std. err.      z    P>|z|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
          x1 |   .5272862    .090853     5.80   0.000     .3492176    .7053548
          x2 |   .0058862   .0894056     0.07   0.948    -.1693456    .1811179
       _cons |   1.194401   .0912093    13.10   0.000     1.015634    1.373168
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
    /lnalpha |   .2630268   .1342525                     -.0001032    .5261568
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
       alpha |   1.300862   .1746439                      .9998968    1.692416
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
LR test of alpha=0: chibar2(01) = 469.33              Prob >= chibar2 = 0.000
```

**Notes:** `e(ll_c)` is the log-likelihood at the Poisson (i.e. `alpha = 0`) limit, and `e(chi2_c)` is twice the difference between it and `e(ll)`; on this data `e(chi2_c)` reproduces `2*(e(ll) - e(ll_c))` exactly, so the dispersion test is available from the macros. No `'score'` prediction type is registered, because the score would be one column per equation, and `'pr'` again requires `n=`.

### `NegativeBinomialNB1`

**Stata:** `nbreg yod x1 x2, dispersion(constant)`

Negative binomial regression with a constant dispersion, NB1 -- Stata's `nbreg, dispersion(constant)`, where `Var = mu*(1 + delta)` is linear in the mean rather than quadratic. This is a different model with a different likelihood from `NegativeBinomial`, which is why it is a separate class rather than an option on that one. The dispersion is reported as `/lndelta` with `e(delta)` as its exponential, and the likelihood follows `nbreg_al.ado`, whose gamma terms involve `mu/delta` rather than `mu`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.NegativeBinomialNB1(endog: 'Any', exog: 'Any', *, param_names: 'list[str] | None' = None, freq_weights: 'Any' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`NegativeBinomialNB1.from_formula`**

