```python
NegativeBinomialNB1.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, subset: 'Any' = None, freq_weights: 'str | None' = None) -> "'_DiscreteT'"
```

**`NegativeBinomialNB1.fit`**

```python
NegativeBinomialNB1.fit(self, cov_type: 'str | None' = None, cov_kwds: 'Any' = None, ssc: 'Any' = None, *, maxiter: 'int' = 200, tol: 'float' = 1e-12, strict: 'bool' = False) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
d = base.assign(yod=base.ycnt ** 2)
ws.NegativeBinomialNB1.from_formula('yod ~ x1 + x2', d).fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text

Negative binomial (NB1)                                 Number of obs =    200
                                                        LR chi2(2)    =  35.90
Log likelihood = -473.81429                             Prob > chi2   = 0.0000

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
             | Coefficient  Std. err.      z    P>|z|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
          x1 |    .439003   .0744434     5.90   0.000     .2930967    .5849093
          x2 |     .00851   .0752843     0.11   0.910    -.1390445    .1560645
       _cons |   1.242011   .0957695    12.97   0.000     1.054306    1.429715
    /lndelta |   1.602285   .1626415     9.85   0.000     1.283513    1.921056
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
```

**Notes:** `e(aic)`, `e(bic)` and `e(r2_p)` are absent, and `e(cmd)` is the descriptive string `'Negative binomial (NB1)'` rather than a Stata command name, so `predict` registers no type for it: call `estat_ic` if a criterion is needed. Robust and cluster VCEs are refused by name -- only `cov_type='nonrobust'` is implemented.

### `ZeroInflatedPoisson`

**Stata:** `zip ycnt x1 x2, inflate(x3)`

Zero-inflated Poisson -- Stata's `zip`. The count equation is Poisson and the inflation equation is binary logit on the variables named by `inflate=`; the two blocks appear in one table, the inflation rows prefixed `inflate#`. `e(n_zero)` and `e(n_nonzero)` count the zeros and non-zeros in the estimation sample. With `inflate=None` there is no mixture and the model is the plain Poisson regression, fitted by the same likelihood.

**Signature**

```python
ws.ZeroInflatedPoisson(endog: 'Any', exog: 'Any', *, inflate_exog: 'Any' = None, inflate_names: 'list[str] | None' = None, param_names: 'list[str] | None' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`ZeroInflatedPoisson.from_formula`**

```python
ZeroInflatedPoisson.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, inflate: 'str | None' = None, subset: 'Any' = None, freq_weights: 'str | None' = None) -> "'_ZeroInflatedBaseT'"
```

**`ZeroInflatedPoisson.fit`**

```python
ZeroInflatedPoisson.fit(self, cov_type: 'str | None' = None, cov_kwds: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, ssc: 'Any' = None, *, maxiter: 'int' = 500, tol: 'float' = 1e-10, strict: 'bool' = False) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.ZeroInflatedPoisson.from_formula('ycnt ~ x1 + x2', base, inflate='x3').fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text

Zero-inflated Poisson regression                        Number of obs =    200
Inflation model: logit                                  Nonzero obs   =    148
                                                        Zero obs      =     52
                                                        LR chi2(2)    =  33.03
Log likelihood = -299.2566                              Prob > chi2   = 0.0000

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
        ycnt | Coefficient  Std. err.      z    P>|z|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
ycnt         |
          x1 |   .3327988   .0562969     5.91   0.000     .2224588    .4431387
          x2 |   .0039316   .0591759     0.07   0.947     -.112051    .1199142
       _cons |   .3681895   .0761911     4.83   0.000     .2188577    .5175212
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
inflate      |
          x3 |  -.3229142   1.022661    -0.32   0.752    -2.327293    1.681464
       _cons |  -3.374413   1.491321    -2.26   0.024    -6.297348   -.4514782
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
```

**Notes:** `predict` supports only `'xb'`, `'n'`, `'pr'` and `'stdp'`; `'stdf'` and every other kind are refused. `'pr'` is the inflation probability `logistic(w'g)`, not a count probability, and it raises when the model was fitted with `inflate=None`. `e(df_m)` counts only the main-equation regressors beyond the constant, so the printed `LR chi2` degrees of freedom are smaller than `e(rank)`.

### `ZeroInflatedNegativeBinomial`

**Stata:** `zinb yz x1 x2, inflate(x3)`

Zero-inflated negative binomial, NB2 -- Stata's `zinb`. A negative-binomial count equation plus a logit inflation equation; the dispersion is estimated as `/lnalpha` on the log scale, exactly as `nbreg` stores it. It uses a quasi-Newton optimiser for the same reason `NegativeBinomial` does. With `inflate=None` it reduces to the plain NB2 fit.

**Signature**

```python
ws.ZeroInflatedNegativeBinomial(endog: 'Any', exog: 'Any', *, inflate_exog: 'Any' = None, inflate_names: 'list[str] | None' = None, param_names: 'list[str] | None' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`ZeroInflatedNegativeBinomial.from_formula`**

```python
ZeroInflatedNegativeBinomial.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, inflate: 'str | None' = None, subset: 'Any' = None, freq_weights: 'str | None' = None) -> "'_ZeroInflatedBaseT'"
```

**`ZeroInflatedNegativeBinomial.fit`**

```python
ZeroInflatedNegativeBinomial.fit(self, cov_type: 'str | None' = None, cov_kwds: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, ssc: 'Any' = None, *, maxiter: 'int' = 500, tol: 'float' = 1e-10, strict: 'bool' = False) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
mu = np.exp(0.4 + 0.6 * base.x1 + 0.2 * base.x2)
cnt = rng.negative_binomial(1 / 0.8, 1 / (1 + 0.8 * mu))
d = base.assign(yz=np.where(rng.uniform(size=200) < 1 / (1 + np.exp(-base.x3)), 0, cnt))
ws.ZeroInflatedNegativeBinomial.from_formula('yz ~ x1 + x2', d, inflate='x3').fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text

Zero-inflated negative binomial regression              Number of obs =    200
Inflation model: logit                                  Nonzero obs   =     66
                                                        Zero obs      =    134
                                                        LR chi2(2)    =  25.08
Log likelihood = -235.1402                              Prob > chi2   = 0.0000

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
          yz | Coefficient  Std. err.      z    P>|z|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
yz           |
          x1 |    .523662    .121246     4.32   0.000     .2860242    .7612998
          x2 |    .306602   .1051965     2.91   0.004     .1004207    .5127834
       _cons |   .5377467   .1747467     3.08   0.002     .1952495    .8802439
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
inflate      |
          x3 |   .8042556   .2505497     3.21   0.001     .3131873    1.295324
       _cons |    .290147   .2802637     1.04   0.301    -.2591598    .8394537
    /lnalpha |  -.9197992   .5600437    -1.64   0.101    -2.017465    .1778664
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
       alpha |   .3985991   .2232329                      .1329922    1.194666
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
LR test of alpha=0: chibar2(01) = 25.08               Prob >= chibar2 = 0.000
```

**Notes:** `predict` offers only `'xb'`, `'n'`, `'pr'` and `'stdp'`; `'pr'` is the inflation probability and raises when `inflate=None`. On data without genuine excess zeros the inflation equation is not identified: the estimate is then driven to a boundary and the information matrix can be singular, which is reported as a `ConvergenceError` rather than a table of huge standard errors.

### `TruncatedPoisson`

**Stata:** `ztp ycnt x1 x2`

Poisson regression truncated to `[lower, upper]` -- Stata's `ztp` when `lower=1` with no upper limit, and `tpoisson` for any other limit; `model_name` (`e(cmd)`) follows suit, so the same class reports `ztp` or `tpoisson` depending on the limits. Every observation must lie inside the limits: the constructor raises a `ConsistencyError` naming how many observations fall outside rather than silently dropping them.

**Signature**

```python
ws.TruncatedPoisson(endog: 'Any', exog: 'Any', *, lower: 'float | None' = 1, upper: 'float | None' = None, param_names: 'list[str] | None' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`TruncatedPoisson.from_formula`**

```python
TruncatedPoisson.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, lower: 'float | None' = 1, upper: 'float | None' = None, subset: 'Any' = None) -> "'_TruncatedBaseT'"
```

**`TruncatedPoisson.fit`**

```python
TruncatedPoisson.fit(self, cov_type: 'str | None' = None, cov_kwds: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, ssc: 'Any' = None, *, maxiter: 'int' = 500, tol: 'float' = 1e-10, strict: 'bool' = False) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
d = base[base.ycnt > 0]
ws.TruncatedPoisson.from_formula('ycnt ~ x1 + x2', d).fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text

Zero-truncated Poisson regression                       Number of obs =    148
                                                        LR chi2(2)    =  35.95
                                                        Prob > chi2   = 0.0000
Log likelihood = -187.9949                              Pseudo R2     = 0.0873

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
        ycnt | Coefficient  Std. err.      z    P>|z|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
          x1 |   .3660199     .07135     5.13   0.000     .2261764    .5058634
          x2 |   .0715222   .0709734     1.01   0.314     -.067583    .2106274
       _cons |   .3364123    .088168     3.82   0.000     .1636061    .5092184
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
```

**Notes:** Truncation means the out-of-range rows are ABSENT from the sample, so the caller has to subset first -- this class refuses to subset for you, which is the opposite of `Truncreg`'s behaviour. `predict` offers `'xb'`, `'n'` and `'stdp'` only; there is no `'pr'`.

### `TruncatedNegativeBinomial`

**Stata:** `tnbreg yod x1, ll(1)`

Negative binomial regression truncated to `[lower, upper]` -- Stata's `tnbreg`. The NB2 dispersion is estimated alongside the slopes and reported as `/lnalpha`; as with `TruncatedPoisson`, the data must already be subset to the interior of the limits or the constructor raises. `predict` offers `'xb'`, `'n'` and `'stdp'` only.

**Signature**

```python
ws.TruncatedNegativeBinomial(endog: 'Any', exog: 'Any', *, lower: 'float | None' = 1, upper: 'float | None' = None, param_names: 'list[str] | None' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`TruncatedNegativeBinomial.from_formula`**

```python
TruncatedNegativeBinomial.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, lower: 'float | None' = 1, upper: 'float | None' = None, subset: 'Any' = None) -> "'_TruncatedBaseT'"
```

**`TruncatedNegativeBinomial.fit`**

```python
TruncatedNegativeBinomial.fit(self, cov_type: 'str | None' = None, cov_kwds: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, ssc: 'Any' = None, *, maxiter: 'int' = 500, tol: 'float' = 1e-10, strict: 'bool' = False) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
d = base[base.ycnt > 0].assign(yod=lambda f: f.ycnt ** 2)
ws.TruncatedNegativeBinomial.from_formula('yod ~ x1', d).fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text

Truncated negative binomial regression                  Number of obs =    148
Truncation point = 1                                    LR chi2(1)    =  19.31
                                                        Prob > chi2   = 0.0000
Log likelihood = -360.61677                             Pseudo R2     = 0.0261

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
         yod | Coefficient  Std. err.      z    P>|z|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
          x1 |   .5768012   .1302026     4.43   0.000     .3216088    .8319936
       _cons |   .8655364   .2784346     3.11   0.002     .3198146    1.411258
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
    /lnalpha |   .9244537   .4734482                     -.0034877    1.852395
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
       alpha |   2.520491   1.193322                      .9965184     6.37507
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
LR test of alpha=0: chibar2(01) = 19.31               Prob >= chibar2 = 0.000
```

**Notes:** On data where the negative binomial collapses to the Poisson limit the optimiser warns that it did not fully converge; the coefficient table is still produced, so check the warning before trusting the dispersion. No `'pr'` or `'score'` type is registered.

### `Tobit`

**Stata:** `tobit ycens x1 x2, ll(0)`

Tobit regression with left and/or right censoring -- Stata's `tobit`, defaulting to `lower=0` as Stata's `ll(0)` does. Censored observations contribute their censoring probability and uncensored ones the normal density, with `/sigma` estimated and `e(sigma) = var_e` derived. `e(N_lc)`, `e(N_rc)` and `e(N_unc)` count left-censored, right-censored and uncensored observations, and `e(df_r)` is the residual degrees of freedom.

**Signature**

```python
ws.Tobit(endog: 'Any', exog: 'Any', *, lower: 'float | None' = 0.0, upper: 'float | None' = None, param_names: 'list[str] | None' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`Tobit.from_formula`**

```python
Tobit.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, lower: 'float | None' = 0.0, upper: 'float | None' = None, subset: 'Any' = None) -> "'Tobit'"
```

**`Tobit.fit`**

```python
Tobit.fit(self, cov_type: 'str | None' = None, cov_kwds: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, ssc: 'Any' = None, *, maxiter: 'int' = 1000, tol: 'float' = 1e-10, strict: 'bool' = False, start_params: 'np.ndarray | None' = None) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.Tobit.from_formula('ycens ~ x1 + x2', base).fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text

Tobit regression                                    Number of obs     =    200
                                                           Uncensored =    158
Limits: Lower =    0                                    Left-censored =     42
        Upper = +inf                                   Right-censored =      0

                                                    LR chi2(2)        = 112.26
                                                    Prob > chi2       = 0.0000
Log likelihood = -252.67183                         Pseudo R2         = 0.1818

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
       ycens | Coefficient  Std. err.      t    P>|t|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
          x1 |    .849565   .0730346    11.63   0.000     .7055396    .9935905
          x2 |   .0661611   .0717033     0.92   0.357    -.0752391    .2075613
       _cons |   .9811939   .0729483    13.45   0.000     .8373387    1.125049
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
 var(e.ycens)|   .9382956   .1089417                      .7462808    1.179715
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
```

**Notes:** `tobit` stores NO `e(aic)`; `estat_ic` computes AIC and BIC on demand instead, so the criterion is still available. `predict` offers `'xb'`, `'stdp'`, `'stdf'`, `'e'`, `'ystar'`, `'pr'` and `'residuals'`; `'e'` and `'ystar'` take `lower=`/`upper=` for the censoring limits, and `'pr'` without them returns 1 for every observation because the default interval covers the whole line. Only `cov_type='nonrobust'` is implemented.

### `Truncreg`

**Stata:** `truncreg y x1 x2, ll(0)`

Truncated regression -- Stata's `truncreg`. Each observation contributes the normal density divided by the probability of lying inside the limits, and at least one limit is required. Observations at or outside the limits are dropped from the estimation sample and the count is reported: `e(N_bf)` is the number of observations before truncation, so `e(N_bf) - e(N)` is how many were dropped, which is what Stata prints as `(N obs truncated)`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.Truncreg(endog: 'Any', exog: 'Any', *, lower: 'float | None' = None, upper: 'float | None' = None, param_names: 'list[str] | None' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`Truncreg.from_formula`**

```python
Truncreg.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, lower: 'float | None' = None, upper: 'float | None' = None, subset: 'Any' = None) -> "'Truncreg'"
```

**`Truncreg.fit`**

```python
Truncreg.fit(self, cov_type: 'str | None' = None, cov_kwds: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, ssc: 'Any' = None, *, maxiter: 'int' = 500, tol: 'float' = 1e-10, strict: 'bool' = False) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.Truncreg.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base, lower=0.0).fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text

Limit: Lower =    0                             Number of obs     =  151
       Upper = +inf                             Wald chi2(2)      = 65.66
                                                Prob > chi2       = 0.0000
Log likelihood = -169.3211                      Pseudo R2         = 0.1635

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
           y | Coefficient  Std. err.      z    P>|z|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
          x1 |    .663382   .0974009     6.81   0.000     .4724797    .8542842
          x2 |  -.4890241   .0918261    -5.33   0.000    -.6689999   -.3090484
       _cons |   1.234907   .1145149    10.78   0.000     1.010462    1.459352
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
      /sigma |   .9158148   .0717033                      .7752789    1.056351
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
```

**Notes:** Unlike `TruncatedPoisson`, this class drops the out-of-range rows itself and warns instead of raising, because `truncreg`'s sample really is defined by the limits. The dropped count is an attribute on the model (`_n_truncated`) and is NOT stored in `e()`: `e()` follows `truncreg.sthlp`, which has no such scalar -- use `e(N_bf) - e(N)`. `predict` offers `'xb'`, `'stdp'`, `'stdf'`, `'residuals'` and `'fitted'`.

### `Intreg`

**Stata:** `intreg lo hi x1`

Interval regression -- Stata's `intreg`, written with the two-sided formula `'lo hi ~ x'`. Only the interval is known for each observation: `lower < upper` is interval-censored, `lower == upper` an exactly observed value, and a missing bound means that side is censored. `e(N_int)`, `e(N_lc)`, `e(N_rc)` and `e(N_unc)` count the four kinds, `/lnsigma` is estimated and `e(sigma)` derived.

**Signature**

```python
ws.Intreg(lower: 'Any', upper: 'Any', exog: 'Any', *, param_names: 'list[str] | None' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`Intreg.from_formula`**

```python
Intreg.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, subset: 'Any' = None) -> "'Intreg'"
```

**`Intreg.fit`**

```python
Intreg.fit(self, cov_type: 'str | None' = None, cov_kwds: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, ssc: 'Any' = None, *, maxiter: 'int' = 500, tol: 'float' = 1e-10, strict: 'bool' = False) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
d = base.assign(lo=np.floor(base.y1), hi=np.floor(base.y1) + 1)
ws.Intreg.from_formula('lo hi ~ x1', d).fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text

Interval regression                                 Number of obs     =    200
                                                           Uncensored =      0
                                                        Left-censored =      0
                                                       Right-censored =      0
                                                    Interval-cens.    =    200

                                                    LR chi2(1)        =  59.68
                                                    Prob > chi2       = 0.0000
Log likelihood = -292.97704                         Pseudo R2         = 0.0924

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
       lo hi | Coefficient  Std. err.      z    P>|z|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
          x1 |   .6033076   .0723365     8.34   0.000     .4615308    .7450845
       _cons |   .9150882   .0741438    12.34   0.000      .769769    1.060408
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
    /lnsigma |   .0063061   .0540379                     -.0996063    .1122185
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
       sigma |   1.006326   .0543798                      .9051937    1.118757
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
```

**Notes:** If every `hi - lo` is tiny relative to the model's residual scale, `sigma` is driven to zero and the standard errors of the other parameters become unusable above the reported values -- the fit is then essentially an exact interpolation, so pass intervals whose width is realistic for the outcome. `predict` offers `'xb'`, `'stdp'`, `'stdf'`, `'e'`, `'ystar'`, `'pr'` and `'fitted'`; `'e'`, `'ystar'` and `'fitted'` all return the linear prediction here, and there is no `'residuals'` type.

### `Heckman`

**Stata:** `heckman yselect x1, select(sel = x3)`

Heckman sample-selection model -- Stata's `heckman`, written `'y ~ x + select(s ~ z)'`. The selection equation is a probit on the full sample; the outcome equation is fitted only where `s == 1`, and a MISSING `y` is what marks a non-selected observation, so the outcome column may be NaN off the selected rows. `fit(method='twostep')` (the default) is the two-step estimator with the inverse Mills ratio added as a regressor and `e(lambda) = rho*sigma`; `fit(method='ml')` fits the joint likelihood with `/athrho` and `/lnsigma`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.Heckman(outcome: 'np.ndarray', outcome_exog: 'np.ndarray', selection: 'np.ndarray', selection_exog: 'np.ndarray', *, outcome_names: 'Sequence[str]', selection_names: 'Sequence[str]', selected: 'np.ndarray', model_data: 'pd.DataFrame | None' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`Heckman.from_formula`**

```python
Heckman.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, subset: 'Any' = None) -> "'Heckman'"
```

**`Heckman.fit`**

```python
Heckman.fit(self, cov_type: 'str | None' = None, cov_kwds: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, ssc: 'Any' = None, *, method: 'str' = 'twostep', maxiter: 'int' = 200, strict: 'bool' = False) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.Heckman.from_formula('yselect ~ x1 + select(sel ~ x3)', base).fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text

Heckman selection model                                 Number of obs =    124
                                                        Wald chi2(1)  = 228.30
                                                        Prob > chi2   = 0.0000
Log likelihood = -94.069719                             Pseudo R2     = 0.4061

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
             | Coefficient  Std. err.      z    P>|z|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
y            |
          x1 |   .6667734    .044129    15.11   0.000     .5802821    .7532646
      lambda |  -.1293741   .1393244    -0.93   0.353    -.4024449    .1436968
       _cons |   1.026394   .0807803    12.71   0.000     .8680676     1.18472
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
select       |
          x3 |   .8066883   .1199658     6.72   0.000     .5715598    1.041817
       _cons |   .4765848    .105997     4.50   0.000     .2688345     .684335
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
         rho | -.24996944   (fraction of variance due to u_i)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
```

**Notes:** The two-step result stores NO `e(aic)` (the two-step estimator is not an ML fit of the joint model) and `e(N_selected)`/`e(N_nonselected)` split the sample, while `e(N)` is the full sample size even though the printed `Number of obs` is the selected count. On the ML path `e(aic)` IS stored and equals `-2*e(ll) + 2*k`, agreeing with `estat_ic`. Standard errors are the two-step M-estimator sandwich, not naive OLS ones. `predict` offers `'xb'`, `'stdp'`, `'stdf'`, `'fitted'`, `'psel'`, `'ycond'`, `'yexpected'`, `'mills'` and `'nshazard'`.

### `Etregress`

**Stata:** `etregress ytreat x1, treat(treat = x3)`

Endogenous treatment regression by the control-function method -- Stata's `etregress`, written `'y ~ x + treat(t ~ z)'`. A probit treatment equation is estimated on the full sample and its generalized residual is added to the outcome regression, which absorbs the correlation between the two errors; unlike `Heckman`, `y` is observed for treated and control units alike, so every row enters the outcome equation. The treatment indicator is added to the outcome equation automatically, and naming it there as well does not duplicate it.

**Signature**

```python
ws.Etregress(outcome: 'np.ndarray', outcome_exog: 'np.ndarray', treatment: 'np.ndarray', treatment_exog: 'np.ndarray', *, outcome_names: 'Sequence[str]', treatment_names: 'Sequence[str]', treated: 'np.ndarray', model_data: 'pd.DataFrame | None' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`Etregress.from_formula`**

```python
Etregress.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, subset: 'Any' = None) -> "'Etregress'"
```

**`Etregress.fit`**

```python
Etregress.fit(self, cov_type: 'str | None' = None, cov_kwds: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, ssc: 'Any' = None, *, method: 'str' = 'control', maxiter: 'int' = 200, strict: 'bool' = False) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.Etregress.from_formula('ytreat ~ x1 + treat(treat ~ x3)', base).fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text

Linear regression with endogenous treatment             Number of obs =    200
Estimator: Two-step                                     Wald chi2(2)  = 142.69
                                                        Prob > chi2   = 0.0000
Log likelihood = -281.73931                             Pseudo R2     = 0.2059

---------------------------------------------------------------------------------
                | Coefficient  Std. err.      z    P>|z|     [95% conf. interval]
----------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
----------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
y               |
          _cons |   .8850983   .1885729     4.69   0.000     .5155021    1.254694
             x1 |    .776287   .0686343    11.31   0.000     .6417662    .9108077
          treat |   1.539547   .2729629     5.64   0.000      1.00455    2.074545
         lambda |  -.0033512   .1888875    -0.02   0.986    -.3735639    .3668615
----------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
treat           |
          _cons |   .5573098   .1093099     5.10   0.000     .3430664    .7715532
             x3 |   .8581738   .1263191     6.79   0.000     .6105928    1.105755
---------------------------------------------------------------------------------
----------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
         rho | -.00342009   (fraction of variance due to u_i)
---------------------------------------------------------------------------------
```

**Notes:** `e(N)` is the full sample and `e(N_treated)` the treated count. Standard errors are the two-step M-estimator sandwich (`e(se_corrected) = 1`), not naive OLS ones and not Stata's ML ones. `predict` offers `'xb'`, `'stdp'`, `'stdf'`, `'fitted'`, `'cte'`, `'yctrt'`, `'ycntrt'`, `'ptrt'` and `'xbtrt'`, i.e. the treated and untreated conditional means, the treatment probability and the treatment-equation index.

### `Frontier`

**Stata:** `frontier y x1 x2, distribution(halfnormal)`

Stochastic production frontier -- Stata's `frontier`. The model is `y = x'b + v - u` with a symmetric two-sided error `v` and a one-sided inefficiency `u`; `distribution='halfnormal'` (the default) or `'exponential'` is accepted, and the truncated-normal variants are not implemented and raise `UnsupportedError`. The table reports `/lnsig2v` and `/lnsig2u` on the log-variance scale, and `e()` carries the derived `sigma_v`, `sigma_u`, `sigma2_v`, `sigma2_u`, `lambda` and `gamma`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.Frontier(endog: 'Any', exog: 'Any', *, param_names: 'Sequence[str]', distribution: 'str' = 'halfnormal', model_data: 'pd.DataFrame | None' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`Frontier.from_formula`**

```python
Frontier.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, distribution: 'str' = 'halfnormal', subset: 'Any' = None) -> "'Frontier'"
```

**`Frontier.fit`**

```python
Frontier.fit(self, cov_type: 'str | None' = None, cov_kwds: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, ssc: 'Any' = None, *, maxiter: 'int' = 500, strict: 'bool' = False) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.Frontier.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text

Stoc. frontier normal/half-normal model                 Number of obs =    200
                                                        Wald chi2(2)  = 180.57
Log likelihood = -291.49689                             Prob > chi2   = 0.0000

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
           y | Coefficient  Std. err.      z    P>|z|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
          x1 |   .8347526   .0727708    11.47   0.000     .6921246    .9773807
          x2 |  -.5112541   .0737976    -6.93   0.000    -.6558948   -.3666135
       _cons |   1.768313   .3472315     5.09   0.000     1.087752    2.448874
    /lnsig2v |  -.2832233   .3824229    -0.74   0.459    -1.032758    .4663118
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
    /lnsig2u |  -.0917742   .8897731                     -1.835697    1.652149
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
     sigma_u |   .9551498                                        0           .
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
```

**Notes:** There is no `e(aic)` or `e(bic)`, and the model test is a Wald statistic (`e(chi2type)='Wald'`), with the likelihood-ratio statistic against the no-inefficiency model in `e(lr_chi2)` and its p-value in `e(p_lr)`. `predict` registers no types for `frontier`, so all prediction goes through the macros or by hand.

### `XtLogit`

**Stata:** `xtlogit yb x1, fe`

Panel logit -- Stata's `xtlogit`, with `method='fe'`, `'re'` or `'pa'`, and `model_name` set to `'xtlogit, fe'`, `'xtlogit, re'` or `'xtlogit, pa'` accordingly. `fe` is Chamberlain's conditional likelihood: groups whose outcome never varies are dropped, `e(N_drop)` and `e(N_group_drop)` record the cost, there is no intercept and the model test is an LR. `re` is an integrated likelihood with a Gaussian random effect by adaptive Gauss-Hermite quadrature, storing `/lnsig2u` as a parameter plus `e(sigma_u)` and `e(rho)`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.XtLogit(endog: 'Any', exog: 'Any', groups: 'Any', *, param_names: 'Sequence[str]', method: 'str', model_data: 'pd.DataFrame | None' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`XtLogit.from_formula`**

```python
XtLogit.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, entity: 'str', time: 'str | None' = None, method: 'str' = 're', subset: 'Any' = None) -> "'XtLogit'"
```

**`XtLogit.fit`**

```python
XtLogit.fit(self, cov_type: 'str | None' = None, cov_kwds: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, ssc: 'Any' = None, *, intpoints: 'int' = 12, maxiter: 'int' = 500, strict: 'bool' = False) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.XtLogit.from_formula('yb ~ x1', base, entity='firm', method='fe').fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text
note: multiple positive outcomes within groups encountered.
note: 3 groups (15 obs) omitted because of all positive or
      all negative outcomes.

Conditional fixed-effects logistic regression        Number of obs    =    185
Group variable: firm                                 Number of groups =     37

                                                     Obs per group:
                                                                  min =      5
                                                                  avg =    5.0
                                                                  max =      5

                                                     LR chi2(1)       =  13.54
Log likelihood = -71.492167                          Prob > chi2      = 0.0002

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
          yb | Coefficient  Std. err.      z    P>|z|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
          x1 |   .6103907   .1789254     3.41   0.001     .2597033    .9610781
```

**Notes:** `method='pa'` is a GEE with an exchangeable working correlation and is NOT a likelihood: it stores no `e(ll)`, `e(ll_0)`, `e(r2_p)`, `e(aic)` or `e(bic)`, its model test is a Wald statistic and `e(estimator)='gee'` marks it. `e(ll_0)` on the `fe` path is the intercept-only logit on the retained observations, which is what makes the LR statistic comparable. `predict` registers no types for any of the three.

### `XtProbit`

**Stata:** `xtprobit ybp x1, re`

Panel probit -- Stata's `xtprobit`, random effects only. It is the same integral as `xtlogit, re` with the normal CDF in place of the logistic one, and it stores `/lnsig2u` as a parameter plus `e(sigma_u)` and `e(rho)`. Any other `method` raises `UnsupportedError`: Stata offers no fixed-effects or population-averaged `xtprobit` in this shape, because there is no sufficient statistic to condition on for the probit.

**Signature**

```python
ws.XtProbit(endog: 'Any', exog: 'Any', groups: 'Any', *, param_names: 'Sequence[str]', method: 'str', model_data: 'pd.DataFrame | None' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`XtProbit.from_formula`**

```python
XtProbit.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, entity: 'str', time: 'str | None' = None, method: 'str' = 're', subset: 'Any' = None) -> "'XtProbit'"
```

**`XtProbit.fit`**

```python
XtProbit.fit(self, cov_type: 'str | None' = None, cov_kwds: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, ssc: 'Any' = None, *, intpoints: 'int' = 12, maxiter: 'int' = 500, strict: 'bool' = False) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
d = base.assign(ybp=(base.ypanel > 1).astype(int))
ws.XtProbit.from_formula('ybp ~ x1', d, entity='firm', method='re').fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text

Random-effects probit regression                     Number of obs    =    200
Group variable: firm                                 Number of groups =     40

Random effects u_i ~ Gaussian                        Obs per group:
                                                                  min =      5
                                                                  avg =    5.0
                                                                  max =      5

Integration method: mvaghermite                      Integration pts. =     12

                                                     Wald chi2(1)     =  31.47
Log likelihood = -115.42465                          Prob > chi2      = 0.0000

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
         ybp | Coefficient  Std. err.      z    P>|z|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
          x1 |   .6975478    .124335     5.61   0.000     .4538557    .9412399
       _cons |  -.1688001   .1333415    -1.27   0.206    -.4301446    .0925444
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
    /lnsig2u |  -1.211642   .6409358                     -2.467853    .0445689
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
     sigma_u |   .5456262                                        0           .
         rho |   .2294106                                        0           .
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
LR test of rho=0: chibar2(01) = 5.54                   Prob >= chibar2 = 0.009
```

**Notes:** `method='fe'` and `method='pa'` are refused by name with `method='fe' is not available; supported: 're'` (and the same for `'pa'`), not silently mapped to `re`. On a binary outcome with little within-group correlation the estimate of `/lnsig2u` can be driven far negative, so `sigma_u` is effectively zero -- that is the model hitting the boundary, not a failure to converge.

### `XtPoisson`

**Stata:** `xtpoisson ycnt x1, fe`

Panel Poisson -- Stata's `xtpoisson`, with `method='fe'` or `'re'`. `fe` conditions on the group total `S_i = sum_t y_it`, which removes the group effect exactly (the multinomial reduction), so it is a genuine likelihood and groups with `S_i = 0` are dropped. `re` uses a gamma random effect, which is conjugate to the Poisson, so the group likelihood is closed form and no quadrature is involved; the dispersion is reported as `/lnalpha` and `e(alpha)` as its exponential.

**Signature**

```python
ws.XtPoisson(endog: 'Any', exog: 'Any', groups: 'Any', *, param_names: 'Sequence[str]', method: 'str', model_data: 'pd.DataFrame | None' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`XtPoisson.from_formula`**

```python
XtPoisson.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, entity: 'str', time: 'str | None' = None, method: 'str' = 're', subset: 'Any' = None) -> "'XtPoisson'"
```

**`XtPoisson.fit`**

```python
XtPoisson.fit(self, cov_type: 'str | None' = None, cov_kwds: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, ssc: 'Any' = None, *, intpoints: 'int' = 12, maxiter: 'int' = 500, strict: 'bool' = False) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.XtPoisson.from_formula('ycnt ~ x1', base, entity='firm', method='fe').fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text

Conditional fixed-effects Poisson regression         Number of obs    =    200
Group variable: firm                                 Number of groups =     40

                                                     Obs per group:
                                                                  min =      5
                                                                  avg =    5.0
                                                                  max =      5

                                                     Wald chi2(1)     =  25.31
Log likelihood = -203.70227                          Prob > chi2      = 0.0000

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
        ycnt | Coefficient  Std. err.      z    P>|z|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
          x1 |   .3128918   .0621931     5.03   0.000     .1909956     .434788
```

**Notes:** `method='pa'` is refused (`supported: 'fe', 're'`). The `fe` likelihood has no intercept -- the group effect absorbs it, and the likelihood is exactly flat in a constant -- so the patsy intercept is removed rather than estimated. `e(ivar)` and `e(tvar)` are not populated for this class, so the printed `Group variable:` line shows `.` even though `e(N_g)` does count the groups. `predict` registers no types.

### `XtNbreg`

**Stata:** `xtnbreg ycnt x1, fe`

Conditional fixed-effects negative binomial -- Stata's `xtnbreg, fe`, and only `fe` is available. Stata maximises the Hausman-Hall-Griliches (1984) conditional kernel, which is not the conditional Poisson kernel: the dispersion enters through the `ln Gamma(lam + y) - ln Gamma(lam)` terms, which is why `xtnbreg, fe` and `xtpoisson, fe` report different standard errors on the same data. `e(estimator)='hhg_conditional_ml'` and `e(quasi_likelihood)=1` mark it, and `e(ll)` is the maximised conditional log-kernel -- the value Stata prints under `Log likelihood`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.XtNbreg(endog: 'Any', exog: 'Any', groups: 'Any', *, param_names: 'Sequence[str]', method: 'str', model_data: 'pd.DataFrame | None' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`XtNbreg.from_formula`**

```python
XtNbreg.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, entity: 'str', time: 'str | None' = None, method: 'str' = 'fe', subset: 'Any' = None) -> "'XtNbreg'"
```

**`XtNbreg.fit`**

```python
XtNbreg.fit(self, cov_type: 'str | None' = None, cov_kwds: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, ssc: 'Any' = None, *, intpoints: 'int' = 12, maxiter: 'int' = 500, strict: 'bool' = False) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.XtNbreg.from_formula('ycnt ~ x1', base, entity='firm', method='fe').fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text

Conditional FE negative binomial regression          Number of obs    =    200
Group variable: firm                                 Number of groups =     40

                                                     Obs per group:
                                                                  min =      5
                                                                  avg =    5.0
                                                                  max =      5

                                                     Wald chi2(1)     =  25.31
Log likelihood = -203.70227                          Prob > chi2      = 0.0000

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
        ycnt | Coefficient  Std. err.      z    P>|z|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
          x1 |   .3128918   .0621933     5.03   0.000     .1909953    .4347884
       _cons |   23.17064   38374.96     0.00   1.000    -75190.37    75236.71
```

**Notes:** The objective is a quasi-likelihood, so `e(ll)` is not a likelihood of the data and no AIC/BIC is stored. `_cons` is part of the parameter vector as in Stata, but the objective is nearly flat in it -- adding a constant to every `lam_it` barely moves the kernel -- so its estimate (and its enormous standard error) is optimiser-dependent while the slopes are not; dropping the constant would move the slopes, so it is kept.

### `XtTobit`

**Stata:** `xttobit ycensp x1, re`

Random-effects Tobit -- Stata's `xttobit`, random effects only. The latent outcome is `y*_it = x_it'b + u_i + e_it` with `u_i ~ N(0, sigma_u^2)` and `e_it ~ N(0, sigma_e^2)`, censored at `lower` and/or `upper`. Both variance components are reported as `sigma_u` and `sigma_e`, and `e(rho) = sigma_u^2/(sigma_u^2 + sigma_e^2)`; the integration is Stata's adaptive Gauss-Hermite (`e(intmethod)='mvaghermite'`) with `e(intpoints)` defaulting to 12.

**Signature**

```python
ws.XtTobit(*args: 'Any', lower: 'float | None' = 0.0, upper: 'float | None' = None, **kwargs: 'Any') -> 'None'
```

**`XtTobit.from_formula`**

```python
XtTobit.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, entity: 'str', time: 'str | None' = None, lower: 'float | None' = 0.0, upper: 'float | None' = None, method: 'str' = 're', subset: 'Any' = None) -> "'XtTobit'"
```

**`XtTobit.fit`**

```python
XtTobit.fit(self, cov_type: 'str | None' = None, cov_kwds: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, ssc: 'Any' = None, *, intpoints: 'int' = 12, maxiter: 'int' = 500, strict: 'bool' = False) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
d = base.assign(ycensp=np.clip(base.ypanel, 0.0, None))
ws.XtTobit.from_formula('ycensp ~ x1', d, entity='firm', method='re').fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text

Random-effects tobit regression                      Number of obs    =    200
Group variable: .                                    Number of groups =     40

Random effects u_i ~ Gaussian                        Obs per group:
                                                                  min =      5
                                                                  avg =    5.0
                                                                  max =      5

Integration method: mvaghermite                      Integration pts. =     12

                                                     Wald chi2(1)     =  80.89
Log likelihood = -271.51546                          Prob > chi2      = 0.0000

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
             | Coefficient  Std. err.      z    P>|z|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
          x1 |   .7848094   .0872602     8.99   0.000     .6137825    .9558363
     sigma_u |   .8057239   .1338431     6.02   0.000     .5433962    1.068052
     sigma_e |   1.066372    .073274    14.55   0.000     .9227576    1.209987
       _cons |     .76606   .1536457     4.99   0.000       .46492      1.0672
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
         rho |   .3634196                                        0           .
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
LR test of rho=0: chibar2(01) = 89.96                  Prob >= chibar2 = 0.000
```

**Notes:** `method='fe'` is refused (`supported: 're'`). The number of quadrature points buys accuracy only up to a point -- 12 is Stata's default and this module's default, and more points do not change the estimates on well-behaved panels. `e(ivar)` is not populated for this class either, so the header's `Group variable:` shows `.` while `e(N_g)` still counts the groups. `predict` registers no types for `xttobit`.


## Time series, multivariate and panel roots

### `ARIMA`

**Stata:** `arima y, arima(1,1,0)`

ARIMA and SARIMA estimation by maximum likelihood, wrapping statsmodels' state-space ARIMA. `fit()` returns a `StataResults` with `dist="z"`, so the header prints a Wald chi-square like Stata's `arima` rather than an F test. The `trend` default is `"n"` -- no constant -- because that is Stata's default too; ask for `trend="c"` explicitly when the specification has an intercept. `e(ar_max)` and `e(ma_max)` are the effective lag orders including the seasonal order multiplied through, as Stata reports them.

**Signature**

```python
ws.ARIMA(endog: 'Any', order: 'tuple[int, int, int]' = (1, 0, 0), seasonal_order: 'tuple[int, int, int, int]' = (0, 0, 0, 0), *, trend: 'str' = 'n') -> 'None'
```

**`ARIMA.fit`**

```python
ARIMA.fit(self, cov_type: 'str | None' = None, cov_kwds: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, ssc: 'Any' = None, *, maxiter: 'int' = 500, strict: 'bool' = False) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.ARIMA(pd.Series(np.cumsum(np.random.default_rng(1).normal(size=120))), order=(1, 1, 0)).fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text
ARIMA regression

Sample: 2 thru 120                              Number of obs     =        119
                                                Wald chi2(1)      =       0.53
Log likelihood = -149.227                       Prob > chi2       =          .

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
           y | Coefficient  Std. err.      z    P>|z|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
ARMA         |
          ar |
         L1. |  -.0579121   .0795261    -0.73   0.466    -.2137804    .0979562
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
      /sigma |   .8479354   .0501061    16.92   0.000     .7497292    .9461416
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
```

**Notes:** For an integrated model `e(N)` is the estimation-sample size after differencing (119 here for a 120-point I(1) series), while the printed "Number of obs" is the full series length -- the two legitimately differ and Stata's own `estat ic` uses the smaller one. `fit()` also accepts only `cov_type="nonrobust"` and raises for anything else.

### `GARCH`

**Stata:** `arch y, arch(1) garch(1)`

GARCH and EGARCH volatility models, the counterpart of Stata's `arch`. Parameters are `alpha[i]` (ARCH), `beta[i]` (GARCH), `gamma[i]` (asymmetry, enabled by `o=1` for GJR-GARCH), plus `mu` and `omega`; `vol="EGARCH"` switches the volatility equation and `dist="t"` switches the innovation distribution. Two defaults are deliberately Stata's rather than the underlying `arch` package's: `cov_type="opg"` (BHHH standard errors, Stata's `e(vcetype) = "OPG"`) and `presample="stata"`, which starts the variance recursion from the mean squared demeaned residual instead of an exponentially weighted backcast.

**Signature**

```python
ws.GARCH(endog: 'Any', *, p: 'int' = 1, q: 'int' = 1, o: 'int' = 0, vol: 'str' = 'GARCH', dist: 'str' = 'normal', mean: 'str' = 'Constant') -> 'None'
```

**`GARCH.fit`**

```python
GARCH.fit(self, *, strict: 'bool' = False, maxiter: 'int' = 1000, cov_type: 'str' = 'opg', presample: 'str' = 'stata', max_presample_iter: 'int' = 5) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.GARCH(pd.Series(np.random.default_rng(1).normal(size=200) * 0.01), p=1, q=1).fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text
ARCH family regression

Sample: 1 thru 200                              Number of obs     =        200
                                                Wald chi2(.)      =          .
Log likelihood = 654.1268                       Prob > chi2       =          .

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
             |                 OPG
           y | Coefficient  std. err.      z    P>|z|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
y            |
       _cons |  -.0007742   .0006318    -1.23   0.220    -.0020125    .0004641
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
ARCH         |
        arch |
         L1. |   .1118666   .0934251     1.20   0.231    -.0712433    .2949765
       garch |
         L1. |   .5372114   .3791344     1.42   0.156    -.2058783    1.280301
       _cons |   .0000304   .0000294     1.04   0.301    -.0000272    .0000879
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
```

**Notes:** Neither override makes the estimates identical to Stata's: the documented residual gap on the reference GARCH(1,1) series is about 1.8e-4 relative in the parameters and 1.5e-4 in the standard errors, and the source module traces it to Stata's first-observation handling plus its stopping point rather than to a formula difference. `e(archi)` records the initial variance actually used.

### `VAR`

**Stata:** `var a b, lags(1)`

Vector autoregression over several series, the counterpart of Stata's `var`. The endog argument is an `(n_obs, n_variables)` array and `names` labels the equations. `fit()` returns a `VARResults`, not a `StataResults`: by default it reports the maximum-likelihood residual covariance (dividing each equation's RSS by `T`, which is what Stata stores in `e(Sigma)` and `e(V)`), and `ml=False` reproduces Stata's `var ..., dfk` instead. `select_order()` returns the AIC/BIC/HQIC/FPE table Stata's `varsoc` prints rather than silently picking one lag order.

**Signature**

```python
ws.VAR(endog: 'Any', lags: 'int | None' = 1, *, trend: 'str' = 'c', names: 'Sequence[str] | None' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`VAR.fit`**

```python
VAR.fit(self, cov_type: 'str | None' = None, cov_kwds: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, ssc: 'Any' = None, *, ml: 'bool' = True) -> "'VARResults'"
```

**Example**

```python
d = np.column_stack([np.cumsum(np.random.default_rng(1).normal(size=100)), np.cumsum(np.random.default_rng(2).normal(size=100))])
ws.VAR(d, lags=1, names=['a', 'b']).fit().estat_ic()
```

**Output**

```text

Akaike's information criterion and Bayesian information criterion

-----------------------------------------------------------------------------
       Model |          N   ll(null)  ll(model)      df        AIC        BIC
-------------+---------------------------------------------------------------
           . |         99          .  -255.3751       6   522.7501   538.3208
-----------------------------------------------------------------------------
Note: BIC uses N = number of observations. See [R] IC note.
```

**Notes:** `VARResults` has no `.summary()`, so `summary(style="stata")` raises `AttributeError`; use `estat_ic()`, `estat_summarize()`, `stata_params`/`stata_param_names`, `granger_causality()` or `irf()`. Also, `granger_causality()` resolves the caused/causing names against the **fitted result's** own names, not against the `names=` you passed to `VAR` -- a `names=['a','b']` model still answers to `y1`/`y2`, and naming it `'a'` raises `ValueError`.

### `VECM`

**Stata:** `vec a b, rank(1) lags(1)`

Vector error-correction model, the counterpart of Stata's `vec`. `coint_rank` is the cointegrating rank, normally read off `johansen()` first; that method is the `vecrank` counterpart and returns trace and max-eigenvalue statistics with the critical values Stata prints. Coefficient accessors are `beta` (cointegrating vectors), `alpha` (adjustment speeds) and `gamma` (short-run terms), and the `e()` payload carries Stata's names such as `k_ce`, `k_ar_diff`, `r2_1` and `r2_2`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.VECM(endog: 'Any', *, coint_rank: 'int' = 1, lags: 'int' = 1, deterministic: 'str' = 'co', names: 'Sequence[str] | None' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`VECM.fit`**

```python
VECM.fit(self, cov_type: 'str | None' = None) -> 'Any'
```

**Example**

```python
d = np.column_stack([np.cumsum(np.random.default_rng(1).normal(size=100)), np.cumsum(np.random.default_rng(2).normal(size=100))])
ws.VECM(d, coint_rank=1, lags=1, names=['a', 'b']).fit().beta
```

**Output**

```text
[[1.      ]
 [3.174865]]
```

**Notes:** The critical values in `johansen()` are Stata's Johansen (1995) tables, not statsmodels' Osterwald-Lenum values, and the difference changes the selected rank on marginal data (3.76 against 3.8415 at 5%). The 90% column is `nan` because Stata's ado hard-codes only 95% and 99%. `VECMResults` has no `.summary()` either -- read `beta`/`alpha`/`gamma` and `e()` directly.

### `XtUnitroot`

**Stata:** `xtunitroot ips ypanel   /   xtunitroot llc ypanel`

Panel unit-root tests by the Im-Pesaran-Shin (`test="ips"`) and Levin-Lin-Chu (`test="llc"`) procedures, matching Stata's `xtunitroot`. `from_formula` takes the panel identifiers through `entity=` and `time=`; `fit()` returns a `PanelUnitRootResult` that carries Stata's `r()` scalars, since `xtunitroot` is an r-class command and stores no `e(b)` or `e(V)`. Both procedures require a strongly balanced panel without gaps, and this implementation refuses an unbalanced one rather than silently using whichever rows happen to line up.

**Signature**

```python
ws.XtUnitroot(values: 'Any', codes: 'np.ndarray', sizes: 'np.ndarray', *, test: 'str' = 'ips', lags: 'int | None' = None, trend: 'bool' = False, noconstant: 'bool' = False, demean: 'bool' = False, variable_name: 'str | None' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`XtUnitroot.from_formula`**

```python
XtUnitroot.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, entity: 'str', time: 'str | None' = None, test: 'str' = 'ips', lags: 'int | None' = None, trend: 'bool' = False, noconstant: 'bool' = False, demean: 'bool' = False, subset: 'Any' = None) -> "'XtUnitroot'"
```

**`XtUnitroot.fit`**

```python
XtUnitroot.fit(self) -> 'PanelUnitRootResult'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.XtUnitroot.from_formula('ypanel ~ 1', base, entity='firm', time='year', test='ips').fit().r
```

**Output**

```text
{'test': 'ips', 't-bar': -2.443828941269152, 't-tilde-bar': -1.3462848052625447, 'Z-t-bar': -4.423070314618218, 'N_t': 5.0, 'lags': 0.0, 'Zt': -1.9851960161510092, 'p': 0.02356133369222999}
```

**Notes:** `trend=True`, `noconstant=True` and `demean=True` are accepted as arguments but raise `UnsupportedError`, because each changes the look-up tables and they were not transcribed. `lags=1` on a short panel can also raise `ConsistencyError` ("a panel has no residual degrees of freedom in its ADF regression") -- the default of no lags is what Stata's default reproduces.

### `IpsResult`

**Stata:** `xtunitroot ips ypanel   (the r() results it returns)`

The object `im_pesaran_shin` and `XtUnitroot(test="ips").fit()` return: the t-bar, t-tilde-bar and Z-t-tilde-bar statistics, the p-value attached to the Z statistic, panel dimensions and the interpolation moments. `str()` prints the block Stata's `xtunitroot ips` prints, and the `r` property maps the fields onto Stata's `r()` scalars (`r(N)`, `r(N_g)`, `r(N_t)`, `r(tbar)`, `r(ttildebar)`, `r(Zt)`, `r(p)`).

**Signature**

```python
ws.IpsResult(tbar: 'float', ttildebar: 'float', zt: 'float', zttildebar: 'float', pvalue: 'float', critical: 'dict[str, float]', n_groups: 'int', n_periods: 'int', observations: 'int', mean_t: 'float', var_t: 'float', mean_ttilde: 'float', var_ttilde: 'float') -> None
```

**Example**

```python
ws.im_pesaran_shin(base, variable='ypanel', entity='firm', time='year')
```

**Output**

```text
Im-Pesaran-Shin unit-root test
  t-bar     -2.4438
  t-tilde-bar -1.3463
  Z-t-tilde-bar -1.9852  p = 0.0236
  N = 40 panels x 5 periods
```

**Notes:** `critical` is an empty dict in practice: the fixed-N exact critical values (IPS Table 2) are not implemented and the code raises `UnsupportedError` rather than filling in a number from a different table, so `r()` carries no `cv_1`/`cv_5`/`cv_10`. Only t-bar has no p-value, exactly as in Stata.

### `LlcResult`

**Stata:** `xtunitroot llc ypanel   (the r() results it returns)`

The object `levin_lin_chu` and `XtUnitroot(test="llc").fit()` return: the unadjusted t (`td`), the bias-adjusted t* (`tds`), the long-run to short-run standard-deviation ratio `sbar`, the adjustment moments `mu_adj`/`sig_adj`, the Bartlett-kernel bandwidth `hac_lags` and the ADF lag order `adf_lags`. `str()` prints the three-line block Stata's `xtunitroot llc` prints, and `pvalue_tds` is the lower-tail normal p-value of the adjusted statistic.

**Signature**

```python
ws.LlcResult(tds: 'float', td: 'float', ttilde: 'float', sbar: 'float', nu_hat: 'float', nu_se: 'float', var_ep: 'float', mu_adj: 'float', sig_adj: 'float', pvalue_tds: 'float', pvalue_td: 'float | None', n_groups: 'int', n_periods: 'int', observations: 'int', adf_lags: 'int', hac_lags: 'int') -> None
```

**Example**

```python
ws.levin_lin_chu(base, variable='ypanel', entity='firm', time='year')
```

**Output**

```text
Levin-Lin-Chu unit-root test
  Unadjusted t   -15.8380
  Adjusted t*    -17.2339   p = 0.0000
  40 panels x 5 periods
```

**Notes:** Unlike `IpsResult` and `UnitRootResult`, `LlcResult` exposes no `r` property, so the Stata `r()` names have to be assembled from the fields by hand. `pvalue_td` is `None` unless the no-constant model is selected, because Stata only tabulates a p-value for that case.

### `UnitRootResult`

**Stata:** `dfuller y   /   pperron y   /   kpss y`

The object `ws.unitroot` returns for a univariate series: `test` (the descriptive name), `statistic`, `pvalue`, the lag order or Newey-West truncation actually used, the observation count, the critical-value band and the null hypothesis in words. `str()` prints the block Stata's `dfuller`/`pperron`/`kpss` print, and the `r` property exposes the `r()` scalars under Stata's names -- `r(Zt)`, `r(N)`, `r(lags)`, `r(p)` (or `r(pval)` for `pperron`, `r(stat)` for `kpss`) and `r(cv_1)`/`r(cv_5)`/`r(cv_10)`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.UnitRootResult(test: 'str', statistic: 'float', pvalue: 'float', lags: 'int' = 0, observations: 'int' = 0, critical: 'Mapping[str, float]' = <factory>, null: 'str' = '', regression: 'str' = 'c') -> None
```

**Example**

```python
ws.unitroot(np.cumsum(np.random.default_rng(1).normal(size=120)), test='adf', regression='c')
```

**Output**

```text

Dickey–Fuller test for unit root          Number of obs  = 119
Variable: y                               Number of lags =   0

H0: Random walk without drift, d = 0

                                       Dickey–Fuller
                   Test      -------- critical value ---------
              statistic           1%           5%          10%
--------------------------------------------------------------
 Z(t)            -1.496       -3.504       -2.889       -2.579
--------------------------------------------------------------
MacKinnon approximate p-value for Z(t) = 0.5356.
```

**Notes:** The printed critical values are NOT MacKinnon response-surface values: `dfuller.ado` interpolates a six-point table linearly in N, and these are that table's numbers, so the band matches what Stata prints. The p-value is still the MacKinnon one. `kpss` inverts the null -- its `H0` is stationarity, and Stata stores no MacKinnon p-value for it.

### `Manova`

**Stata:** `manova y1 y2 = treat`

One-way multivariate analysis of variance, the counterpart of Stata's `manova`. Note the formula spelling: the two sides are separated by `=` rather than `~`, so `from_formula("y1 y2 = g", data)` reads exactly like the Stata command. Each response is regressed on the same factor and the between/within sum-of-squares matrices are formed once; `fit()` returns a `ManovaResult` carrying Wilks' lambda and Rao's F. The additional multivariate statistics that Stata prints from `manovatest` are computed too and reachable through `multivariate_tests()`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.Manova(responses: 'np.ndarray', groups: 'Any', *, term: 'str' = 'group') -> 'None'
```

**`Manova.from_formula`**

```python
Manova.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, subset: 'Any' = None) -> "'Manova'"
```

**`Manova.fit`**

```python
Manova.fit(self) -> 'ManovaResult'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.Manova.from_formula('y1 y2 = treat', base).fit()
```

**Output**

```text
MANOVA treat
  Wilks' lambda = 0.9925
  F(2, 197) = 0.75   p = 0.4740
```

**Notes:** Only one factor is supported and covariates are not: a formula with several right-hand terms raises `UnsupportedError` instead of fitting something else. A non-integer numeric factor is refused with Stata's own diagnosis, because Stata's `manova` treats such a variable as a continuous covariate it does not implement here.

### `ManovaResult`

**Stata:** `manova y1 y2 = treat   (followed by manovatest)`

The result of a one-way MANOVA: `wilks_lambda`, the Rao F approximation `F` with its numerator and denominator degrees of freedom `df1`/`df2` and p-value `p`, the factor name `term`, and per-equation fit rows (`r2_by_equation`, `rmse_by_equation`, `f_by_equation`, `p_f_by_equation`) that mirror Stata's per-equation table. `df` is Stata's hypothesis degrees of freedom for the factor (`g - 1`), which is not the same quantity as `df1`. `multivariate_tests()` returns all four statistics -- Wilks' lambda, Pillai's trace, Lawley-Hotelling trace and Roy's largest root -- with the names `manovatest` uses.

**Signature**

```python
ws.ManovaResult(wilks_lambda: 'float', df: 'float', df1: 'float', df2: 'float', F: 'float', p: 'float', term: 'str' = 'group', nobs: 'int' = 0, groups: 'int' = 0, statistics: 'Mapping[str, float]' = <factory>, n_equations: 'int' = 0, r2_by_equation: "'Sequence[float]'" = <factory>, rmse_by_equation: "'Sequence[float]'" = <factory>, f_by_equation: "'Sequence[float]'" = <factory>, p_f_by_equation: "'Sequence[float]'" = <factory>, depvar_names: "'Sequence[str]'" = <factory>, design_matrix: 'Any' = None, coefficients: 'Any' = None, fitted_values: 'Any' = None, responses: 'Any' = None) -> None
```

**Example**

```python
ws.Manova.from_formula('y1 y2 = treat', base).fit().multivariate_tests()
```

**Output**

```text
{"Wilks' lambda": 0.9924503215154242, "Pillai's trace": 0.007549678484575742, 'Lawley-Hotelling trace': 0.007607109717137019, "Roy's largest root": 0.007549678484575742}
```

**Notes:** `df_hypothesis`/`df_error`/`f_statistic`/`pvalue` are retained as aliases for the older field names, so `df1` and `df_hypothesis` are not interchangeable. Asking for a statistic that a given fit did not record raises `KeyError` rather than returning `nan`, so an unavailable statistic cannot be mistaken for a computed zero.


## Treatment effects and survey data

### `Didregress`

**Stata:** `didregress (y x1) (treat), group(firm) time(year)`

Two-way fixed-effects difference-in-differences, the counterpart of Stata's `didregress`. Stata's two parenthesised blocks are passed as the first two arguments: the outcome block (dependent variable, then covariates) and the treatment block (exactly one 0/1 indicator), with `group=` naming the unit whose fixed effect is absorbed and `time=` naming the period. Both effects are absorbed rather than dummied out, and the reported ATET is the treatment coefficient. The default covariance clusters on `group()`, like Stata.

**Signature**

```python
ws.Didregress(outcome: 'Any', treatment: 'Any', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, group: 'str', time: 'str', covariates: 'Any' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`Didregress.from_formula`**

```python
Didregress.from_formula(outcome: 'Any', treatment: 'Any', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, group: 'str', time: 'str', covariates: 'Any' = None, subset: 'Any' = None) -> "'Didregress'"
```

**`Didregress.fit`**

```python
Didregress.fit(self, cov_type: 'str' = 'cluster') -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.Didregress.from_formula('y', 'treat', base, group='firm', time='year').fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text
Treatment and time information

Time variable: year
Control:       treat = 0
Treatment:     treat = 1
-----------------------------------
             |   Control  Treatment
-------------+---------------------
Group        |
        firm |         0         40
-------------+---------------------
Time         |
     Minimum |         .          1
     Maximum |         .          3
-----------------------------------

Difference-in-differences regression                       Number of obs = 200
Data type: Repeated cross-sectional

                                  (Std. err. adjusted for 40 clusters in firm)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
             |               Robust
           y | Coefficient  std. err.      t    P>|t|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
ATET         |
       treat |
   (1 vs 0)  |   .2838325   .2816428     1.01   0.320    -.2858438    .8535088
Note: ATET estimate adjusted for group effects and time effects.
```

**Notes:** A bare `"y"` means NO covariates, exactly as in Stata's `(y)` block; pass `"y x1"` to include one, or `covariates="all"` to explicitly infer every time-varying numeric column. A time-invariant treatment indicator is the unit fixed effect itself, so it is refused with `ConsistencyError` (Stata's r(198) collinearity diagnosis) instead of reporting whatever the solver leaves. `e(r2)`, `e(r2_a)`, `e(rmse)`, `e(F)` and `e(ll)` are westata additions, not Stata-verified output.

### `Xtdidregress`

**Stata:** `xtdidregress (y x1) (treat), group(firm) time(year)`

Panel difference-in-differences, the counterpart of Stata's `xtdidregress`. The estimator is the same two-way fixed-effects regression as `Didregress` and the ATET is identical on a balanced panel; only the degrees-of-freedom convention differs, because a panel unit effect is nested inside the `group()` cluster variable and therefore adds nothing to the `(N-1)/(N-K)` factor. The clustered standard error is consequently the smaller of the two, which is the difference between `didregress` and `xtdidregress` in Stata's own output.

**Signature**

```python
ws.Xtdidregress(outcome: 'Any', treatment: 'Any', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, group: 'str', time: 'str', covariates: 'Any' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`Xtdidregress.from_formula`**

```python
Xtdidregress.from_formula(outcome: 'Any', treatment: 'Any', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, group: 'str', time: 'str', covariates: 'Any' = None, subset: 'Any' = None) -> "'Didregress'"
```

**`Xtdidregress.fit`**

```python
Xtdidregress.fit(self, cov_type: 'str' = 'cluster') -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.Xtdidregress.from_formula('y', 'treat', base, group='firm', time='year').fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text
Treatment and time information

Time variable: year
Control:       treat = 0
Treatment:     treat = 1
-----------------------------------
             |   Control  Treatment
-------------+---------------------
Group        |
        firm |         0         40
-------------+---------------------
Time         |
     Minimum |         .          1
     Maximum |         .          3
-----------------------------------

Difference-in-differences regression                       Number of obs = 200
Data type: Longitudinal

                                  (Std. err. adjusted for 40 clusters in firm)
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
             |               Robust
           y | Coefficient  std. err.      t    P>|t|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
ATET         |
       treat |
   (1 vs 0)  |   .2838325   .2517466     1.13   0.266    -.2253731     .793038
Note: ATET estimate adjusted for panel effects and time effects.
```

**Notes:** Unlike `didregress`, this spelling requires a genuine panel: a repeated `(group, time)` cell raises `ConsistencyError` with Stata's `xtset` diagnosis ("repeated time values within panel"). Unbalanced panels are still estimated; the exact agreement with `Didregress` is guaranteed and tested for balanced ones.

### `TreatmentEffectResult`

**Stata:** `teffects ipw / aipw / nnmatch / ra   (the table they print)`

The shared result type for the four `teffects` estimators: the `estimand` ("ATE" or "ATET"), the effect with its standard error, normal-based `z` and two-sided `pvalue`, and the 95% Wald interval, plus the `potential_outcomes` mapping `{"0": (mean, se), "1": (mean, se)}` that corresponds to Stata's `POmean` rows. `se_method` records how the standard error was produced, and the header fields `nobs`, `estimator`, `outcome_model` and `treatment_model` reproduce the block Stata prints above the table. The `e` property mirrors the table onto `e()`-style scalars such as `atet`, `se_atet` and `ci_lower`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.TreatmentEffectResult(estimand: 'str', estimate: 'float', std_err: 'float', z: 'float', pvalue: 'float', ci_lower: 'float', ci_upper: 'float', potential_outcomes: 'dict[str, tuple[float, float]]' = <factory>, se_method: 'str' = 'influence function', nobs: 'int' = 0, estimator: 'str' = '', outcome_model: 'str' = '', treatment_model: 'str' = '', weights: 'Any' = None) -> None
```

**Example**

```python
r = ws.IPW.from_formula('ytreat', 'treat', base, covariates=['x1', 'x2'], estimand='atet').fit()
r.e
```

**Output**

```text
{'N': 200.0, 'atet': 1.5423356362095726, 'se_atet': 0.1505521705826877, 'z_atet': 10.244526068539653, 'p_atet': 1.2523713423322037e-24, 'ci_lower': 1.2472588040731742, 'ci_upper': 1.837412468345971, 'estimator': 'inverse probability weighting', 'outcome_model': 'none', 'treatment_model': 'logit', 'se_method': 'influence function', 'po0': 0.8694097819079406, 'se_po0': 0.13823329458547928, 'po1': 2.4117454181175133, 'se_po1': 0.11180252552264044}
```

**Notes:** `se_method` is `"Abadie-Imbens approximation"` for `NearestNeighbourMatching` and `"influence function"` for the other three, so a caller never has to guess whether a standard error is conformant. The potential-outcome means are stored separately rather than derived from the effect, because an estimator can hit the right difference at the wrong level. `with_header()` needs all four header keyword arguments and returns a copy, since the dataclass is frozen.

### `IPW`

**Stata:** `teffects ipw (ytreat) (treat x1 x2) (x1 x2)`

Inverse-probability weighting, the counterpart of Stata's `teffects ipw`. A logit treatment model gives each observation a propensity score, and the reported ATE is the difference between the two arms' **normalised** (Hajek) weighted means rather than the plain ratio of weighted sums. The ATET reweights the controls to the treated distribution with the odds `p/(1-p)`. `estimand="atet"` switches which of the two is reported.

**Signature**

```python
ws.IPW(y: 'Any', treated: 'Any', x: 'Any', *, estimand: 'str' = 'ate') -> 'None'
```

**`IPW.from_formula`**

```python
IPW.from_formula(outcome: 'str', treatment: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', **kwargs: 'Any') -> 'Any'
```

**`IPW.fit`**

```python
IPW.fit(self) -> 'TreatmentEffectResult'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.IPW.from_formula('ytreat', 'treat', base, covariates=['x1', 'x2']).fit()
```

**Output**

```text
Treatment-effects estimation
  Number of obs = 200
  Estimator      : inverse probability weighting
  Outcome model  : none
  Treatment model: logit
  ATE        1.559812   se 0.1452071   z   10.74   p 0.0000   [1.275212, 1.844413]
  POmean(0) 0.9280727   se 0.129868
  POmean(1)  2.487885   se 0.1024434
```

**Notes:** The normalisation is not cosmetic: on the reference data the unnormalised sums give 1.9355 where Stata gives 1.8882, because estimated propensities do not add up to the arm sizes exactly. The standard error is the exact influence-function variance, not one read off an OLS covariance.

### `AIPW`

**Stata:** `teffects aipw (ytreat x1 x2) (treat x1 x2) (x1 x2)`

Augmented inverse-probability weighting, the counterpart of Stata's `teffects aipw`. Each arm's outcome regression supplies a prediction and the propensity score corrects its residual, so the imputed potential outcome is `yhat1 + t(y - yhat1)/p` for the treated arm and its mirror image for the control arm. That makes the estimator doubly robust: either the outcome model or the treatment model may be misspecified without destroying consistency.

**Signature**

```python
ws.AIPW(y: 'Any', treated: 'Any', x: 'Any', *, estimand: 'str' = 'ate') -> 'None'
```

**`AIPW.from_formula`**

```python
AIPW.from_formula(outcome: 'str', treatment: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', **kwargs: 'Any') -> 'Any'
```

**`AIPW.fit`**

```python
AIPW.fit(self) -> 'TreatmentEffectResult'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.AIPW.from_formula('ytreat', 'treat', base, covariates=['x1', 'x2']).fit()
```

**Output**

```text
Treatment-effects estimation
  Number of obs = 200
  Estimator      : augmented inverse probability weighting
  Outcome model  : linear
  Treatment model: logit
  ATE        1.584398   se 0.1452802   z   10.91   p 0.0000   [1.299654, 1.869142]
  POmean(0) 0.8994778   se 0.1310723
  POmean(1)  2.483876   se 0.1025504
```

**Notes:** The standard error includes the influence of both the outcome regressions and the propensity model -- the augmented outcome means have a direct non-smooth part as well as the `A^-1 m_i` term, and dropping either piece changes the standard error while leaving the point estimate untouched.

### `NearestNeighbourMatching`

**Stata:** `teffects nnmatch (ytreat x1 x2) (treat)`

Nearest-neighbour matching on covariates, the counterpart of Stata's `teffects nnmatch`. Each observation's counterfactual is imputed from the `nneighbor` closest observations in the other arm, measured by the Mahalanobis distance under the sample covariance of the covariates -- which is what `teffects nnmatch` uses by default. Matching is with replacement, so one control may serve several treated units.

**Signature**

```python
ws.NearestNeighbourMatching(y: 'Any', treated: 'Any', x: 'Any', *, nneighbor: 'int' = 1, estimand: 'str' = 'ate') -> 'None'
```

**`NearestNeighbourMatching.from_formula`**

```python
NearestNeighbourMatching.from_formula(outcome: 'str', treatment: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', **kwargs: 'Any') -> 'Any'
```

**`NearestNeighbourMatching.fit`**

```python
NearestNeighbourMatching.fit(self) -> 'TreatmentEffectResult'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.NearestNeighbourMatching.from_formula('ytreat', 'treat', base, covariates=['x1', 'x2'], nneighbor=3).fit()
```

**Output**

```text
Treatment-effects estimation
  Number of obs = 200
  Estimator      : nearest-neighbor matching
  Outcome model  : none
  Treatment model: logit
  ATE         1.54307   se 0.1651077   z    9.35   p 0.0000   [1.219465, 1.866675]
  POmean(0) 0.9311758   se 0.09906461
  POmean(1)  2.474246   se 0.1320861
  note: standard errors are Abadie-Imbens approximation
```

**Notes:** This is the one estimator in the family whose standard error is an approximation: `se_method` reads `"Abadie-Imbens approximation"`, documenting a gap of roughly 2% against Stata rather than presenting the number as conformant. The distance metric is load-bearing -- Euclidean distance moves the estimate by about 0.25% on the reference data.

### `RegressionAdjustment`

**Stata:** `teffects ra (ytreat x1 x2) (treat)`

Regression adjustment, the counterpart of Stata's `teffects ra`. The outcome model is fitted **separately in each treatment arm**, then both potential outcomes are predicted for every observation and averaged. Allowing the slopes to differ by arm is what separates this from a single regression with a treatment dummy, and it is the difference between a correct estimate and a badly biased one when the covariate effects are heterogeneous.

**Signature**

```python
ws.RegressionAdjustment(y: 'Any', treated: 'Any', x: 'Any', *, estimand: 'str' = 'ate') -> 'None'
```

**`RegressionAdjustment.from_formula`**

```python
RegressionAdjustment.from_formula(outcome: 'str', treatment: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', **kwargs: 'Any') -> 'Any'
```

**`RegressionAdjustment.fit`**

```python
RegressionAdjustment.fit(self) -> 'TreatmentEffectResult'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.RegressionAdjustment.from_formula('ytreat', 'treat', base, covariates=['x1', 'x2']).fit()
```

**Output**

```text
Treatment-effects estimation
  Number of obs = 200
  Estimator      : regression adjustment
  Outcome model  : linear
  Treatment model: none
  ATE        1.583224   se 0.1452821   z   10.90   p 0.0000   [1.298476, 1.867971]
  POmean(0) 0.9002321   se 0.1309727
  POmean(1)  2.483456   se 0.1028893
```

**Notes:** The standard error accounts for the plug-in prediction, not just for the fitted coefficients: the direct non-smooth part of the potential-outcome means belongs to the influence function, so an OLS covariance would understate it.

### `SurveyDesign`
