**Stata:** `svyset firm [pweight=w], strata(strata) fpc(fpc)`

A declared complex survey design -- the counterpart of `svyset`. It records column **names**, not data, so one design can be applied to any frame carrying those columns; `from_frame` validates that they exist and `layout(frame)` binds the design to a frame and reports the bookkeeping (`n_psu`, `n_strata`, `n_pop`, the design degrees of freedom `df_r = #PSU - #strata` and the per-observation weights). `psu` labels are only meaningful within a stratum, which is Stata's convention too, and `fpc` above one is read as the population number of PSUs while a value in `(0, 1]` is read as the sampling rate.

**Signature**

```python
ws.SurveyDesign(psu: 'str', strata: 'str | None' = None, weights: 'str | None' = None, fpc: 'str | None' = None, singleunit: 'str' = 'certainty') -> None
```

**Example**

```python
ws.SurveyDesign.from_frame(base, psu='firm').layout(base).df_r
```

**Output**

```text
39.0
```

**Notes:** `singleunit` governs strata that contain a single PSU: `"certainty"` (the lone PSU contributes zero, and the setting the fixtures were captured under), `"missing"`, `"scaling"` or `"centered"`. Only `certainty` is exercised by captured Stata output, so `scaling` and `centered` are documented approximations rather than verified arithmetic. An unknown strategy raises `ValueError` at declaration time, before any estimation.

### `SvyMean`

**Stata:** `svy: mean y`

Survey-weighted mean, the counterpart of `svy: mean`. The point estimate is `sum(w y) / sum(w)`; the work is in the variance, which is linearised from the influence function `psi_i = w_i (y_i - ybar) / sum(w)` and aggregated over PSUs within strata. Both the weight and the centring belong to the linearisation, and the reported reference distribution is a t with `df_r = #PSU - #strata` degrees of freedom.

**Signature**

```python
ws.SvyMean(variables: 'Any', frame: 'pd.DataFrame', design: 'SurveyDesign', *, params: 'np.ndarray', layout: 'SurveyLayout', sample: 'pd.DataFrame') -> 'None'
```

**`SvyMean.fit`**

```python
SvyMean.fit(self) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
b = base.assign(w=np.random.default_rng(1).uniform(0.5, 2.0, len(base)))
ws.SvyMean.from_frame('y', b, ws.SurveyDesign(psu='firm', weights='w')).fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text
Survey: Mean estimation

Number of strata =   1            Number of obs   =        200
Number of PSUs   =  40            Population size = 252.433526
                                  Design df       =         39

--------------------------------------------------------------
             |             Linearized
             |       Mean   std. err.     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+------------------------------------------------
           y |    1.03929   .1025935      .8317748    1.246805
--------------------------------------------------------------
```

**Notes:** Dropping either piece of the influence function leaves the point estimate untouched and the variance badly wrong: on the fixture design `y_i / sum(w)` is 65% low and `w_i y_i / sum(w)` (weight but no centring) is 30% low, while `w_i (y_i - ybar) / sum(w)` reproduces Stata. The design degrees of freedom `df_r` is not clamped to at least one, because doing so would silently overstate precision.

### `SvyTotal`

**Stata:** `svy: total y`

Survey-weighted total, the counterpart of `svy: total`. The estimate is `sum(w y)` and the influence function is `psi_i = w_i y_i`; the weights are deliberately **not** normalised, because a total estimates a population quantity whose scale is part of the answer. Everything else -- linearisation, stratification, the `df_r = #PSU - #strata` reference distribution and the fpc factor -- is shared with `SvyMean`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.SvyTotal(variables: 'Any', frame: 'pd.DataFrame', design: 'SurveyDesign', *, params: 'np.ndarray', layout: 'SurveyLayout', sample: 'pd.DataFrame') -> 'None'
```

**`SvyTotal.fit`**

```python
SvyTotal.fit(self) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
b = base.assign(w=np.random.default_rng(1).uniform(0.5, 2.0, len(base)))
ws.SvyTotal.from_frame('y', b, ws.SurveyDesign(psu='firm', weights='w')).fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text
Survey: Total estimation

Number of strata =   1            Number of obs   =        200
Number of PSUs   =  40            Population size = 252.433526
                                  Design df       =         39

--------------------------------------------------------------
             |             Linearized
             |      Total   std. err.     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+------------------------------------------------
           y |   262.3516   26.31999      209.1144    315.5888
--------------------------------------------------------------
```

**Notes:** Unlike `SvyMean` the influence function is NOT centred on the estimate. Centring it -- `w_i (y_i - T)` -- looks harmless and is not: on the fixture design it inflates the variance by a factor of about 244,000, because `T` is a global constant whose interaction with the unequal weights does not cancel inside a stratum.

### `SvyProportion`

**Stata:** `svy: proportion yb`

Weighted proportions of one or more categorical variables, the counterpart of `svy: proportion`. One coefficient is reported per observed level, named in Stata's `variable=level` shape and ordered by ascending level, together with the joint covariance. The levels of a binary variable get identical standard errors and perfectly negatively correlated estimates, because each proportion is the mean of its indicator.

**Signature**

```python
ws.SvyProportion(variables: 'Any', frame: 'pd.DataFrame', design: 'SurveyDesign', *, params: 'np.ndarray', indicators: 'np.ndarray', layout: 'SurveyLayout', sample: 'pd.DataFrame', names: 'list[str]') -> 'None'
```

**`SvyProportion.fit`**

```python
SvyProportion.fit(self) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
b = base.assign(w=np.random.default_rng(1).uniform(0.5, 2.0, len(base)))
ws.SvyProportion.from_frame('yb', b, ws.SurveyDesign(psu='firm', weights='w')).fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text
Survey: Proportion estimation

Number of strata =   1            Number of obs   =        200
Number of PSUs   =  40            Population size = 252.433526
                                  Design df       =         39

--------------------------------------------------------------
             |             Linearized            Logit
             | Proportion   std. err.     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+------------------------------------------------
          yb |
          0  |   .4625698   .0374625      .3882178    .5386245
          1  |   .5374302   .0374625      .4613755    .6117822
--------------------------------------------------------------
```

**Notes:** The coefficient names are strings such as `yb=0` and `yb=1`, so positional indexing by `param_names` is the reliable way to read them; the levels are the ones observed after listwise deletion, not the declared value labels.

### `SvyRegress`

**Stata:** `svy: regress y x1 x2`

Survey-weighted linear regression, the counterpart of `svy: regress`. The coefficients are weighted least squares -- the same numbers `ws.OLS(..., weights=..., weight_type="pweight")` produces, and only their order and naming differ (slopes first, constant last as `_cons`) -- so the survey machinery contributes nothing to the point estimates and everything to the variance. The influence function is `psi_i = (X'WX)^-1 w_i x_i e_i`, with both the weight and the inverse cross-product inside the linearisation, and the model F statistic is the survey-adjusted Wald form reported against the design degrees of freedom.

**Signature**

```python
ws.SvyRegress(frame: 'pd.DataFrame', formula: 'str', design: 'SurveyDesign', *, X: 'np.ndarray', y: 'np.ndarray', names: 'list[str]', has_constant: 'bool', weights: 'np.ndarray', layout: 'SurveyLayout', sample: 'pd.DataFrame', params: 'np.ndarray', resid: 'np.ndarray', xtwx_inv_xt: 'np.ndarray') -> 'None'
```

**`SvyRegress.fit`**

```python
SvyRegress.fit(self) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
b = base.assign(w=np.random.default_rng(1).uniform(0.5, 2.0, len(base)))
ws.SvyRegress.from_frame(b, 'y ~ x1 + x2', ws.SurveyDesign(psu='firm', weights='w')).fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text
Survey: Linear regression

Number of strata =   1                            Number of obs   =        200
Number of PSUs   =  40                            Population size = 252.433526
                                                  Design df       =         39
                                                  F(2, 38)        =     101.48
                                                  Prob > F        =     0.0000
                                                  R-squared       =     0.4663

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
             |             Linearized
           y | Coefficient  std. err.      t    P>|t|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
          x1 |   .8086296    .059397    13.61   0.000     .6884878    .9287714
          x2 |   -.513372   .0751049    -6.84   0.000     -.665286    -.361458
       _cons |   .9758079   .0831066    11.74   0.000     .8077089    1.143907
```

**Notes:** Dropping either `w_i` or `(X'WX)^-1` from the influence function leaves the coefficients exact and makes the variance grossly wrong. The design-based `F` is the survey-adjusted form `(d - q + 1)/(d q) * W` on `F(q, d - q + 1)`, not the ordinary regression F, so it will not equal an unweighted `regress` F even when the coefficients coincide.

### `SvyLogit`

**Stata:** `svy: logit yb x1 x2`

Survey-weighted logistic regression, the counterpart of `svy: logit`. It is pseudo-maximum-likelihood: the log-likelihood carries the sampling weights, and the variance is linearised from `psi_i = I^-1 w_i x_i (y_i - p_i)` with `I = X' diag(w p(1-p)) X`. The dependent variable must be coded 0/1 and a constant outcome is refused rather than fitted.

**Signature**

```python
ws.SvyLogit(frame: 'pd.DataFrame', formula: 'str', design: 'SurveyDesign', *, X: 'np.ndarray', y: 'np.ndarray', names: 'list[str]', has_constant: 'bool', weights: 'np.ndarray', layout: 'SurveyLayout', sample: 'pd.DataFrame', params: 'np.ndarray', probabilities: 'np.ndarray', information_inverse_xt: 'np.ndarray', loglik: 'float', iterations: 'int') -> 'None'
```

**`SvyLogit.fit`**

```python
SvyLogit.fit(self) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
b = base.assign(w=np.random.default_rng(1).uniform(0.5, 2.0, len(base)))
ws.SvyLogit.from_frame(b, 'yb ~ x1 + x2', ws.SurveyDesign(psu='firm', weights='w')).fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text
Survey: Logistic regression

Number of strata =   1                            Number of obs   =        200
Number of PSUs   =  40                            Population size = 252.433526
                                                  Design df       =         39
                                                  F(2, 38)        =       5.38
                                                  Prob > F        =     0.0087

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
             |             Linearized
          yb | Coefficient  std. err.      t    P>|t|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
          x1 |   .4975352   .1496844     3.32   0.002     .1947699    .8003005
          x2 |  -.0758175   .1557108    -0.49   0.629    -.3907723    .2391373
       _cons |   .1178733    .161271     0.73   0.469     -.208328    .4440747
```

**Notes:** On the fixture design the estimates reproduce Stata to about 5e-10 relative, and that residual is Stata's stopping tolerance rather than this code's: evaluating Stata's own score equations at Stata's published coefficients leaves a gradient of about 1.7e-8, while the estimates returned here are the exact maximiser. A conformance tolerance tighter than 1e-9 would assert something untrue about Stata.


## Resampling

### `Bootstrap`

**Stata:** `bootstrap, reps(100): regress y x1 x2`

Nonparametric bootstrap, the counterpart of Stata's `bootstrap`. Each replicate draws a resample and the estimator is refitted on it; `fit()` returns a `StataResults` whose standard errors are the replicate standard deviations, with `e(reps)`, `e(seed)` and `e(vce) = "bootstrap"` recorded. Only the `regress` and `mean` estimators are supported -- Stata's arbitrary `r()` statistics would need a small expression interpreter, and an unsupported name raises `UnsupportedError` instead of quietly doing something else.

**Signature**

```python
ws.Bootstrap(frame: 'pd.DataFrame', response: 'np.ndarray', design: 'np.ndarray', param_names: 'Sequence[str]', *, estimator: 'str') -> 'None'
```

**`Bootstrap.from_formula`**

```python
Bootstrap.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, estimator: 'str' = 'regress', reps: 'int' = 200, seed: 'int | None' = None, subset: 'Any' = None, absorb: 'Any' = None) -> "'Bootstrap'"
```

**`Bootstrap.fit`**

```python
Bootstrap.fit(self) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.Bootstrap.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base, reps=100, seed=1).fit().bse
```

**Output**

```text
Intercept    0.066543
x1           0.076657
x2           0.068880
Name: bse, dtype: float64
```

**Notes:** Stata's random-number generator is not numpy's, so replicate-for-replicate agreement is impossible by construction; only the point estimate and the magnitude of the standard errors are comparable, and `e(note)` says so in the result itself.

### `Jackknife`

**Stata:** `jackknife: regress y x1 x2`

Delete-one jackknife, the counterpart of Stata's `jackknife`. Each of the N observations is dropped in turn and the estimator refitted N times; the default pseudovalue form combines the replicates, and `fit(mse=True)` selects Stata's simpler `(N-1)/N` form instead. `e(note)` records which form was used. Stata's jackknife reports `e(df_r) = N - 1` regardless of the coefficient count, and so does this.

**Signature**

```python
ws.Jackknife(frame: 'pd.DataFrame', response: 'np.ndarray', design: 'np.ndarray', param_names: 'Sequence[str]', *, estimator: 'str') -> 'None'
```

**`Jackknife.from_formula`**

```python
Jackknife.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, estimator: 'str' = 'regress', subset: 'Any' = None, absorb: 'Any' = None) -> "'Jackknife'"
```

**`Jackknife.fit`**

```python
Jackknife.fit(self, *, mse: 'bool' = False) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.Jackknife.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit().bse
```

**Output**

```text
Intercept    0.074965
x1           0.071961
x2           0.072703
Name: bse, dtype: float64
```

**Notes:** The jackknife involves no random numbers, so it is held to a much tighter standard than the bootstrap -- but the regression variance still sits about 7e-6 relative away from Stata's. That gap is diagnosed rather than tuned: the scaling factor is right and the difference lies in the leave-one-out estimates past their sixth digit, consistent with Stata using a Sherman-Morrison update instead of refitting. `e(expected_gap)` states this so nobody chases it again.

### `Permute`

**Stata:** `permute x1, reps(200): regress y x1 x2`

Permutation test, the counterpart of Stata's `permute`. The named regressor's values are shuffled `reps` times, the model is re-estimated each time, and the p-value is the share of permuted statistics at least as extreme in absolute value as the observed one -- computed as `(count + 1) / (reps + 1)`, which is why it can never be exactly zero. `e(permuted)` names the shuffled variable and `e(pvalue_permuted)` carries the p-value.

**Signature**

```python
ws.Permute(*args: 'Any', permuted_column: 'int' = 0, **kwargs: 'Any') -> 'None'
```

**`Permute.from_formula`**

```python
Permute.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, permute: 'str', reps: 'int' = 200, seed: 'int | None' = None, subset: 'Any' = None) -> "'Permute'"
```

**`Permute.fit`**

```python
Permute.fit(self) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.Permute.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base, permute='x1', reps=200, seed=1).fit().e['pvalue_permuted']
```

**Output**

```text
0.004975124378109453
```

**Notes:** The reported covariance is the analytic OLS one, not a permutation-based estimate: `permute` produces a p-value, not a VCE. `permute=` must name an actual regressor in the formula or a `ConsistencyError` lists the regressors that do exist. The random stream is not Stata's, so only the magnitude of the p-value is comparable.


## Postestimation functions

### `testnl`

**Stata:** `testnl exp(_b[x1]) = 2`

Wald test of one or more nonlinear constraints (Stata `testnl`). The expression is parsed against a fixed whitelist -- `_b[name]`, the seven functions `exp`, `log`, `sqrt`, `sin`, `cos`, `logit`, `invlogit`, and arithmetic -- and is never handed to `eval`, so text such as `__import__('os')` fails validation instead of running. A bare expression is tested against zero; several constraints separated by commas or by whitespace (including Stata's grouped `(a = 0) (b = 0)`) are tested jointly. The statistic is chi2(q) with q the number of constraints, as `[R] testnl` reports it, and the command is reachable as `result.testnl(expr)` or `ws.testnl(result, expr)`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.testnl(result: 'Any', expression: 'str') -> 'WaldTest'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.testnl(ws.Logit.from_formula('yb ~ x1 + x2', base).fit(), 'exp(_b[x1]) = 2')
```

**Output**

```text

 ( 1)  exp(_b[x1]) = 2

       chi2(  1) =    1.36
            Prob > chi2 =    0.2437
```

**Notes:** The derivatives entering the variance are central differences with the step `1e-6 * max(1, |b_j|)`. A linear constraint therefore reproduces `lincom` exactly; only genuinely nonlinear functions are approximated.

### `lrtest`

**Stata:** `lrtest unrestricted restricted`

Likelihood-ratio test of a nested pair (Stata `lrtest`): `LR = 2(ll_unrestricted - ll_restricted)`, referred to chi2 with the difference in parameter counts. Both fits must be on the same sample, and equal parameter counts raise `ConsistencyError` -- Stata's `r(498)` -- because chi2(0) is a point mass and reporting `p = 1` would turn a Stata error into a successful command. A pair whose counts differ but which is not nested still gets a statistic; it is usually negative, and that is reported with a warning rather than clamped. Callable as `ws.lrtest(unrestricted, restricted)` or `unrestricted.lrtest(restricted)`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.lrtest(unrestricted: 'Any', restricted: 'Any') -> 'LRTestResult'
```

**Example**

```python
full = ws.Logit.from_formula('yb ~ x1 + x2 + x3', base).fit()
full.lrtest(ws.Logit.from_formula('yb ~ x1 + x2', base).fit())
```

**Output**

```text
Likelihood-ratio test
Assumption: logit nested within logit
LR chi2(1) = 6.72
Prob > chi2 = 0.0095
```

**Notes:** Nesting is decided from the parameter COUNTS alone, exactly as Stata decides it. A coefficient-name mismatch is reported as a warning rather than a refusal, because Stata computes the statistic anyway.

### `hausman`

**Stata:** `hausman fe re`

Hausman specification test of an efficient estimator's consistency (Stata `hausman`). The consistent-but-inefficient fit (e.g. `xtreg, fe`) comes first and the efficient one (e.g. `xtreg, re`) second, and the statistic is `(b_c - b_e)'[V_c - V_e]^-1(b_c - b_e)` over the shared coefficients. The constant is excluded unless `constant=True`, because a within-transformed intercept and a GLS intercept estimate different population quantities; `constant=True` is Stata's `hausman ..., constant`. Callable as `ws.hausman(consistent, efficient)` or `consistent.hausman(efficient, constant=True)`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.hausman(consistent: 'Any', efficient: 'Any', *, constant: 'bool' = False, sigmamore: 'bool' = False, sigmaless: 'bool' = False) -> 'HausmanResult'
```

**Example**

```python
fe = ws.PanelOLS.from_formula('ypanel ~ x1 + x2', base, entity='firm', time='year').fit()
re = ws.RandomEffects.from_formula('ypanel ~ x1 + x2', base, entity='firm', time='year').fit()
ws.hausman(fe, re)
```

**Output**

```text

                 ---- Coefficients ----
             |      (b)          (B)            (b-B)     sqrt(diag(V_b-V_B))
             |       fe           re         Difference       Std. err.
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
          x1 |    .7088762     .7277599       -.0188837         .016279
          x2 |   -.1092899     -.119745        .0104551        .0225716
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
                          b = Consistent under H0 and Ha; obtained from xtreg.
           B = Inconsistent under Ha, efficient under H0; obtained from xtreg.

Test of H0: Difference in coefficients not systematic

    chi2(2) = (b-B)'[(V_b-V_B)^(-1)](b-B)
            =   1.60
Prob > chi2 = 0.4501
```

**Notes:** When `V_c - V_e` is not positive definite the matrix is inverted with a pseudo-inverse and the statistic is floored at 0, whereas Stata prints the negative value it computes. `df`, `p` and the warning still agree; only the sign of an uninterpretable statistic differs, because a pseudo-inverse routinely yields about `-1e-18` through round-off.

### `contrast`

**Stata:** `contrast r.treat`

ANOVA-style contrasts of a factor variable's estimated means (Stata `contrast`). `term` is the factor's design-matrix prefix (`'C(g)'` or `'g'`), matched on the data variable, so `'C(g)'` also finds the columns of a model fitted with `C(g, base='a')`. `method='r'` compares each level with the reference, `'g'` with the unweighted grand mean (L rows) and `'a'` compares adjacent levels; anything else raises `UnsupportedError` instead of silently falling back to a default. It returns one `LincomResult` per comparison, in level order, and is reachable as `ws.contrast(result, term, method)` or `result.contrast(term, method)`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.contrast(result: 'Any', term: 'str', method: 'str' = 'r', *, level: 'float' = 0.95, nowald: 'bool' = False) -> 'list[LincomResult]'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.contrast(ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + C(treat)', base).fit(), 'C(treat)')[0]
```

**Output**

```text

 ( 1)  C(treat)[T.1] - base(treat[0])

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
             | Coefficient  Std. err.      t    P>|t|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
         (1) |   .3762187   .1705568     2.21   0.029     .0398673    .7125702
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
```

### `pwcompare`

**Stata:** `pwcompare g`

All pairwise differences between the levels of a factor variable (Stata `pwcompare`). Row (i, j) with j > i reports `level_j - level_i`, so a factor with L levels yields L*(L-1)/2 rows in that order; the example indexes the first of them because the return value is a list. `adjust` accepts only `None` -- Stata's `adjust(none)` -- because every real multiple-comparison correction is unimplemented, and anything else raises `UnsupportedError` rather than dropping the correction silently. Reachable as `ws.pwcompare(result, term)` or `result.pwcompare(term)`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.pwcompare(result: 'Any', term: 'str', *, level: 'float' = 0.95, adjust: 'str | None' = None) -> 'Any'
```

**Example**

```python
d = base.assign(g=pd.cut(base['x3'], 3, labels=['lo', 'mid', 'hi']))
ws.pwcompare(ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + C(g)', d).fit(), 'C(g)')[0]
```

**Output**

```text

 ( 1)  C(g)[T.mid] - base(g[lo])

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
             | Coefficient  Std. err.      t    P>|t|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
         (1) |   .0508092   .2187468     0.23   0.817    -.3805904    .4822089
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
```

### `nlcom`

**Stata:** `nlcom exp(_b[x1])`

Point estimate, standard error and confidence interval for a nonlinear function of the coefficients (Stata `nlcom`), by the delta method: `Var(g(b)) = G V G'` with G the Jacobian at the estimate. The expression language is `_b[name]`, arithmetic, and the seven whitelisted functions; it returns the same `LincomResult` container `lincom` returns, so downstream code needs no special case, and `eform=True` reports `exp(g(b))` with an exponentiated interval. Reachable as `ws.nlcom(result, expr)` or `result.nlcom(expr)`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.nlcom(result: 'Any', expression: 'str', *, level: 'float' = 0.95, eform: 'bool' = False) -> 'LincomResult'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.nlcom(ws.Logit.from_formula('yb ~ x1 + x2', base).fit(), 'exp(_b[x1])')
```

**Output**

```text

 ( 1)  exp(_b[x1])

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
             | Coefficient  Std. err.      z    P>|z|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
         (1) |   1.697737   .2592614     6.55   0.000     1.189595     2.20588
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
```

**Notes:** Known deviation from Stata: the inference follows the model's own distribution -- t with `e(df_r)` after `regress`, normal after a z model -- while Stata prints `nlcom` with z and a normal interval even after `regress`. The point estimate and standard error agree; Stata's exact interval can be rebuilt from `std_err`.

### `margins`

**Stata:** `margins, dydx(x1) at(x2=0)`

Marginal effects and predictive margins. Supports the derivative forms Stata spells `dydx`, `eyex`, `dyex` and `eydx`, plus `at()` for fixing covariates and `over()` for group-wise margins. After a discrete-choice fit the derivative is taken on the response scale, which is what Stata's `margins` does -- the linear-index derivative would be a different quantity.

**Signature**

```python
ws.margins(result: 'Any', *, dydx: 'Sequence[str] | str | None' = None, eyex: 'Sequence[str] | str | None' = None, dyex: 'Sequence[str] | str | None' = None, eydx: 'Sequence[str] | str | None' = None, at: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, atmeans: 'bool' = False, over: 'str | None' = None, exog: 'np.ndarray | None' = None, param_names: 'Sequence[str] | None' = None, level: 'float' = 0.95, predict_kind: 'str | None' = None, factor_vars: 'Sequence[str] | None' = None, outcome: 'int | None' = None) -> 'list[MarginsResult] | MarginsResult'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.margins(ws.Logit.from_formula('yb ~ x1 + x2', base).fit(), dydx='x1', at={'x2': 0.0})
```

**Output**

```text

Average marginal effects                                   Number of obs = 200
Model VCE: OIM

Expression: Pr(yb), predict()
dy/dx wrt:  x1

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
             |            Delta-method
             |      dy/dx   std. err.      z    P>|z|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
          x1 |   .1229425   .0311677     3.94   0.000      .061855      .18403
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
```

**Notes:** Which prediction types `margins` may use is declared by the command itself in `e(marginsok)`/`e(marginsnotok)`; a type the command forbids is refused rather than silently computed.

### `estat_classification`

**Stata:** `estat classification`

The 2x2 classification table Stata prints after `logit`/`probit` (Stata `estat classification`), thresholding the predicted probability at `cutoff` (default 0.5, Stata's `cutoff()`). The positive class is the outcome being non-zero, and the percentages are on Stata's 0-100 scale. `to_dict()` returns the `r()` names `P_corr`, `P_p1`, `P_n0`, `P_p0`, `P_n1`, `P_1p`, `P_0n`, `P_0p`, `P_1n`. Reachable as `ws.estat_classification(result)` or `result.estat_classification()`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.estat_classification(result: 'Any', *, cutoff: 'float' = 0.5, y: 'Any' = None, probabilities: 'Any' = None) -> 'ClassificationResult'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.estat_classification(ws.Logit.from_formula('yb ~ x1 + x2', base).fit())
```

**Output**

```text

Logistic model for yb

              -------- True --------
Classified |         D            ~D  |      Total
-----------+--------------------------+-----------
     +     |        76            44  |        120
     -     |        32            48  |         80
-----------+--------------------------+-----------
   Total   |       108            92  |        200

Classified + if predicted Pr(D) >= .5
True D defined as yb != 0
--------------------------------------------------
Sensitivity                     Pr( +| D)   70.37%
Specificity                     Pr( -|~D)   52.17%
Positive predictive value       Pr( D| +)   63.33%
Negative predictive value       Pr(~D| -)   60.00%
--------------------------------------------------
False + rate for true ~D        Pr( +|~D)   47.83%
False - rate for true D         Pr( -| D)   29.63%
False + rate for classified +   Pr(~D| +)   36.67%
False - rate for classified -   Pr( D| -)   40.00%
--------------------------------------------------
Correctly classified                        62.00%
--------------------------------------------------
```

**Notes:** A `cutoff` outside [0, 1] or a non-binary response raises `ValueError`; a model Stata does not route the subcommand from raises `NotImplementedError` rather than producing a table for a command that has none.

### `estat_endogenous`

**Stata:** `estat endogenous`

Endogeneity test after an IV fit (Stata `estat endogenous`). Which test comes back depends on the VCE: an unadjusted VCE gives the Durbin and Wu-Hausman statistics, a robust VCE the robust score, a HAC VCE the HAC score, and a clustered VCE the regression-based F (`regF`), which is the only test Stata reports there. `lags` is the HAC truncation, defaulting to 1 under a HAC VCE and rejected everywhere else. LIML and GMM fits are refused with their reasons -- Stata dispatches only 2SLS and GMM, and the GMM branch reports a `C` statistic from a constrained refit that is not implemented here. Reachable as `ws.estat_endogenous(result)` or `result.estat_endogenous()`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.estat_endogenous(result: 'Any', *, lags: 'int | None' = None) -> 'Any'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.estat_endogenous(ws.IV2SLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + [x2 ~ x3]', base).fit())
```

**Output**

```text

  Tests of endogeneity
  H0: Variables are exogenous

  Durbin (score) chi2(1)          =  1.64391  (p = 0.1998)
  Wu-Hausman F(1,196)             =  1.62438  (p = 0.2040)
```

**Notes:** Non-IV results, GMM fits, and -- for a different reason -- LIML fits all raise `NotImplementedError`; `lags` on a non-HAC VCE raises `ValueError` (`lags() only valid with models with HAC VCEs`).

### `estat_firststage`

**Stata:** `estat firststage`

IV first-stage diagnostics (Stata `estat firststage`). For each endogenous regressor it reports the R-squared and adjusted R-squared of its regression on all exogenous variables, the partial R-squared of the excluded instruments, and the joint F of those instruments. With more than one endogenous regressor the report switches to Shea's partial R-squared unless `all=True`, and `forcenonrobust=True` reports the minimum-eigenvalue statistic and its critical values even under a robust VCE. Reachable as `ws.estat_firststage(result)` or `result.estat_firststage()`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.estat_firststage(result: 'Any', *, all: 'bool' = False, forcenonrobust: 'bool' = False) -> 'FirstStageResult'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.estat_firststage(ws.IV2SLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + [x2 ~ x3]', base).fit())
```

**Output**

```text

  First-stage regression summary statistics
  --------------------------------------------------------------------------
               |            Adjusted      Partial
      Variable |   R-sq.       R-sq.        R-sq.      F(1,197)   Prob > F
  -------------+------------------------------------------------------------
            x2 |  0.0008     -0.0093       0.0008      0.159849    0.6897
  --------------------------------------------------------------------------


  Minimum eigenvalue statistic = 0.159849    

  Critical Values                      # of endogenous regressors:    1
  H0: Instruments are weak             # of excluded instruments:     1
  ---------------------------------------------------------------------
                                     |    5%     10%     20%     30%
  2SLS relative bias                 |         (not available)
  -----------------------------------+---------------------------------
                                     |   10%     15%     20%     25%
  2SLS size of nominal 5% Wald test  |  16.38    8.96    6.66    5.53
  LIML size of nominal 5% Wald test  |  16.38    8.96    6.66    5.53
  ---------------------------------------------------------------------
```

**Notes:** Without `forcenonrobust`, the eigenvalue statistic is suppressed under a robust VCE because its critical values assume independent, identically distributed normal errors.

### `estat_gof`

**Stata:** `estat gof`

Poisson goodness-of-fit tests (Stata `estat gof`): the deviance and Pearson chi-squared statistics, each on `N - df_m - 1` degrees of freedom, printed as Stata's two-block table. It exists only for `poisson` -- `nbreg` sets no `e(estat_cmd)` in Stata, so there is no `estat gof` after a negative binomial fit, and this raises `NotImplementedError` rather than inventing one. `to_dict()` returns `chi2_d`, `chi2_p`, `df`, `p_d`, `p_p`. Reachable as `ws.estat_gof(result)` or `result.estat_gof()`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.estat_gof(result: 'Any', *, y: 'Any' = None, mu: 'Any' = None, group: 'int | None' = None) -> "'GofResult | LfitResult'"
```

**Example**

```python
ws.estat_gof(ws.Poisson.from_formula('ycnt ~ x1 + x2', base).fit())
```

**Output**

```text

Deviance goodness-of-fit           =   227.199
Prob > chi2(197)                   =    0.0690

Pearson goodness-of-fit            =   187.533
Prob > chi2(197)                   =    0.6740
```

**Notes:** `y` and `mu` default to the retained response and fitted means; pass them explicitly when the result did not keep a usable residual.

### `estat_hettest`

**Stata:** `estat hettest`

Breusch-Pagan / Cook-Weisberg test for heteroskedasticity (Stata `estat hettest`). The auxiliary regression is of the standardised squared residual on the fitted values when `variables` is omitted -- that is what `hettest.ado:110-115` does with no varlist, and it is NOT the same test as `rhs=True`, which regresses on all of `e(b)`'s regressors (chi2(1) = 7.89 versus chi2(2) = 24.17 on `regress mpg weight foreign`). `variables` names source data variables (`'rep78'` is the raw 1-5 column, one degree of freedom) or, failing that, design columns (`'foreign[T.Foreign]'`). The DEFAULT statistic is `e(mss)/2` of that regression, not `N*R2`; `method='iid'` gives `N*R2` and `method='fstat'` the auxiliary F. Estimation weights are reused by the auxiliary regression, so `fweight`/`iweight` `e(N)` is `sum(w)`. A robust or clustered VCE, pweighted data and a `noconstant` fit are refused, as `hettest.ado` refuses them. All statistics verified against captured StataNow 19.5 output. Reachable as `ws.estat_hettest(result)` or `result.estat_hettest()`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.estat_hettest(result: 'Any', *, method: 'str' = 'normal', variables: 'Sequence[str] | None' = None, rhs: 'bool' = False, mtest: 'str | None' = None) -> 'Any'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.estat_hettest(ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit())
```

**Output**

```text

Breusch–Pagan/Cook–Weisberg test for heteroskedasticity 
Assumption: Normal error terms
Variable: Fitted values of y

H0: Constant variance

    chi2(1) =   0.27
Prob > chi2 = 0.6006
```

**Notes:** `normal` combined with `iid`/`fstat` is rejected, matching Stata's `opts_exclusive`. `mtest()` requires a multi-regressor auxiliary regression: `hettest.ado:126-128` silently drops it when only one regressor is used, so the varlist-less call returns just the simultaneous test.

### `estat_ic`

**Stata:** `estat ic`

Information criteria for one fitted model. Prints AIC and BIC computed from the log-likelihood and the parameter count, following `estimates.ado:985-1002`. Unlike the `e(aic)` macro -- which several commands do not store at all, and which `regress` stores on a different scale -- this is the surface that matches what Stata's `estat ic` prints.

**Signature**

```python
ws.estat_ic(result: 'Any', *, n: 'float | None' = None, df: 'float | None' = None) -> 'IcResult'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.estat_ic(ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit())
```

**Output**

```text

Akaike's information criterion and Bayesian information criterion

-----------------------------------------------------------------------------
       Model |          N   ll(null)  ll(model)      df        AIC        BIC
-------------+---------------------------------------------------------------
           . |        200  -357.2327  -291.7997       3   589.5994   599.4944
-----------------------------------------------------------------------------
Note: BIC uses N = number of observations. See [R] IC note.
```

**Notes:** `regress` stores no `e(aic)`; this function computes the criterion on demand, so it works there anyway.

### `estat_imtest`

**Stata:** `estat imtest, white`

Cameron & Trivedi's information-matrix test after `regress` (Stata `estat imtest`): a decomposition of the score test into heteroskedasticity, skewness and kurtosis components plus the total. `white=True` also computes White's original test, which is the heteroskedasticity component reported on its own. Weights and `cluster()` are refused because `imtest.ado` refuses them -- the statistic would then be computed from the unweighted residual geometry and mean something else -- and a non-`regress` result raises `NotImplementedError`. Reachable as `ws.estat_imtest(result)` or `result.estat_imtest()`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.estat_imtest(result: 'Any', *, white: 'bool' = False, design: 'Any' = None) -> 'ImTestResult'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.estat_imtest(ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit(), white=True)
```

**Output**

```text

White's test
H0: Homoskedasticity
Ha: Unrestricted heteroskedasticity

    chi2(5) =   0.85
Prob > chi2 = 0.9737

Cameron & Trivedi's decomposition of IM-test

--------------------------------------------------
              Source |       chi2     df         p
---------------------+----------------------------
  Heteroskedasticity |       0.85      5    0.9737
            Skewness |       1.19      2    0.5526
            Kurtosis |       2.04      1    0.1535
---------------------+----------------------------
               Total |       4.07      8    0.8503
--------------------------------------------------
```

### `estat_overid`

**Stata:** `estat overid`

Test of the overidentifying restrictions after an IV fit (Stata `estat overid`). For 2SLS and LIML it reports Sargan and its finite-sample Basmann variant, `N*(1 - rss_aux/rss_main)`, where the auxiliary regression is of the IV residuals on ALL instruments -- included exogenous variables plus excluded instruments -- and dividing by the auxiliary RSS instead would give a statistic with no distribution. For GMM it returns Hansen's J from `e(J)`, because Sargan assumes homoskedasticity and J does not. Exactly identified models, a clustered VCE, and a `lags` value on a non-HAC fit are all refused. Reachable as `ws.estat_overid(result)` or `result.estat_overid()`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.estat_overid(result: 'Any', *, lags: 'int | None' = None, forcenonrobust: 'bool' = False) -> 'OverIdResult'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.estat_overid(ws.IV2SLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + [x2 ~ x3 + lo]', base).fit())
```

**Output**

```text

  Tests of overidentifying restrictions:

  Sargan (score) chi2(1) =  .141587  (p = 0.7067)
  Basmann chi2(1)        =  .138854  (p = 0.7094)
```

**Notes:** `lags` is the HAC prewhitening truncation and defaults to 1 under a HAC VCE, 0 otherwise; Stata rejects a negative value and so does this.

### `estat_ovtest`

**Stata:** `estat ovtest`

Ramsey's RESET test for omitted variables (Stata `estat ovtest`). By default the auxiliary regression adds the second, third and fourth powers of the fitted values RESCALED to [0, 1]; the rescaling is not cosmetic -- the fourth power of a fitted value in the hundreds makes the auxiliary design ill-conditioned, and dropping it changes the statistic. The test is the joint F on those three added terms. `rhs=True` takes Stata's alternative path and uses powers of the right-hand-side variables, where an indicator regressor contributes only its own level. Reachable as `ws.estat_ovtest(result)` or `result.estat_ovtest()`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.estat_ovtest(result: 'Any', *, rhs: 'bool' = False) -> 'OvTestResult'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.estat_ovtest(ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit(), rhs=True)
```

**Output**

```text

Ramsey RESET test for omitted variables
Omitted: Powers of independent variables

H0: Model has no omitted variables

F(6, 191) =   1.28
 Prob > F = 0.2694
```

### `estat_summarize`

**Stata:** `estat summarize`

Summary statistics for the estimation sample, variable by variable (Stata `estat summarize`): Obs, Mean, Std. dev., Min and Max for the response plus the reported regressors, or for `variables` when given. Only `fweight` changes the reported Obs, matching `estat_summ.ado`; `aweight`, `pweight` and `iweight` do not. The frame defaults to the retained `model_data`, so it must be supplied as `data=` for an estimator that did not keep one, and `cluster=` adds Stata's Obs-per-cluster block. Reachable as `ws.estat_summarize(result)` or `result.estat_summarize()`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.estat_summarize(result: 'Any', *, data: 'pd.DataFrame | None' = None, variables: 'Sequence[str] | None' = None, weights: 'Any' = None, weight_type: 'str | None' = None, cluster: 'Any' = None, cluster_name: 'str' = '', noweights: 'bool' = False, noheader: 'bool' = False, labels: 'Mapping[str, str] | None' = None) -> 'SummarizeResult'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.estat_summarize(ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit(), variables=['y', 'x1'])
```

**Output**

```text

  Estimation sample regress                Number of obs =        200

  -------------------------------------------------------------------
      Variable |         Mean      Std. dev.         Min          Max
  -------------+-----------------------------------------------------
             y |     1.020667      1.447346    -2.752204     4.881985
            x1 |     .0573402      1.025868    -2.832134     3.278208
  -------------------------------------------------------------------
```

### `estat_vce`

**Stata:** `estat vce, correlation`

Stata's `estat vce`: renders the coefficient covariance matrix, or with `correlation=True` the coefficient correlation matrix, whose diagonal is blanked to zero exactly as Stata prints it (a variable is not shown as correlated with itself). Both are pure renderings of `cov_params()` -- nothing is recomputed -- and only the lower triangle is printed. `to_frame()` returns the full symmetric matrix as a DataFrame. Reachable as `ws.estat_vce(result)` or `result.estat_vce(correlation=True)`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.estat_vce(result: 'Any', *, correlation: 'bool' = False) -> 'VceMatrix'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit().estat_vce(correlation=True)
```

**Output**

```text

Correlation matrix of coefficients

        e(V) |         x1          x2       _cons 
-------------+-----------------------------------
          x1 |          0                         
          x2 |  .00379118           0             
       _cons | -.05607434  -.06408771           0
```

### `estat_vif`

**Stata:** `estat vif`

Variance inflation factors (Stata `estat vif`): for each regressor, the auxiliary regression on all the others gives `VIF = 1/(1 - R2)` and its reciprocal as the tolerance, plus the arithmetic mean VIF. `uncentered=True` appends a constant to the auxiliary regressions and is required for a model without a constant, mirroring `vif.ado`'s refusal; `nosort=True` keeps the model's own regressor order instead of sorting by VIF. Non-linear models raise `NotImplementedError`, because the auxiliary regressions are ordinary least squares by construction. Reachable as `ws.estat_vif(result)` or `result.estat_vif()`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.estat_vif(result: 'Any', *, uncentered: 'bool' = False, nosort: 'bool' = False) -> 'VifResult'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.estat_vif(ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit())
```

**Output**

```text

    Variable |       VIF       1/VIF  
-------------+----------------------
          x1 |      1.00    0.999986
          x2 |      1.00    0.999986
-------------+----------------------
    Mean VIF |      1.00
```

### `estat_auc`

**Stata:** `estat auc`

Area under the ROC curve after `logit` or `probit` (Stata `estat auc`, which `logit_estat.ado` dispatches to `lroc, nograph`). It is computed as the Mann-Whitney U statistic divided by the product of the two group sizes, using average ranks, which handles tied predicted probabilities the way `lroc`'s iteration over distinct predicted values does -- so the two agree exactly rather than only when the predictions are distinct. `to_dict()` gives `r(area)` and `r(N)`; `str(...)` prints Stata's one-line note. Reachable as `result.estat_auc()` or as `westata.postestimation.auc.estat_auc(result)` -- unlike the other `estat_*` commands it is not re-exported into the top-level `ws` namespace.

**Signature**

```python
ws.estat_auc(result: 'Any') -> 'AucResult'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.Logit.from_formula('yb ~ x1 + x2', base).fit().estat_auc()
```

**Output**

```text
Area under ROC curve = 0.6435
```

**Notes:** Only `logit`/`probit` results are accepted: `ologit` sets no `e(estat_cmd)` and has no such subcommand.

### `estat_esize`

**Stata:** `estat esize`

Effect sizes for a linear model (Stata `estat esize`): eta-squared, which is R-squared, by default; `epsilon=True` gives epsilon-squared and `omega=True` omega-squared, the two adjusted-R-squared analogues that differ only in the denominator, where omega adds one `MS_error` and is therefore the smaller and the less negative on a null effect. The two options are mutually exclusive, as `esize.ado`'s `opts_exclusive` enforces, and only the eta-squared interval is defined, from the noncentral F. Only `regress`/`areg`/`anova` fits are served. Use `result.estat_esize()` or `westata.postestimation.esize.estat_esize(result)`; like `estat auc` it is not re-exported into the top-level `ws` namespace.

**Signature**

```python
ws.estat_esize(result: 'Any', *, epsilon: 'bool' = False, omega: 'bool' = False, level: 'float' = 0.95) -> 'EffectSizeResult'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit().estat_esize()
```

**Output**

```text

Effect sizes for linear models

         Eta-squared   [ 2.5% Conf. Interval]
            0.480210       0.379815     0.555658
```

**Notes:** `to_dict()` returns only the estimate that was computed (`eta2`, `epsilon2` or `omega2`), so a caller checking a key learns which one the command produced. Epsilon and omega carry no interval because the help file documents none.

### `estat_moran`

**Stata:** `estat moran, errorlag(W)`

Moran's test for spatial correlation in the residuals after `regress` (Stata `estat moran`). A spatial weighting matrix is required -- `errorlag=` (or its Python alias `weights=`), with repeated matrices summed into one lag operator -- because this package has no `spmatrix` toolchain. The statistic is the standard randomisation-based Moran's I, `I = (n/S0)(e'We)/(e'e)` with `E[I] = -1/(n-1)` and variance from the weights' first and second moments and the residuals' kurtosis, reported as chi2 = z^2 on one degree of freedom. Reachable as `result.estat_moran(W)` or as the module function `westata.postestimation.moran.estat_moran(result, W)`; it is not re-exported into `ws`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.estat_moran(result: 'Any', errorlag: 'Any' = None, *, weights: 'Any' = None) -> 'MoranResult'
```

**Example**

```python
r = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
W = (np.abs(np.subtract.outer(base['year'].to_numpy(), base['year'].to_numpy())) == 1).astype(float)
r.estat_moran(weights=W)
```

**Output**

```text

Moran test for spatial dependence
H0: error terms are independent
Ha: error terms are spatially correlated

  chi2(1)      =    7.1149
  Prob > chi2   =    0.0076

  Moran's I     = -0.031262
  E[I]          = -0.005025
  z             =   -2.6674
```

**Notes:** Stata's own arithmetic here lives in a compiled builtin and its "Methods and formulas" section is not in the help file, so its `r(chi2)` cannot be reproduced from local evidence. What is reported is the standard Moran test under the randomisation assumption; the p-value is exact for "no spatial correlation" but may differ from Stata's number.

### `estat_mundlak`

**Stata:** `estat mundlak`

The Mundlak specification test after `xtreg` (Stata `estat mundlak`): the within-group means of the regressors are added to the random-effects equation and tested jointly to zero, since those means are what carry any correlation between the unobserved effect and `x`; a rejection means the random-effects estimator is inconsistent. Only `xtreg, re`/`cre`/`fe` are accepted. `entity=` (and `time=` when the original fit used it) must be passed, because the fitted result keeps the coded group labels but not the column names the augmented refit needs. Available as `result.estat_mundlak(entity='firm', time='year')` or as `westata.postestimation.mundlak.estat_mundlak(result, entity=..., time=...)`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.estat_mundlak(result: 'Any', *, storer: 'Any' = None, entity: 'str | None' = None, time: 'str | None' = None) -> 'MundlakResult'
```

**Example**

```python
re = ws.RandomEffects.from_formula('ypanel ~ x1 + x2', base, entity='firm', time='year').fit()
re.estat_mundlak(entity='firm', time='year')
```

**Output**

```text

Mundlak specification test
H0: x1, x2 jointly zero (nu uncorrelated with x)

  chi2(2) =    1.6529
  Prob > chi2 =    0.4376
```

**Notes:** The coefficient and standard error come from re-estimating the augmented equation, not from the original fit, so the test is not a rescaling of the Hausman statistic.

### `estat_szroeter`

**Stata:** `estat szroeter x1 x2`

Szroeter's rank test for heteroskedasticity (Stata `estat szroeter`), one chi2(1) per variable: it ranks the variable and asks whether the squared residuals concentrate at high or low ranks, i.e. whether the variance is monotone in it. Either `variables` or `rhs=True` must be given -- with `rhs` the model's own regressors are tested -- and a single variable returns one `SzroeterResult` while several return a table. `mtest` accepts `bonferroni`, `holm`, `sidak` or `noadjust`. Only `regress`/`areg` fits that are neither weighted nor clustered are accepted, as `szroeter.ado` refuses the rest. Use `result.estat_szroeter(rhs=True)` or `westata.postestimation.szroeter.estat_szroeter(result, rhs=True)`; it is not re-exported into `ws`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.estat_szroeter(result: 'Any', variables: 'Sequence[str] | None' = None, *, rhs: 'bool' = False, mtest: 'str | None' = None) -> 'SzroeterResult | SzroeterTable'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit().estat_szroeter(rhs=True)
```

**Output**

```text

Szroeter's test for homoskedasticity
H0: Variance constant
Ha: Variance monotonic in variables

  Variable             chi2    df         p
  -----------------------------------------
  x1                 0.3760     1    0.5397
  x2                 0.1033     1    0.7479
  -----------------------------------------
```

**Notes:** Stata always performs multiple testing but adjusts nothing by default, so a single variable drops the adjusted p-value and reports `r(chi2)`/`r(df)`/`r(p)`, while several variables keep it in the `r(mtest)` matrix instead. Here one variable returns a single result and several return a table; the table's `to_dict()` flattens the p-value column to `{'p_<var>': ...}` and `to_frame()` keeps chi2, df and both p-values.

### `estat_weakrobust`

**Stata:** `estat weakrobust`

Weak-instrument-robust inference (Stata `estat weakrobust`): it reports the Anderson-Rubin test, whose distribution does not depend on instrument strength. That is Stata's default for a just-identified model; for an overidentified one Stata's default is Moreira's conditional likelihood-ratio test, and since CLR needs a simulated conditional distribution that is not implemented here, an overidentified fit must pass `ar=True` or the call raises `NotImplementedError` with that reason. `betanull` sets the null on the endogenous coefficients: a mapping, a positional sequence, or `None` for all zero. Use `result.estat_weakrobust()` or `westata.postestimation.weakrobust.estat_weakrobust(result, ar=True)`; it is not re-exported into `ws`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.estat_weakrobust(result: 'Any', *, ar: 'bool | None' = None, clr: 'bool | None' = None, betanull: 'Any' = None) -> 'WeakRobustResult'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.IV2SLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + [x2 ~ x3]', base).fit().estat_weakrobust()
```

**Output**

```text

Weak-instrument-robust inference
Anderson-Rubin chi2(1) =    1.7538
               F(1,197) =    1.7538   Prob > F = 0.1869
```

**Notes:** `betanull` as a sequence is assigned BY POSITION, which is Stata's `copy` suboption; a sequence of the wrong length is refused rather than padded or truncated, because either would test a different hypothesis than the caller wrote.

### `linktest`

**Stata:** `linktest`

Pregibon's link test for a specification error (Stata `linktest`). The model is REFIT with `_hat` (the fitted linear index) and `_hatsq` in place of the original regressors, and the reported t is `b[_hatsq]/se[_hatsq]` with the REFIT's residual degrees of freedom (`linktest.ado:131-132`). Because that refit always has three parameters -- `_hat`, `_hatsq` and the constant -- the df is n-3 whatever the original model was, so it is not simply the original fit's `df_r`. A significant squared term says the linear index is mis-specified: the functional form is wrong, not merely the fit. `xt*`, `iv*`, `sureg`, `mvreg`, `eivreg`, `zip` and `zinb` are refused because their linear index is not a single `xb`. Available as `result.linktest()`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.linktest(result: 'Any') -> 'LinkTestResult'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit().linktest()
```

**Output**

```text

Pregibon's link test
H0: the link is specified correctly

  _hat        =  1.092729
  _hatsq      = -0.041148
  t           =   -0.7700
  P>|t|       =    0.4422   (df = 197)
```

**Notes:** The standalone function is `westata.postestimation.linktest.linktest(result)` -- not `ws.linktest`; it needs the estimation-sample data and the response name, which are only retained by the formula interfaces.

### `suest`

**Stata:** `suest model1 model2`

Stack several fits into one parameter vector and one simultaneous sandwich covariance (Stata `suest`). The cross-model block is NOT zero: the stacked score of each observation puts the two models' contributions together, which is exactly what keeps the covariance valid when the models share observations. `cluster=` sums each observation's stacked score within its cluster before the outer product. The models must have the same sample size, and a model that cannot produce scores -- a non-linear estimator whose score is not the residual, or one with no design matrix -- raises `NotImplementedError`. Reachable as `result.suest(other, ...)` -- note that `suest` is a METHOD here, not a top-level `ws.suest`; the module function is `westata.postestimation.suest.suest((model1, model2), cluster=...)`, which takes the fits as one sequence.

**Signature**

```python
ws.suest(models: 'Sequence[Any]', *, names: 'Sequence[str] | None' = None, cluster: 'Any' = None) -> 'SucestResult'
```

**Example**

```python
a = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
a.suest(ws.OLS.from_formula('y1 ~ x1 + x2', base).fit())
```

**Output**

```text

Simultaneous covariance matrix
  2 models, 6 parameters, 200 observations
  parameters: model1:Intercept, model1:x1, model1:x2, model2:Intercept, model2:x1, model2:x2
```

**Notes:** Estimate the components WITHOUT `vce(robust)`/`vce(cluster)`, as `suest.sthlp` advises: `suest` supplies the robust covariance itself, so a robust component would be doubly corrected.

### `newey`

**Stata:** `newey y x1 x2, lag(2)`

Newey-West regression, `newey depvar indepvars, lag(#)` (Stata `newey`). This one is a FUNCTION, not a class: pass a formula and a frame and get a NeweyWestResult. `lag` is required and must be non-negative; `time=` plays the role of `tsset` and enables the `Checkt` spacing validation, with `force=True` allowing irregularly spaced values; only `aweight` is accepted. The `e()` collection is rewritten to `newey`'s own list, so the keys `regress` stores but `newey` does not (`r2`, `rss`, `mss`, `rmse`, `sigma`, `ll`, ...) are REMOVED rather than left behind.

**Signature**

```python
ws.newey(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, lag: 'int', time: 'Any' = None, force: 'bool' = False, noconstant: 'bool' = False, weights: 'Any' = None, weight_type: 'str | None' = None) -> 'NeweyWestResult'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.newey('y ~ x1 + x2', base, lag=2, time=np.arange(1.0, 201.0)).summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text

Regression with Newey–West standard errors      Number of obs     =        200
Maximum lag = 2                                 F(  2,       197) =     107.69
                                                Prob > F          =     0.0000

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
             |             Newey–West
           y | Coefficient  std. err.      t    P>|t|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
          x1 |   .8406445   .0662943    12.68   0.000     .7099069    .9713822
          x2 |   -.512087   .0707643    -7.24   0.000    -.6516398   -.3725342
       _cons |   1.005003   .0768392    13.08   0.000     .8534696    1.156535
```

**Notes:** The `time=` array here stands in for `tsset`. `base`'s own `year` column repeats 1..5 across entities and is therefore irregular over the whole sample, which `newey` refuses unless `force=True`; a genuine time series needs neither.

### `unitroot`

**Stata:** `dfuller y, lags(2)`

Unit-root test, selected by `test`: `'adf'` for Stata's `dfuller`, `'pp'` for `pperron` and `'kpss'`. `regression` picks the deterministic terms (`'c'`, `'n'`, `'ct'`, `'ctt'`) and `autolag` selects the lag length the statsmodels way, while `None` uses `maxlag` verbatim as Stata's `lags(#)` does. For the ADF form the statistic and the MacKinnon p-value are the same test Stata runs, but the PRINTED critical values are not MacKinnon response-surface values: `dfuller.ado` interpolates a six-point table linearly in N, and this function returns that table's values so the printed band matches Stata's. Returns a UnitRootResult.

**Signature**

```python
ws.unitroot(endog: 'Any', *, test: 'str' = 'adf', regression: 'str' = 'c', maxlag: 'int | None' = None, autolag: 'str | None' = None) -> 'UnitRootResult'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.unitroot(base['y'], test='adf', regression='c', maxlag=2)
```

**Output**

```text

Augmented Dickey–Fuller test for unit root
Variable: y                               Number of obs  = 197
                                          Number of lags =   2

H0: Random walk without drift, d = 0

                                       Dickey–Fuller
                   Test      -------- critical value ---------
              statistic           1%           5%          10%
--------------------------------------------------------------
 Z(t)            -6.706       -3.478       -2.884       -2.574
--------------------------------------------------------------
MacKinnon approximate p-value for Z(t) = 0.0000.
```

**Notes:** The p-value is unaffected by the critical-value convention and remains the MacKinnon one; only the printed band comes from Stata's table.

### `levin_lin_chu`

**Stata:** `xtunitroot llc ypanel`

Levin-Lin-Chu panel unit-root test (Stata `xtunitroot llc`). The frame must be in long format and the panel column and time column are named explicitly. `constant` and `trend` choose the deterministic specification the way Stata's `noconstant` and `trend` do: neither gives model 1, constant only model 2 (the default), both model 3. Returns an LlcResult whose printed form gives the unadjusted t, the adjusted t* with its p-value, and the panel dimensions.

**Signature**

```python
ws.levin_lin_chu(data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, variable: 'str', entity: 'str', time: 'str', trend: 'bool' = False, constant: 'bool' = True, demean: 'bool' = False, lags: 'int | None' = None, hac_lags: 'int | None' = None) -> 'LlcResult'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.levin_lin_chu(base, variable='ypanel', entity='firm', time='year')
```

**Output**

```text
Levin-Lin-Chu unit-root test
  Unadjusted t   -15.8380
  Adjusted t*    -17.2339   p = 0.0000
  40 panels x 5 periods
```

**Notes:** `lags=None` gives Stata's ONE lagged difference for LLC, not zero -- `_xturllc.ado` sets `adf_lags = 1`, unlike the IPS test, whose default is none. Getting that backwards shifts each panel's sample by one observation. `hac_lags` sets the Bartlett kernel bandwidth, defaulting to `int(3.21 * T^(1/3))`.

### `im_pesaran_shin`

**Stata:** `xtunitroot ips ypanel`

Im-Pesaran-Shin panel unit-root test (Stata `xtunitroot ips`). `trend=True` adds a linear trend to each panel's ADF regression, `demean=True` removes the cross-sectional mean before running the per-panel regressions (the IPS test with cross-sectional means removed), and `lags` sets the number of lagged differences, with `None` reproducing Stata's default of none. Returns an IpsResult printing t-bar, t-tilde-bar, the standardised Z and its p-value.

**Signature**

```python
ws.im_pesaran_shin(data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, variable: 'str', entity: 'str', time: 'str', trend: 'bool' = False, demean: 'bool' = False, lags: 'int | None' = None, level: 'float' = 0.05) -> 'IpsResult'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.im_pesaran_shin(base, variable='ypanel', entity='firm', time='year')
```

**Output**

```text
Im-Pesaran-Shin unit-root test
  t-bar     -2.4438
  t-tilde-bar -1.3463
  Z-t-tilde-bar -1.9852  p = 0.0236
  N = 40 panels x 5 periods
```

### `check_time_spacing`

**Stata:** `(no Stata equivalent -- this is the Checkt routine inside newey.ado)`

Classify the spacing of a time variable: the `Checkt` routine from `newey.ado`, exposed because `newey` needs it and because it is the only place in the package where Stata's tsset-spacing rule is stated. Returns `(tflag, delta)`, where `tflag` is 0 for regular spacing at interval 1, 1 for irregular, and 2 for regular but at an interval greater than 1, with `delta` the common interval when regular and `None` otherwise. Differences are taken on the ROW ORDER of the array, matching Stata's `sort touse tvar`, and everything is compared as floats.

**Signature**

```python
ws.check_time_spacing(time_values: 'Any') -> 'tuple[int, float | None]'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.check_time_spacing(np.arange(1.0, 21.0))
```

**Output**

```text
(0, 1.0)
```

**Notes:** Pass an integer-valued time index (or a float one), not a datetime: the array is cast to float, so a `datetime64` column becomes NANOSECONDS and a one-day series is then classified as `tflag=2` with `delta` around 8.6e13, not as interval 1.

### `parse_exp_list`

**Stata:** `(no single command -- implements the `exp_list` statistics syntax of [U] exp_list)`

Parse an `exp_list` in the syntax of `[U] exp_list`, the statistics language `table`, `estat` and `collect` use. Both the fully parenthesised form `(mean=r(mean))` and the bare top-level form Stata allows (`or=(exp(_b[mpg]))`, `_b[x1]`) are accepted, and the result is a `ParsedExpressionList` of groups, each holding named `Statistic` entries whose expression is already tokenised into lookup/call/stat nodes. Malformed text raises `ExpListError` rather than returning a partial parse.

**Signature**

```python
ws.parse_exp_list(text: 'str') -> 'ParsedExpressionList'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.parse_exp_list('(mean=r(mean)) (sd=r(sd))')
```

**Output**

```text
ParsedExpressionList(groups=(StatisticGroup(label=None, statistics=(Statistic(name='mean', expression=('stat', 'r', 'mean')),)), StatisticGroup(label=None, statistics=(Statistic(name='sd', expression=('stat', 'r', 'sd')),))))
```

### `normalize_weights`

**Stata:** `[fw=], [aw=], [pw=], [iw=] -- the weight normalization rules of [U] 20.24`

Normalizes a weight array according to Stata's rule for the given type and returns a `WeightSpec`. The four types differ in a way that changes the numbers: `fweight` and `iweight` are left unnormalized and contribute `sum(w)` as the effective sample size, while `aweight` and `pweight` are rescaled to sum to the row count and contribute `N`; `pweight` additionally sets `implies_robust`, which is the only difference between it and `aweight`. Validation rejects an unknown type, negative, missing or non-finite weights, and a zero total.

**Signature**

```python
ws.normalize_weights(weights: 'Any', weight_type: 'str') -> 'WeightSpec'
```

**Example**

```python
s = ws.normalize_weights(np.array([1.0, 2.0, 3.0, 4.0]), 'fweight')
(s.effective_n, s.implies_robust, s.n_obs)
```

**Output**

```text
(10.0, False, 4)
```

**Notes:** `effective_n` is what the degrees of freedom use: 10 for this four-row `fweight` example, because each row counts as its weight. That is why `nobs` and `e(N)` diverge under frequency weights.

### `ereturn_list`

**Stata:** `ereturn list`

The four sections of Stata's `ereturn list` for one estimation result: scalars as `e(name) = value`, macros as `e(name) : "text"`, matrices as `e(name) : r x c`, and functions as bare names. WHICH scalars a command publishes is its own decision, and Stata's choice is narrower than the full `e()` dictionary, so the printed block is a faithful subset rather than a dump. The whole block opens with a blank line, as Stata's does.

**Signature**

```python
ws.ereturn_list(self) -> 'str'
```

**Example**

```python
r = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
r.ereturn_list()
```

**Output**

```text

scalars:
                  e(N) =  200
              e(sum_w) =  200
               e(df_m) =  2
               e(df_r) =  197
                  e(F) =  90.99954130641439
                 e(r2) =  .4802098236178376
               e(rmse) =  1.048768791147598
                e(mss) =  200.1836988170982
                e(rss) =  216.6834475251831
               e(r2_a) =  .4749327659895922
                 e(ll) =  -291.7997221200006
               e(ll_0) =  -357.2327276994888
               e(rank) =  3

macros:
              e(title) : "Linear regression"
          e(marginsok) : "XB default"
                e(vce) : "ols"
             e(depvar) : "y"
                e(cmd) : "regress"
         e(properties) : "b V"
            e(predict) : "regres_p"
              e(model) : "ols"
          e(estat_cmd) : "regress_estat"

matrices:
                  e(b) :  1 x 3
                  e(V) :  3 x 3
               e(beta) :  1 x 2

functions:
             e(sample)
```

### `estat_bgodfrey`

**Stata:** `estat bgodfrey, lags(#)`

Breusch-Godfrey LM test for serial correlation of order `lags`. The test regresses the residuals on the original regressors PLUS the lagged residuals; including the regressors is what makes it valid, and omitting them turns it into a different (invalid) test. Lags follow the TIME axis, not the row axis: pass `time=` (a column name of the estimation frame, or the period values) to reproduce `tsset` semantics, where a period missing from the sample makes that lag missing rather than pairing the neighbouring rows. Measured on icecream.dta with `time == 5` dropped, Stata's 4.220 against 3.286 by row order. Missing lags are zero-filled by default and kept missing under `nomiss0`. Refused after a robust or clustered VCE, on panel data spanning several units, and on non-`regress` fits, as Stata refuses them (r(198)/r(459)/r(321)).

**Signature**

```python
ws.estat_bgodfrey(result: 'Any', *, lags: 'int | Sequence[int]' = 1, nomiss0: 'bool' = False, time: 'str | Sequence[Any] | None' = None, delta: 'float' = 1.0) -> 'BgTestResult'
```

**Example**

```python
print(ws.estat_bgodfrey(ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit(), lags=2))
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

### `estat_corrgram`

**Stata:** `estat corrgram`

Correlogram: autocorrelations, partial autocorrelations, the Ljung-Box Q statistic and its p-value at each lag. The default order is `max(1, min(int(n/2) - 2, 40))` (`corrgram.ado:18`). The PAC is the coefficient on lag k in a regression of the residual on its first k lags WITH a constant (`pac.ado:128-156`), not the Yule-Walker recursion. `time=` supplies the `tsset` axis, so a gap makes those lags missing; Stata then prints `(note: time series has 1 gap)` and this reproduces it. A PAC that cannot be estimated prints `.` and draws no bar.

**Signature**

```python
ws.estat_corrgram(result: 'Any', *, lags: 'int | None' = None, time: 'str | Any | None' = None, delta: 'float' = 1.0) -> 'Correlogram'
```

**Example**

```python
print(ws.estat_corrgram(ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit(), lags=4))
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

### `estat_dwatson`

**Stata:** `estat dwatson`

Durbin-Watson d statistic for first-order autocorrelation of the residuals. Stata prints it on ONE line as `Durbin–Watson d-statistic(  k,    N) = value` with an EN-DASH in the label and the two counts padded; the value alone is not enough to compare against a printed table. `time=` stands in for `tsset`: across a gap the straddling pair is skipped rather than differenced, and a `Number of gaps in sample = N` line is printed first. Measured on icecream.dta with `time == 5` dropped: .8930395 under `time=`, 1.0868969 by row order.

**Signature**

```python
ws.estat_dwatson(result: 'Any', *, time: 'str | Any | None' = None, delta: 'float' = 1.0) -> 'DurbinWatsonResult'
```

**Example**

```python
print(ws.estat_dwatson(ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()))
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

### `f_test`

**Stata:** `test x1 = 0, x2 = 0 (joint F)`

statsmodels-shaped joint F test on a fitted result. `r_matrix` accepts one or more restriction strings separated by commas (or a sequence); they are parsed by the same Stata constraint grammar as `test`, then reported jointly as F. Returns a `ContrastResults` carrying `statistic`, `pvalue`, `df_num`/`df_denom` and the `fvalue`/`tvalue` aliases. This is the statsmodels-ecosystem surface, not Stata's own `test` method.

**Signature**

```python
ws.f_test(self, r_matrix: 'Any', cov_p: 'Any' = None, invcov: 'Any' = None) -> 'Any'
```

**Example**

```python
r = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
r.f_test('x1 = 0, x2 = 0')
```

**Output**

```text
<F contrast: statistic=90.9995, pvalue=1.02109e-28, df_denom=197>
```

### `first_stage`

**Stata:** `ivregress 2sls ..., first`

First-stage regression table for an IV fit (Stata `ivregress ..., first`). One block per endogenous regressor, headed "First-stage regressions", giving the F, R-squared, adjusted R-squared and Root MSE of that equation, then its coefficient table with the included exogenous variables and excluded instruments. This is a different output from `estat_firststage`, which prints the DIAGNOSTICS for the same equations; Stata offers both and they are not substitutes, so both are kept.

**Signature**

```python
ws.first_stage(self) -> 'str'
```

**Example**

```python
r = ws.IV2SLS.from_formula('ytreat ~ x1 + [treat ~ x3]', base).fit()
r.first_stage()
```

**Output**

```text
First-stage regressions
-----------------------

                                                        Number of obs =    200
                                                        F(2, 197)     =  36.02
                                                        Prob > F      = 0.0000
                                                        R-squared     = 0.2678
                                                        Adj R-squared = 0.2603
                                                        Root MSE      = 0.4139

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
       treat | Coefficient  Std. err.      t    P>|t|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
          x1 |   .0022204   .0291298     0.08   0.939    -.0552258    .0596667
          x3 |   .2398927   .0287406     8.35   0.000     .1832141    .2965714
       _cons |   .6613358   .0293962    22.50   0.000     .6033641    .7193075
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
```

### `get_influence`

**Stata:** `(no Stata equivalent -- statsmodels OLSInfluence names)`

Influence measures for a fitted result, named as statsmodels' `OLSInfluence` names them. Returns a dict with `hat_matrix_diag` (leverage), `resid`, `resid_studentized_internal`, `resid_studentized`, `standardized_resid`, `cooks_distance` (array plus observation indices) and `nobs`. Only leverage-based measures are offered: deletion diagnostics would need one re-fit per observation, and returning a plausible-but-wrong array was judged worse than omitting the key.

**Signature**

```python
ws.get_influence(self) -> 'Any'
```

**Example**

```python
r = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
r.get_influence()['hat_matrix_diag'][:5]
```

**Output**

```text
[0.024179 0.010089 0.005392 0.011287 0.005195]
```

### `t_test`

**Stata:** `test x1 = 0 (single restriction, t form)`

statsmodels-shaped t test of a single linear restriction. `r_matrix` is one restriction string (or a one-element sequence); `use_t` forces the t form, whose statistic is the square root of the corresponding F. Returns a `ContrastResults` with `statistic`, `pvalue`, `conf_int()` for a single restriction, and the `fvalue`/`tvalue` aliases. For a joint set of restrictions use `f_test` instead.

**Signature**

```python
ws.t_test(self, r_matrix: 'Any', cov_p: 'Any' = None, use_t: 'bool | None' = None) -> 'Any'
```

**Example**

```python
r = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
r.t_test('x1 = 0')
```

**Output**

```text
<t contrast: statistic=11.5997, pvalue=4.75584e-24, df_denom=197>
```

### `wntestq`

**Stata:** `wntestq varname, lags(#)`

Portmanteau (Ljung-Box) test for white noise: whether the series' autocorrelations up to `lags` are jointly zero. Unlike `estat bgodfrey` it tests the SERIES, not the residuals of a regression, so it takes the values or a result whose residuals you mean. The default order is `max(1, min(int(n/2) - 2, 40))` (`ac.ado:71`), independent of any previous regression's parameter count and capped at 40 -- at n = 758 Stata reports chi2(40), not 377. `time=` gives it the `tsset` axis; a gap then drops those pairs from the autocovariance sums and adds a `(note: time series has N gaps)` line.

**Signature**

```python
ws.wntestq(result: 'Any', *, lags: 'int | None' = None, time: 'str | Any | None' = None, delta: 'float' = 1.0) -> 'PortmanteauResult'
```

**Example**

```python
print(ws.wntestq(ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit(), lags=2))
```

**Output**

```text
None
```


## Working with a fitted result

### `StataResults`

**Stata:** `(the object every estimation command returns, analogous to Stata's e() results)`

The object returned by every `fit()`. It carries a statsmodels-compatible surface (`params`, `bse`, `pvalues`, `conf_int`, `cov_params`), the Stata `e()` macros as a dictionary, and the postestimation commands as methods -- `test`, `lincom`, `margins`, `predict`, and the twenty `estat_*` forwarders. This is where most day-to-day work happens after a fit.

**Signature**

```python
ws.StataResults(*, params: 'np.ndarray', param_names: 'list[str]', cov: 'np.ndarray', resid: 'np.ndarray', fittedvalues: 'np.ndarray', nobs: 'int', df_resid: 'float', df_model: 'float', dist: 'str' = 't', model_name: 'str' = '', cov_type: 'str' = 'nonrobust', e: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, design_matrix: 'tuple[np.ndarray, list[str]] | None' = None, model_data: 'pd.DataFrame | None' = None, level: 'float | None' = None) -> 'None'
```

**Example**

```python
r = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
r.e['r2']
```

**Output**

```text
0.4802098236178376
```

**Notes:** The `e()` key set differs per command; it is not uniform, and assuming otherwise is how invented keys get added. `e(ic)`, `e(aic)` and `e(title)` are each present for some commands and absent for others.

### `params`

**Stata:** `regress -- the `Coef.` column, i.e. `e(b)``

Point estimates as a `pandas.Series` indexed by `param_names`, in the column order the design matrix was built with. That order is patsy's, which puts the constant FIRST (`Intercept`), whereas Stata's `e(b)` puts `_cons` LAST; the two vectors hold the same numbers in a different order. Use `stata_params` when the comparison is against Stata's `e(b)`.

**Example**

```python
r = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
r.params
```

**Output**

```text
Intercept    1.005003
x1           0.840645
x2          -0.512087
Name: params, dtype: float64
```

### `bse`

**Stata:** `regress -- the `Std. err.` column`

Standard errors, the square roots of the diagonal of the covariance matrix that `cov_type` selected. They are computed once at construction from `cov`, so a caller who changes the covariance afterwards does not change `bse`.

**Example**

```python
r = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
r.bse
```

**Output**

```text
Intercept    0.074428
x1           0.072471
x2           0.074822
Name: bse, dtype: float64
```

### `pvalues`

**Stata:** `regress -- the `P>|t|` column; logit -- the `P>|z|` column`

Two-sided p-values for the null that each coefficient is zero. The reference distribution follows `dist`: Student t with `df_resid` degrees of freedom when `dist` is `"t"`, the standard normal when it is `"z"`. A model whose `df_resid` is `G - 1` from a clustered VCE therefore uses that count, not `nobs - k`.

**Example**

```python
r = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
r.pvalues
```

**Output**

```text
Intercept    7.633899e-30
x1           4.755835e-24
x2           9.518706e-11
Name: pvalues, dtype: float64
```

### `tvalues`

**Stata:** `regress -- the `t` column`

The coefficient divided by its standard error, always named `tvalues` even for a z-based model. It is the test statistic, not a t statistic as such: for a logit fit the same numbers are the z statistics Stata prints. `zvalues` is the same object under the name the distribution calls for.
