**Example**

```python
r = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
r.tvalues
```

**Output**

```text
Intercept    13.502927
x1           11.599724
x2           -6.844043
Name: tvalues, dtype: float64
```

### `zvalues`

**Stata:** `logit, probit, poisson -- the `z` column`

The normal statistic for the null that a coefficient is zero. It is numerically identical to `tvalues` -- both are `params / bse` -- and is exposed under this name so downstream code can branch on the inference distribution without recomputing it. It is an alias, not an independent calculation, so the Series it returns still carries the name `tvalues`.

**Example**

```python
r = ws.Logit.from_formula('yb ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
r.zvalues
```

**Output**

```text
Intercept    1.032644
x1           3.466025
x2          -0.888860
Name: tvalues, dtype: float64
```

### `fvalue`

**Stata:** `regress -- the `F(k, n-k)` model test`

The model's joint F statistic, read from `e(F)`; NaN when the command does not store it. Likelihood-based commands report a chi-square model test instead, stored as `e(chi2)`, so `fvalue` is NaN after a logit or Poisson fit -- that is the correct absence, not a missing calculation.

**Example**

```python
r = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
r.fvalue
```

**Output**

```text
90.99954130641439
```

### `llf`

**Stata:** `e(ll) -- the log-likelihood`

The maximised log-likelihood, read from `e(ll)`; NaN when the command does not record it. It is the number `estat ic` and `lrtest` build their statistics from, so a fit that reports NaN here cannot support either of them.

**Example**

```python
r = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
r.llf
```

**Output**

```text
-291.7997221200006
```

### `aic`

**Stata:** `e(aic) -- note that `estat ic` is the surface that matches Stata's printed table`

AIC read from `e(aic)`; NaN when the command does not record it. For `regress` this is NOT the number `estat ic` prints: `regress` stores no AIC at all, and the value exposed here is on the Gaussian-deviance scale, `N log(rss/N) + 2k`, which is a different quantity from the likelihood-scale `-2 logL + 2k`. For commands that do store `e(aic)` the two agree.

**Example**

```python
r = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
(r.aic, ws.estat_ic(r).aic)
```

**Output**

```text
(589.5994442400012, 589.5994442400012)
```

**Notes:** Do not compare `result.aic` against `estat ic` output for a `regress` fit; they are on different scales. `e(_aic_is_stata)` records which sense applies, and the summary table prints AIC only when that flag says the value is Stata's.

### `bic`

**Stata:** `e(bic) -- with the same caveat as `aic``

BIC read from `e(bic)`; NaN when the command does not record it. The same scale caveat as `aic` applies: after `regress` the stored value is the Gaussian-deviance `N log(rss/N) + k log(N)`, not the likelihood-scale criterion `estat ic` prints.

**Example**

```python
r = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
(r.bic, ws.estat_ic(r).bic)
```

**Output**

```text
(599.4943963396453, 599.4943963396453)
```

### `nobs`

**Stata:** `e(N) / the `Number of obs` line`

The number of ROWS in the estimation sample, as an integer. It is deliberately not `e(N)` under frequency weights: with `fweight` the rows are replication counts, so `e(N)` becomes `sum(w)` while `nobs` stays the row count. Use `result.e['N']` for the effective sample size.

**Example**

```python
r = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
(r.nobs, r.e['N'])
```

**Output**

```text
(200, 200.0)
```

### `df_model`

**Stata:** `e(df_m) -- model degrees of freedom`

Model degrees of freedom, i.e. the number of restrictions the model test imposes: the regressors excluding the constant. It is read from `e(df_m)`, so for a one-regressor model it is 1, not 2, even though the coefficient vector has two entries.

**Example**

```python
r = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
r.df_model
```

**Output**

```text
2.0
```

### `df_resid`

**Stata:** `e(df_r) -- residual degrees of freedom`

The denominator degrees of freedom the reference distribution uses. It is NOT always `nobs - k`: for a clustered VCE it is `G - 1`, the number of clusters minus one. This property returns the value the estimator computed at fit time; `e(df_r)` is a separate matter, because that macro is only present for non-z models (or when an estimator such as `tobit` or `xtivreg` supplied it explicitly), so a logit fit has a `df_resid` here and no `e(df_r)`.

**Example**

```python
r = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1', base).fit(cov_type='cluster', cov_kwds={'groups': base['firm']})
(r.df_resid, r.cov_type)
```

**Output**

```text
(39.0, 'cluster')
```

### `dist`

**Stata:** `(no Stata equivalent -- Stata picks t or z per command and stores it in e() indirectly)`

Which reference distribution inference uses: `"t"` for t statistics and t confidence intervals, `"z"` for normal tests. It is decided by the estimator, not by the caller, and it is what `pvalues` and `conf_int` branch on. A linear fit reports `t`; logit, probit and the count models report `z`.

**Example**

```python
r = ws.Logit.from_formula('yb ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
r.dist
```

**Output**

```text
z
```

### `model_name`

**Stata:** `e(cmd) -- the command name, e.g. `regress``

The Stata command name of the estimator that produced the result -- `regress`, `logit`, `reghdfe` and so on -- which is also the label the summary prints. When the estimator did not record `e(cmd)`, this value is used to synthesise it, so `result.e['cmd']` is always available even though Stata always stores it.

**Example**

```python
r = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
r.model_name
```

**Output**

```text
regress
```

### `cov_type`

**Stata:** `e(vce) / e(vcetype) -- the `Covariance Type` line of the summary`

Label of the covariance estimator that was used, kept for display and auditing: `nonrobust` by default, `HC1` for heteroskedasticity-robust, `cluster` for clustered. It is a recorded label, not a switch; changing it after the fact changes nothing, because the matrix itself was built at fit time.

**Example**

```python
r = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit(cov_type='HC1')
r.cov_type
```

**Output**

```text
HC1
```

### `cov`

**Stata:** `e(V) -- the coefficient covariance matrix`

The coefficient covariance matrix as a `k x k` numpy array, already carrying every small-sample correction that was requested. It is the same object `cov_params()` returns; `cov_params()` exists only so statsmodels-compatible code that calls a method finds one. Order follows `param_names`, so it is patsy's order, not Stata's `e(V)` order.

**Example**

```python
r = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
np.round(r.cov, 4)
```

**Output**

```text
[[ 0.0055 -0.0003 -0.0004]
 [-0.0003  0.0053  0.    ]
 [-0.0004  0.      0.0056]]
```

### `e`

**Stata:** `e() -- the stored estimation results`

The Stata `e()` macros as a dictionary-like object with BOTH item and attribute access. Item access keeps normal mapping semantics and raises `KeyError` for a macro the command did not store; attribute access is deliberately forgiving and returns `None` instead. The key set DIFFERS PER COMMAND -- assuming uniformity is exactly how invented keys get added to a manual: `regress` stores no `aic` on the `estat ic` scale and `tobit` stores no `aic` at all.

**Example**

```python
r = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
sorted(r.e)
```

**Output**

```text
['F', 'N', '_aic_is_stata', 'aic', 'bic', 'cmd', 'depvar', 'df_m', 'df_r', 'estat_cmd', 'll', 'll_0', 'marginsok', 'model', 'mss', 'predict', 'properties', 'r2', 'r2_a', 'rank', 'rmse', 'rss', 'sigma', 'sum_w', 'title', 'vce', 'vcetype']
```

**Notes:** Two macros are synthesised because every command documents them: `e(depvar)` from the response name and `e(cmd)` from `model_name`. `e(cmdline)` is deliberately NOT synthesised -- it records the command as typed, options included, and this package has no such string, so inventing one would fabricate a value.

### `fittedvalues`

**Stata:** `predict, xb -- the linear prediction`

The fitted values on the estimation sample, a numpy array of length `nobs` in row order. For weighted fits these are the predictions of the weighted least-squares problem, not weighted fitted values. `predict(kind='xb')` recomputes the same quantity from the retained design matrix, which is what to use for out-of-sample data.

**Example**

```python
r = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
r.fittedvalues[:5]
```

**Output**

```text
[2.524914 0.144945 1.126616 2.107762 0.869349]
```

### `resid`

**Stata:** `predict, residuals -- the residuals`

Residuals on the estimation sample, `y - fittedvalues`, a numpy array of length `nobs`. They are raw residuals, not standardised or studentised ones, and for weighted fits they are the raw deviations rather than the weighted residuals.

**Example**

```python
r = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
r.resid[:5]
```

**Output**

```text
[-0.245449 -0.01285  -1.598986  0.354059  0.682509]
```

### `rsquared`

**Stata:** `e(r2)`

R-squared read from `e(r2)`; NaN when the command does not record it. This exists for the OLS family; the maximum-likelihood families store a pseudo R-squared in `e(r2_p)` instead and leave this NaN, so a NaN here means "this model has no R-squared", not a failed computation. Read `e['r2_p']` for logit, probit and the count models.

**Example**

```python
ols = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
lg = ws.Logit.from_formula('yb ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
((ols.rsquared, lg.rsquared), lg.e['r2_p'])
```

**Output**

```text
((0.4802098236178376, nan), 0.05037590968400629)
```

### `rsquared_adj`

**Stata:** `e(r2_a)`

Adjusted R-squared read from `e(r2_a)`; NaN when the command does not record it. Like `rsquared` it belongs to the linear family, and the degrees-of-freedom adjustment uses the effective sample size, so a frequency-weighted fit corrects by `sum(w)` rather than by the row count.

**Example**

```python
r = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
r.rsquared_adj
```

**Output**

```text
0.4749327659895922
```

### `model_data`

**Stata:** `e(sample) / the estimation sample`

The estimation-sample data frame the fit was computed on. Stata exposes the same information through e(sample) and the saved dataset; here it is the frame itself, which is why postestimation commands that must re-read the estimation data -- linktest, estat_mundlak, predict with an explicit sample -- can work from a fitted result without the caller passing the data again.

**Example**

```python
r = ws.OLS.from_formula("y ~ x1 + x2", base).fit()
list(r.model_data.columns)
```

**Output**

```text
['y', 'x1', 'x2', 'x3', 'yb', 'ycnt', 'ycens', 'lo', 'hi', 'y1', 'y2', 'firm', 'year', 'ypanel', 'treat', 'ytreat', 'sel', 'yselect']
```

**Notes:** Only estimators that retained the sample carry it; assuming it is always present is what makes a postestimation call fail late rather than at the call site.

### `converged`

**Stata:** `e(converged) -- 1 if the optimizer converged`

Whether the optimizer reported convergence, read from `e(converged)`. An ABSENT macro is reported as `True` rather than `False`, because it means the estimator runs no iterative optimizer at all -- a closed-form fit has nothing to fail to converge. So `True` has two senses here: "converged" for an ML fit and "not applicable" for OLS.

**Example**

```python
r = ws.Logit.from_formula('yb ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
(r.converged, r.iterations, r.grad_norm)
```

**Output**

```text
(True, 5.0, nan)
```

### `iterations`

**Stata:** `e(ic) -- the number of iterations`

The number of optimizer iterations, read from `e(ic)`, which the help files document as the iteration count. NaN when the estimator is not iterative or the backend reported no count. It is a float, not an int, so a non-iterative fit can be distinguished from a fit that converged in zero iterations.

**Example**

```python
r = ws.Logit.from_formula('yb ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
r.iterations
```

**Output**

```text
5.0
```

### `grad_norm`

**Stata:** `e(gradient_norm) -- the norm of the gradient at the reported estimate`

The norm of the gradient at the reported estimate, read from `e(gradient_norm)`. A small value is the numeric counterpart of `converged`: the optimizer stops when this is near zero. It is NaN whenever the backend does not report it, DELIBERATELY, because `0.0` would read as "exactly converged" and a caller cannot distinguish that from unavailable data.

**Example**

```python
r = ws.Logit.from_formula('yb ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
r.grad_norm
```

**Output**

```text
nan
```

### `param_names`

**Stata:** `row names of e(b) -- but in patsy spelling and order`

The coefficient names, in coefficient order, as a list of strings. The spelling is patsy's: `Intercept` first, factor levels as `foreign[T.Foreign]`, interactions with `:`. Stata spells these `_cons` last, `foreign` for a bare dummy, and `#` for interactions; `stata_param_names` performs that translation.

**Example**

```python
r = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
r.param_names
```

**Output**

```text
['Intercept', 'x1', 'x2']
```

### `test`

**Stata:** `test x1 = 0`

Wald test of linear hypotheses (Stata `test`). A bare coefficient list (`'x1 x2'`) tests that EACH is zero -- a joint test, not a test of their sum -- and `*` expands as a wildcard; `'x1 = x2'` and the chain `'x1 = x2 = x3'` test the corresponding equalities; several constraints are separated by commas inside one string or passed as separate arguments. `coef=True` additionally returns a dict with Stata's constrained estimates `b_c = b - VR'(RVR')^-1(Rb - r)`, their covariance, and the test itself. The statistic is F when the model reports t and chi2 when it reports z, on `df_resid` -- for a clustered VCE that is `G - 1`, not `nobs - k`. Available as `result.test(...)`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.test(self, *specs: 'Any', coef: 'bool' = False) -> 'Any'
```

**Example**

```python
r = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
r.test('x1 = 0')
```

**Output**

```text

 ( 1)  x1 = 0

       F(  1,   197) =  134.55
            Prob > F =    0.0000
```

**Notes:** A constant inside a bare coefficient list is an error, not a hypothesis: Stata answers it with `r(111) '<n>' not found`, so put it on the right-hand side of `=` (`'x1 = 0'`), where it is accepted.

### `lincom`

**Stata:** `lincom x1 - x2`

One linear combination of the coefficients (Stata `lincom`): `L b` with standard error `sqrt(L V L')` and the model's own reference distribution. A bare constant is moved to the right-hand side of the printed constraint and never enters the variance, so `lincom('2*x1 - x2 + 3')` has exactly the same standard error as `lincom('2*x1 - x2')`. An `=` sign or a comma is rejected -- Stata's syntax is ONE expression, and a comma separates the expression from its options -- so several combinations belong in `test`. `eform=True` reports `exp(Lb)` with an exponentiated interval. Returns a LincomResult whose `to_dict()` gives Stata's `r()` names.

**Signature**

```python
ws.lincom(self, expression: 'str', *, level: 'float' = 0.95, eform: 'bool' = False) -> 'Any'
```

**Example**

```python
r = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
r.lincom('x1 - x2')
```

**Output**

```text

 ( 1)  x1 - x2 = 0

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
           y | Coefficient  Std. err.      t    P>|t|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
         (1) |   1.352732   .1039679    13.01   0.000     1.147699    1.557764
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
```

**Notes:** For a z-based fit the statistic is stored as `r(z)` and `r(df)` is genuinely absent -- asking for the other name finds nothing, which matches Stata rather than coercing a value into existence.

### `predict`

**Stata:** `predict yhat, pr`

Typed prediction (Stata `predict newvar, <type>`). `kind` selects the type and `exog` defaults to the design matrix retained on the result, so the usual in-sample call takes no arguments. The accepted kinds are per estimator -- `xb`, `stdp` for every model; `pr` for logit/probit/poisson/ordered/tobit; `n`, `ir` for count models; `e`, `ystar` for tobit/interval regression; `score` for logit/probit/poisson; `hat`, `cooksd`, `dfbeta` and friends for regress -- and the authoritative list is `westata.postestimation.predict.supported_types(model_name)`. A result that did not retain its design raises `ConsistencyError` with an estimator-specific explanation rather than a generic message.

**Signature**

```python
ws.predict(result: 'Any', exog: 'np.ndarray', kind: 'str | None' = None, *, lower: 'float | None' = None, upper: 'float | None' = None, outcome: 'int | None' = None, n: 'int | float | None' = None) -> 'np.ndarray'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.Logit.from_formula('yb ~ x1 + x2', base).fit().predict(kind='pr')[:6]
```

**Output**

```text
[0.76494  0.407506 0.547025 0.670233 0.518222 0.633899]
```

**Notes:** After `reghdfe` no type is offered at all: whether the absorbed effects belong in the prediction is a real choice Stata exposes as separate options, and returning one of the two without saying which would be silently wrong. After `xtreg` the types are available, and `xb` excludes the panel effect, which is Stata's default (`xtreg_postestimation.sthlp:128`); `xbu` is the variant that adds it.

### `summary`

**Stata:** `(no Stata equivalent -- renders the estimation table Stata prints)`

Render a text summary of the fit. `style='statsmodels'` (the default) prints the statsmodels-style table, while `style='stata'` prints the layout Stata prints for the same command -- for `regress` the ANOVA and fit block with the coefficient table, for a discrete-choice model the LR chi-square header and z statistics. It RETURNS a string rather than printing, so it displays in a notebook and can also be written to a file unchanged.

**Signature**

```python
ws.summary(self, style: 'str' = 'stata', *, odds_ratio: 'bool' = False, first: 'bool' = False) -> 'str'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text

      Source |       SS           df       MS      Number of obs   =       200
-------------+----------------------------------   F(2, 197)       =     91.00
       Model |  200.183699         2  100.091849   Prob > F        =    0.0000
    Residual |  216.683448       197  1.09991598   R-squared       =    0.4802
-------------+----------------------------------   Adj R-squared   =    0.4749
       Total |  416.867146       199  2.09480978   Root MSE        =    1.0488

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
           y | Coefficient  Std. err.      t    P>|t|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
          x1 |   .8406445   .0724711    11.60   0.000     .6977258    .9835633
          x2 |   -.512087   .0748223    -6.84   0.000    -.6596425   -.3645315
       _cons |   1.005003   .0744285    13.50   0.000     .8582236    1.151781
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
```

### `wald_test`

**Stata:** `(no Stata spelling -- Stata expresses the same restriction as 'test x1 = 0')`

statsmodels-compatible joint Wald test: `R b = q`, with `R` one row per restriction and one COLUMN PER PARAMETER, and `q` defaulting to zeros. `scalar=True` returns an F test with `df_denom = df_resid` and `scalar=False` a chi2 test, which is statsmodels' own behaviour; the returned object is the same `WaldTest` that `test` returns, exposing `statistic`, `df` (the numerator degrees of freedom), `df_denom`, `pvalue` and `distribution`. Use `test` for Stata's constraint syntax -- this is the matrix interface, so a shape mismatch raises `ValueError` instead of guessing which parameters were meant.

**Signature**

```python
ws.wald_test(self, R: 'Any', q: 'Any' = None, scalar: 'bool' = True) -> 'Any'
```

**Example**

```python
r = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
r.wald_test(np.array([[0.0, 1.0, 0.0]]), scalar=False)
```

**Output**

```text

 ( 1)  x1 = 0

       chi2(  1) =  134.55
            Prob > chi2 =    0.0000
```

### `cov_params`

**Stata:** `matrix list e(V)`

The coefficient covariance matrix as a plain numpy array in `param_names` order. That is patsy's order, which puts the intercept FIRST and spells factor levels its own way, and it is NOT Stata's `e(V)` order -- Stata puts `_cons` last and writes the same restriction differently. `stata_cov_params` and `stata_params` give the permuted and renamed version, and `estat_vce` prints it the way Stata does. The array is the stored object itself, not a copy.

**Signature**

```python
ws.cov_params(self) -> 'np.ndarray'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit().cov_params()
```

**Output**

```text
[[ 0.00554  -0.000302 -0.000357]
 [-0.000302  0.005252  0.000021]
 [-0.000357  0.000021  0.005598]]
```

### `conf_int`

**Stata:** `(no Stata command -- the confidence-interval columns Stata prints in the estimation table)`

Confidence intervals for the coefficients as a DataFrame with `lower` and `upper` columns indexed by parameter name. `alpha=0.05` by default; the critical value comes from Student's t with `df_resid` when `dist='t'` and from the standard normal when `dist='z'`, which is the same choice the coefficient table makes. An `alpha` outside (0, 1) raises `ValueError`. The index is in `param_names` order, so it lines up with `params` and `bse`, not with Stata's `e(b)`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.conf_int(self, alpha: 'float | None' = None) -> 'pd.DataFrame'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit().conf_int(alpha=0.10)
```

**Output**

```text
              lower     upper
Intercept  0.882000  1.128005
x1         0.720877  0.960412
x2        -0.635740 -0.388434
```

### `fitted_series`

**Stata:** `predict yhat, xb -- with the estimation sample's index`

Fitted values as a `pandas.Series` carrying the estimation sample's index and the name `fittedvalues`. This is the labelled counterpart of the plain `fittedvalues` ndarray: replacing that attribute with a Series would break positional access, so the labelled form is a separate name. When the fit did not retain observation labels the index falls back to a `RangeIndex`, which is honest -- the numbers are still there and the labels say only "row i".

**Example**

```python
r = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
r.fitted_series.head()
```

**Output**

```text
0    2.524914
1    0.144945
2    1.126616
3    2.107762
4    0.869349
Name: fittedvalues, dtype: float64
```

### `level`

**Stata:** `regress ..., level(90)`

Confidence level used by `conf_int()` and the printed intervals, as a FRACTION (`0.90` for Stata's `level(90)`). Defaults to `0.95`. A model fitted with `level=` records it here, so a result estimated at 90% reports 90% intervals unless a caller passes an explicit `alpha`. Stata's `level(#)` takes a PERCENTAGE; the estimators accept either spelling and normalise, because reading `0.90` as a percentage would silently produce a 0.9% interval whose only symptom is "intervals look too narrow".

**Example**

```python
ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1', base).fit(level=90).conf_int()
```

**Output**

```text
              lower     upper
Intercept  0.836140  1.108573
x1         0.709618  0.975431
```

**Notes:** The property returns the fraction; `conf_int()` still accepts an explicit `alpha`, which wins over the recorded level for that call. Only weighted and unweighted estimation are affected -- this is a reporting choice and never changes a coefficient or a standard error.

### `model`

**Stata:** `(no Stata equivalent -- a statsmodels interop shim)`

The fitted model as statsmodels consumers expect it. `statsmodels.stats.anova.anova_lm` reads `res.model` and re-fits from it; without this attribute that call raised AttributeError. The returned `ResultModel` exposes exactly `endog`, `exog`, `exog_names` and a `fit()` that returns this same result -- enough for `anova_lm`'s contract without pretending to BE a statsmodels model. A wider imitation would invite callers to rely on behaviour that is not there.

**Example**

```python
r = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
r.model
```

**Output**

```text
<ResultModel: 200 obs, 3 params>
```

### `odds_ratios`

**Stata:** `logit ..., or  (column, not a separate function)`

This name is not an exported function. `logit ..., or` is expressed as `result.summary(style="stata", odds_ratio=True)`, which exponentiates the estimate, its standard error and both interval endpoints and relabels the column `Odds ratio`; z and P>|z| are unchanged because they come from the untransformed scale.

**Example**

```python
print(ws.Logit.from_formula('yb ~ x1 + x2', base).fit().summary(style='stata', odds_ratio=True)[-400:])
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

### `resid_series`

**Stata:** `predict e, residuals -- with the estimation sample's index`

Residuals as a `pandas.Series` carrying the estimation sample's index and the name `resid`, the labelled counterpart of the plain `resid` ndarray. It exists for statsmodels parity without changing the ndarray's positional behaviour, and falls back to a `RangeIndex` when the fit retained no row labels. See `fitted_series` for the matching fitted values.

**Example**

```python
r = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
r.resid_series.head()
```

**Output**

```text
0   -0.245449
1   -0.012850
2   -1.598986
3    0.354059
4    0.682509
Name: resid, dtype: float64
```

### `response_name`

**Stata:** `e(depvar)`

Name of the dependent variable, used for titles and column headings. Some estimators set it as an instance attribute and others do not (ARIMA and GARCH were in the second group), so the property falls back to `e(depvar)` -- a macro every fit carries -- giving the same answer as the formula-based estimators. A subclass that assigns `self.response_name` keeps winning, because the instance dictionary is consulted first.

**Example**

```python
r = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
r.response_name
```

**Output**

```text
y
```

### `stata_cov_params`

**Stata:** `e(V)`

The covariance matrix permuted by the same ordering as `stata_params`, as a numpy array. It surfaces the same refusal as the names for an ambiguous factor term, so a caller cannot receive a matrix whose rows silently disagree with Stata's. Use this rather than `cov` when the comparison target is Stata's `e(V)`.

**Example**

```python
r = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
np.round(r.stata_cov_params, 4)
```

**Output**

```text
[[ 0.0053  0.     -0.0003]
 [ 0.      0.0056 -0.0004]
 [-0.0003 -0.0004  0.0055]]
```

### `stata_factor_terms`

**Stata:** `i.<var> -- the explicit factor expansion in a varlist`

The factor terms whose Stata `e(b)` spelling cannot be reconstructed, as a sorted list; empty when every coefficient has an unambiguous Stata spelling. Stata expands an explicit `i.foreign` into one row per level plus an explicit zero base row (`0b.foreign`), while this package has a single level column, so there is no one-to-one mapping. A bare binary column is NOT reported here, because Stata names its single row after the variable.

**Example**

```python
r = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + C(yb)', base).fit()
r.stata_factor_terms
```

**Output**

```text
['yb']
```

**Notes:** Non-empty here means `stata_param_names`, `stata_params` and `stata_cov_params` all raise `NotImplementedError` on this result; compare with `params`/`bse`, or encode the factor as dummies and refit.

### `stata_param_names`

**Stata:** `the row names of e(b) as Stata spells and orders them`

Coefficient names in Stata's `e(b)` order and spelling: regressors first, then `_cons` LAST, then auxiliary parameters such as `cut1` or `lnalpha`. `Intercept` becomes `_cons`, a bare factor column takes the variable name without the level, `:` becomes `#`, and a leading `/` is dropped. Raises `NotImplementedError` for an explicit `C(...)` factor, where Stata has one row per level plus a zero base row and no one-to-one mapping exists.

**Example**

```python
r = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
(r.param_names, r.stata_param_names)
```

**Output**

```text
(['Intercept', 'x1', 'x2'], ['x1', 'x2', '_cons'])
```

### `stata_params`

**Stata:** `e(b)`

The coefficient vector permuted into Stata's `e(b)` order and spelling: `_cons` last instead of first, factor and interaction names rewritten. It is a property, not a method -- write `r.stata_params`, not `r.stata_params()`. The numbers are identical to `params`, only the order and the labels differ, and this is the view to compare against a Stata fixture.

**Example**

```python
r = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
r.stata_params
```

**Output**

```text
x1       0.840645
x2      -0.512087
_cons    1.005003
Name: params, dtype: float64
```


## Errors and support types

### `WestataError`

**Stata:** `(no Stata equivalent -- an exception base class)`

The base class of every westata-specific failure. Catching it handles the whole family at once: `ConsistencyError`, `ConvergenceError`, `WeightError`, `FormulaError` and `UnsupportedError` all subclass it, so a caller who only wants to separate westata's own refusals from numpy or pandas errors can catch this one name. It does not catch `ValueError` raised for plain bad arguments -- but two of its subclasses, `ConsistencyError` and `FormulaError`, are themselves `ValueError`s, so `except WestataError` does catch every VCE and formula refusal even though generic type errors still escape it.

**Example**

```python
[c.__name__ for c in (ws.ConsistencyError, ws.ConvergenceError, ws.WeightError, ws.FormulaError, ws.UnsupportedError) if issubclass(c, ws.WestataError)]
```

**Output**

```text
['ConsistencyError', 'ConvergenceError', 'WeightError', 'FormulaError', 'UnsupportedError']
```

### `ConsistencyError`

**Stata:** `(no Stata equivalent -- Stata refuses with return codes such as r(498) or r(2000))`

Raised when a result could not be guaranteed Stata-conformant, so the package refuses instead of returning a number it knows may diverge. This is the deliberate-failure channel: a clustered VCE with fewer than two clusters, an unidentified IV model, an `lrtest` between two models with the same parameter count, a `pweight` fit with a nonrobust covariance. The message states what diverged, so the refusal is auditable rather than mysterious. It is ALSO a `ValueError`, deliberately, so `except ValueError` continues to catch it while `except WestataError` now catches the VCE refusals too -- those two bases are what let one user error carry one exception type across OLS, logit and the IV estimators instead of two.

**Example**

```python
msg = None
try:
    r = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1', base).fit(cov_type='cluster', cov_kwds={'groups': np.zeros(len(base), dtype=int)})
except ws.WestataError as exc:
    msg = (type(exc).__name__, isinstance(exc, ValueError))
msg
```

**Output**

```text
('ConsistencyError', True)
```

**Notes:** `cov_kwds` key names are NOT uniform: OLS and the ML estimators take `{'groups': labels}`, while the IV estimators take `{'clusters': labels}` and reject `groups` as an unexpected entry. Pass the key the estimator documents, or the error you get will be about the kwds name rather than about your clusters.

### `ConvergenceError`

**Stata:** `(no Stata equivalent -- Stata reports `convergence not achieved` and returns estimates, r(430))`

Raised when an iterative estimator does not converge and the caller asked for a hard failure via `strict=True`. The default is `strict=False`, which returns the estimates anyway and records `converged=False`, matching Stata's behaviour of reporting non-convergence and keeping the estimates. Pass `strict=True` when an unconverged estimate is worse than no estimate.

**Example**

```python
msg = None
try:
    ws.Logit.from_formula('yb ~ x1 + x2', base).fit(maxiter=1, strict=True)
except ws.ConvergenceError as exc:
    msg = str(exc)
msg
```

**Output**

```text
logit: optimiser did not converge after 1 iterations; estimates may be unreliable
```

### `FormulaError`

**Stata:** `(no Stata equivalent -- Stata raises r(111) for an unknown variable)`

Raised when a model formula cannot be evaluated on the given data: an unknown column, a malformed term, a factor that does not exist. It wraps patsy's own error as a plain exception rather than letting `PatsyError` escape, because patsy keeps a namespace object in the traceback frame and several formatters -- pytest's included -- fail while introspecting it, hiding the real message. Like `ConsistencyError` it subclasses `ValueError` as well as `WestataError`, so generic argument-error handling still catches it.

**Example**

```python
msg = None
try:
    ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ nosuchcolumn', base)
except ws.FormulaError as exc:
    msg = str(exc).splitlines()[0]
msg
```

**Output**

```text
cannot evaluate formula 'y ~ nosuchcolumn': Error evaluating factor: NameError: name 'nosuchcolumn' is not defined
```

### `UnsupportedError`

**Stata:** `(no Stata equivalent -- this means Stata CAN do it and westata cannot yet)`

Raised when a capability exists in Stata but is not implemented here -- the distinction from `ConsistencyError` matters. It also subclasses `NotImplementedError`, so an unknown predict type or an `estat` subcommand Stata supports but this package does not reaches the caller as an explicit gap rather than as a silently wrong number. The message names what was asked for and what IS available.

**Example**

```python
msg = None
try:
    ws.Logit.from_formula('yb ~ x1', base).fit().predict(kind='zzz')
except ws.UnsupportedError as exc:
    msg = str(exc)
msg
```

**Output**

```text
predict: 'logit' supports ('xb', 'stdp', 'pr', 'residuals', 'score'); got 'zzz'
```

### `WeightError`

**Stata:** `(no Stata equivalent -- Stata raises r(402) or r(401) for a bad weight)`

Raised for a missing, unsupported or ambiguous Stata weight specification: an unknown weight type, negative weights, missing weights, weights that sum to zero, or `weights` supplied without a `weight_type` (and vice versa). Weights are validated once, when the model is built, so the error names the offending condition rather than surfacing later as a NaN in the covariance.

**Example**

```python
msg = None
try:
    ws.normalize_weights(np.array([1.0, -1.0]), 'aweight')
except ws.WeightError as exc:
    msg = str(exc)
msg
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

### `ExpListError`

**Stata:** `(no Stata equivalent -- Stata's exp_list parser reports r(198))`

Raised when text does not conform to Stata's `exp_list` grammar, or when a statistic names a stored result that does not exist. It subclasses `ValueError`. The two failure modes are kept apart in the message: a syntax error quotes the offending text and offset, while an unresolvable statistic lists the keys actually available, which is what makes `r(mean)` after a command that stores only `r(N)` diagnosable.

**Example**

```python
msg = None
try:
    ws.parse_exp_list('(((')
except ws.ExpListError as exc:
    msg = str(exc)
msg
```

**Output**

```text
unbalanced parentheses in '((('
```

### `PackageNotFoundError`

**Stata:** `(no Stata equivalent -- an importlib.metadata exception, re-exported)`

The `importlib.metadata` exception raised when a distribution's metadata cannot be found. It is re-exported so callers can catch it without importing `importlib.metadata` themselves, and it is what `westata.__version__` falls back from: running the source tree without installing leaves no distribution metadata, and the version then becomes the literal fallback rather than raising. It is NOT a `WestataError` -- it is an `ImportError` subclass from the standard library.

**Example**

```python
(ws.PackageNotFoundError.__module__, issubclass(ws.PackageNotFoundError, ImportError))
```

**Output**

```text
('importlib.metadata', True)
```

**Notes:** Because it is `importlib.metadata`'s class and not a westata error, `except ws.WestataError` does NOT catch it. Catch `ws.PackageNotFoundError` or `ImportError` directly.

### `StoredResults`

**Stata:** `r(), e(), _b[] and _se[] -- the four tables a replicate leaves behind`

The stored results of ONE replicate's command, as a frozen dataclass with four mapping fields: `r` and `e` hold scalars, `b` and `se` hold coefficients by name. These are exactly the four tables Stata's `exp_list` can reach, which is why a bootstrap or permutation statistic is written against this object. `lookup(table, key)` returns a float and raises `ExpListError` naming the available keys when the entry is absent.

**Signature**

```python
ws.StoredResults(r: 'Mapping[str, float]' = <factory>, e: 'Mapping[str, float]' = <factory>, b: 'Mapping[str, float]' = <factory>, se: 'Mapping[str, float]' = <factory>) -> None
```

**Example**

```python
s = ws.StoredResults(r={'mean': 1.5}, e={'N': 200.0})
ws.parse_exp_list('(n=e(N)) (m=r(mean))').evaluate(s)
```

**Output**

```text
{'n': 200.0, 'm': 1.5}
```

### `WeightSpec`

**Stata:** `[fw=], [aw=], [pw=], [iw=] -- the parsed weight specification`

The normalized weights plus the metadata the estimators need, produced by `normalize_weights` from a weight array and a Stata weight type. Fields: `weights` (already normalized per that type's rule), `weight_type`, `effective_n` (the sample size used for degrees of freedom), `implies_robust` (true for `pweight`, because Stata's `[pw=w]` implies `vce(robust)`), and `n_obs` (the row count, not the effective size). A frozen dataclass, so an estimator cannot quietly rewrite the specification after validation.

**Signature**

```python
ws.WeightSpec(weights: 'np.ndarray', weight_type: 'str', effective_n: 'float', implies_robust: 'bool', n_obs: 'int', dropped: 'int' = 0, kept: 'np.ndarray | None' = None) -> None
```

**Example**

```python
ws.normalize_weights(np.array([1.0, 2.0, 3.0, 4.0]), 'pweight')
```

**Output**

```text
WeightSpec(weights=array([0.4, 0.8, 1.2, 1.6]), weight_type='pweight', effective_n=4.0, implies_robust=True, n_obs=4, dropped=0, kept=None)
```

### `WEIGHT_TYPES`

**Stata:** `[fw=], [aw=], [pw=], [iw=]`

The four Stata weight type names accepted everywhere a weight is accepted, as a tuple: `fweight`, `aweight`, `pweight`, `iweight`. The meanings are the ones in `[U] 20.24`: frequency (integer replication counts, the sample is effectively `sum(w)`), analytic or precision (rescaled to sum to `N`, used like a frequency weight for the estimate), sampling (point estimate as with `aweight`, but the variance MUST be a linearized sandwich -- `[pw=w]` implies `vce(robust)`), and importance (used like a frequency weight without normalization). The part readers get wrong is that `fweight` and `aweight` give the same point estimate and differ only in the degrees of freedom, while `pweight` is the one that changes the VARIANCE.

**Example**

```python
ws.WEIGHT_TYPES
```

**Output**

```text
('fweight', 'aweight', 'pweight', 'iweight')
```

### `SSC`

**Stata:** `the `, ssc(...)` style choices behind vce()'s small-sample corrections`

The Stata-style small-sample correction settings, as a frozen dataclass: `adj` applies the `N/(N-K)` degrees-of-freedom correction, `cluster_adj` applies the `G/(G-1)` cluster correction on top of `adj`, and `df` selects which convention the t/z degrees of freedom follow (`"stata"` is the default and follows the corresponding Stata command's own default). Every field defaults to the Stata choice, so an estimator that accepts `ssc=` behaves like Stata until the caller says otherwise.

**Signature**

```python
ws.SSC(adj: 'bool' = True, cluster_adj: 'bool' = True, df: 'str' = 'stata') -> None
```

**Example**

```python
ws.SSC(adj=False)
```

**Output**

```text
SSC(adj=False, cluster_adj=True, df='stata')
```

### `VceMatrix`

**Stata:** `estat vce / estat vce, correlation`

A printed coefficient covariance (or correlation) matrix. Three fields: `names` (row and column labels in Stata's spelling), `values` (the full symmetric matrix as a DataFrame -- the machine-readable surface), and `kind` (`"covariance"` or `"correlation"`). `str(...)` renders the LOWER TRIANGLE the way Stata prints it, and `to_frame()` returns the whole symmetric matrix. Row order follows the coefficient table, with `_cons` last.

**Signature**

```python
ws.VceMatrix(names: 'Sequence[str]', values: 'np.ndarray', kind: 'str', model_label: 'str' = '', response_label: 'str' = '') -> 'None'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.estat_vce(ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit())
```

**Output**

```text

Covariance matrix of coefficients of regress model

        e(V) |         x1          x2       _cons 
-------------+-----------------------------------
          x1 |  .00525206                         
          x2 |  .00002056   .00559838             
       _cons | -.00030246   -.0003569    .0055396
```

**Notes:** For `kind == "correlation"` the diagonal is blanked to 0 exactly as Stata prints it -- a variable is not "correlated with itself" in that table. Read `values` for the real diagonal, which is 1.

### `ParsedExpressionList`

**Stata:** `(the parsed form of an exp_list -- what Stata's parser builds internally)`

A parsed Stata `exp_list`, ready to evaluate against one replicate's stored results. It holds `groups`, a tuple of statistic groups (a labelled group keeps its label), and exposes `names` (the flattened statistic names, prefixed with `label:` for a labelled group) and `evaluate(results)`, which maps each name to a float by resolving `r()`, `e()`, `_b[]` and `_se[]` lookups in a `StoredResults`. It is a frozen dataclass: parsing produces a value, not a mutable builder.

**Signature**

```python
ws.ParsedExpressionList(groups: 'tuple[StatisticGroup, ...]') -> None
```

**Example**

```python
ws.parse_exp_list('(mean=r(mean)) (sd=r(sd))').names
```

**Output**

```text
['mean', 'sd']
```

### `WaldTest`

**Stata:** `test x1 = 0`

One Wald test of the linear hypothesis `Rb = r`, rendered the way Stata prints it. Fields: `statistic` (`W` for chi-square inference, `W/q` for F), `df` (the numerator `q`), `df_denom` (the denominator for the F form, `None` for chi-square), `pvalue`, `distribution` (`"F"` or `"chi2"`), and `constraint` (a human-readable statement of what was tested, printed above the statistic). Whether the F or the chi-square form is used follows the model's `dist`, so a logit fit yields chi-square and an OLS fit yields F.

**Signature**

```python
ws.WaldTest(statistic: 'float', df: 'float', df_denom: 'float | None', pvalue: 'float', distribution: 'str', constraint: 'str') -> None
```

**Example**

```python
r = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
r.test('x1 = 0')
```

**Output**

```text

 ( 1)  x1 = 0

       F(  1,   197) =  134.55
            Prob > F =    0.0000
```

**Notes:** `to_dict()` gives Stata's `r()` names: the statistic travels under the name of its OWN distribution (`F` or `chi2`), so reading `r(F)` after a chi-square test finds nothing -- which is correct, because Stata stores `r(chi2)` there.

### `LincomResult`

**Stata:** `lincom x1 - x2`

One linear combination of parameters, shaped like Stata's `lincom` output. Fields: `estimate`, `std_err`, `statistic`, `pvalue`, `ci_lower`, `ci_upper`, `distribution` (`"t"` or `"z"`), `df` (`None` for a z-based fit, which genuinely has no degrees of freedom), `expression` (the hypothesis as written), plus `eform` and `label`. The standard error comes from the delta method on `weights @ b`, using the same covariance the coefficient table used.

**Signature**

```python
ws.LincomResult(estimate: 'float', std_err: 'float', statistic: 'float', pvalue: 'float', ci_lower: 'float', ci_upper: 'float', distribution: 'str', df: 'float | None', expression: 'str', eform: 'bool' = False, label: 'str' = '', response_name: 'str' = '') -> None
```

**Example**

```python
r = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
r.lincom('x1 - x2')
```

**Output**

```text

 ( 1)  x1 - x2 = 0

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
           y | Coefficient  Std. err.      t    P>|t|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
         (1) |   1.352732   .1039679    13.01   0.000     1.147699    1.557764
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
```

**Notes:** `to_dict()` omits `df` for a z-based fit rather than reporting 0 or None, because Stata does not store `r(df)` in that case; the statistic appears as `t` or `z` accordingly.

### `IcResult`

**Stata:** `estat ic`

The `estat ic` table for one fitted model. Fields: `n`, `ll_null`, `ll_model`, `df` (the first four columns, matching Stata's `r(S)` layout), the four criteria `aic`, `bic`, `aicc`, `caic`, and `model` (the command name in the `Model` column). A missing statistic prints as `.` rather than `nan`, following Stata's rendering. This is the right surface for `regress`, which stores no information criteria of its own.

**Signature**

```python
ws.IcResult(*, n: 'float', ll_null: 'float', ll_model: 'float', df: 'float', aic: 'float', bic: 'float', aicc: 'float', caic: 'float', model: 'str' = '') -> 'None'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.estat_ic(ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit())
```

**Output**

```text

Akaike's information criterion and Bayesian information criterion

-----------------------------------------------------------------------------
       Model |          N   ll(null)  ll(model)      df        AIC        BIC
-------------+---------------------------------------------------------------
           . |        200  -357.2327  -291.7997       3   589.5994   599.4944
-----------------------------------------------------------------------------
Note: BIC uses N = number of observations. See [R] IC note.
```

### `VifResult`

**Stata:** `estat vif`

The variance inflation factor table for a linear fit. Fields: `names` (regressors in the reported order, sorted by descending VIF unless `nosort=True`), `vif` and `tolerance` (its reciprocal) as arrays, and `mean_vif`. `to_frame()` returns the table as a DataFrame and `to_dict()` gives Stata's `r(vif_#)` scalars keyed by variable name. Only defined for the linear family: the auxiliary regressions are ordinary least squares by construction.

**Signature**

```python
ws.VifResult(names: 'Sequence[str]', vif: 'Sequence[float]', mean_vif: 'float') -> 'None'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.estat_vif(ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit())
```

**Output**

```text

    Variable |       VIF       1/VIF  
-------------+----------------------
          x1 |      1.00    0.999986
          x2 |      1.00    0.999986
-------------+----------------------
    Mean VIF |      1.00
```

### `MarginsResult`

**Stata:** `margins, dydx(x1)`

One row of a `margins` table plus its covariance. Fields: `label` (the row label), `estimate`, `std_err`, `statistic`, `pvalue`, `ci_lower`, `ci_upper` and `prediction` (the prediction type used). The standard error is a delta-method one, with an analytic gradient where the response family has a closed form and an adaptive numeric one otherwise. `to_dict()` flattens to the row labels of Stata's `r(table)`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.MarginsResult(label: 'str', estimate: 'float', std_err: 'float', statistic: 'float', pvalue: 'float', ci_lower: 'float', ci_upper: 'float', prediction: 'str' = '', title: 'str' = '', nobs: 'float' = nan, vce_label: 'str' = '', expression: 'str' = '', variables: 'tuple[str, ...]' = (), column_label: 'str' = '', stat_label: 'str' = 'z', at_means: 'tuple[tuple[str, float], ...]' = ()) -> None
```

**Example**

```python
ws.margins(ws.Logit.from_formula('yb ~ x1 + x2', base).fit(), dydx='x1')
```

**Output**

```text

Average marginal effects                                   Number of obs = 200
Model VCE: OIM

Expression: Pr(yb), predict()
dy/dx wrt:  x1

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
             |            Delta-method
             |      dy/dx   std. err.      z    P>|z|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
          x1 |   .1226231   .0311214     3.94   0.000     .0616263    .1836198
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
```

**Notes:** `to_dict()` labels the statistic row `t` unconditionally, because this object does not carry the distribution flag; Stata would label it `z` after a logit. Use `str(...)` when the label itself matters.

### `GofResult`

**Stata:** `estat gof (after poisson)`

The Poisson goodness-of-fit table: a deviance (likelihood-ratio) test and a Pearson test, each with its upper-tail chi-square p-value. Fields: `deviance`, `deviance_pvalue`, `pearson`, `pearson_pvalue`, `df` (`e(N) - e(df_m) - 1`, shared by both). Stata defines `estat gof` ONLY for `poisson`; `nbreg` sets no `e(estat_cmd)`, so requesting it there raises `NotImplementedError` rather than returning an invented statistic.

**Signature**

```python
ws.GofResult(*, deviance: 'float', deviance_pvalue: 'float', pearson: 'float', pearson_pvalue: 'float', df: 'float') -> 'None'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.estat_gof(ws.Poisson.from_formula('ycnt ~ x1', base).fit())
```

**Output**

```text

Deviance goodness-of-fit           =   227.201
Prob > chi2(198)                   =    0.0758

Pearson goodness-of-fit            =   187.551
Prob > chi2(198)                   =    0.6918
```

### `ClassificationResult`

**Stata:** `estat classification`

The `estat classification` 2x2 table and its nine percentages. Fields: `table` (3x3 DataFrame with rows `+`/`-`/`Total` and columns `D`/`~D`/`Total`), `cutoff` (the probability threshold), and the rates Stata returns as `r(P_*)`: `percent_correct`, `sensitivity`, `specificity`, `false_positive_rate`, `false_negative_rate`, `positive_predictive_value`, `negative_predictive_value`, `false_positive_classified`, `false_negative_classified`. Note that the two "false" families are conditioned differently -- on the true class versus on the classified class.

**Signature**

```python
ws.ClassificationResult(*, table: 'pd.DataFrame', cutoff: 'float', percent_correct: 'float', sensitivity: 'float', specificity: 'float', false_positive_rate: 'float', false_negative_rate: 'float', positive_predictive_value: 'float', negative_predictive_value: 'float', false_positive_classified: 'float', false_negative_classified: 'float', model_label: 'str' = '', depvar: 'str' = '') -> 'None'
```

**Example**

```python
r = ws.estat_classification(ws.Logit.from_formula('yb ~ x1 + x2', base).fit())
(r.cutoff, r.percent_correct)
```

**Output**

```text
(0.5, 62.0)
```

### `HetTestResult`

**Stata:** `estat hettest`

The Breusch-Pagan / Cook-Weisberg test for heteroskedasticity. Fields: `method` (`"normal"`, `"iid"` or `"fstat"`), `statistic`, `df`, `pvalue`, `df_denom` (only for `method="fstat"`), and `variables` (the regressors the auxiliary regression used -- empty means the fitted values were used, which is Stata's default when no varlist is given). Like Stata, it refuses after a robust or clustered VCE and on pweighted data.

**Signature**

```python
ws.HetTestResult(*, method: 'str', statistic: 'float', df: 'float', pvalue: 'float', df_denom: 'float | None' = None, variables: 'Sequence[str]' = (), label: 'str' = '', plural: 'bool' = False) -> 'None'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.estat_hettest(ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit())
```

**Output**

```text

Breusch–Pagan/Cook–Weisberg test for heteroskedasticity 
Assumption: Normal error terms
Variable: Fitted values of y

H0: Constant variance

    chi2(1) =   0.27
Prob > chi2 = 0.6006
```

### `ImTestResult`

**Stata:** `estat imtest`

Cameron & Trivedi's information-matrix test decomposition, optionally with White's original test. Fields: `heteroskedasticity`, `skewness`, `kurtosis` and their degrees of freedom `df_heteroskedasticity`, `df_skewness`, `df_kurtosis`; `total` and `df_total` (the sum of the three); and `white`, `white_df`, `white_pvalue`, which are `None` unless `white=True` was requested.

**Signature**

```python
ws.ImTestResult(*, heteroskedasticity: 'float', skewness: 'float', kurtosis: 'float', df_heteroskedasticity: 'float', df_skewness: 'float', df_kurtosis: 'float', white: 'float | None' = None, white_df: 'float | None' = None) -> 'None'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.estat_imtest(ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit())
```

**Output**

```text

Cameron & Trivedi's decomposition of IM-test

--------------------------------------------------
              Source |       chi2     df         p
---------------------+----------------------------
  Heteroskedasticity |       0.85      5    0.9737
            Skewness |       1.19      2    0.5526
            Kurtosis |       2.04      1    0.1535
---------------------+----------------------------
               Total |       4.07      8    0.8503
--------------------------------------------------
```

### `OvTestResult`

**Stata:** `estat ovtest`

Ramsey's RESET test for omitted variables. Fields: `statistic` (the F statistic), `df` and `df_denom` (numerator and denominator degrees of freedom), `pvalue`, and `omitted` (a human-readable description of what the powers were taken of, matching Stata's `Omitted:` line). The default path is Stata's `freset`, which it takes whenever `rhs` is not given.

**Signature**

```python
ws.OvTestResult(*, statistic: 'float', df: 'float', df_denom: 'float', pvalue: 'float', omitted: 'str', dropped: 'Sequence[str]' = ()) -> 'None'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.estat_ovtest(ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit())
```

**Output**

```text

Ramsey RESET test for omitted variables
Omitted: Powers of fitted values of y

H0: Model has no omitted variables

F(3, 194) =   0.58
 Prob > F = 0.6277
```

### `EndogenousResult`

**Stata:** `estat endogenous`

Durbin and Wu endogeneity tests for an IV fit. Fields: `durbin`, `durbin_pvalue` (Durbin's chi-square, with `df` equal to the number of tested endogenous regressors), `wu`, `wu_pvalue`, `wu_df`, `wu_df_denom` (Wu's F and its two degrees of freedom), and `tested` (the names of the endogenous regressors covered). A rejection means the regressor is endogenous, so OLS on the same equation would be inconsistent.

**Signature**

```python
ws.EndogenousResult(*, durbin: 'float', durbin_pvalue: 'float', wu: 'float', wu_pvalue: 'float', wu_df: 'float', wu_df_denom: 'float', tested) -> 'None'
```

**Example**

```python
iv = ws.IV2SLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + [x2 ~ x3]', base).fit()
ws.estat_endogenous(iv)
```

**Output**

```text

  Tests of endogeneity
  H0: Variables are exogenous

  Durbin (score) chi2(1)          =  1.64391  (p = 0.1998)
  Wu-Hausman F(1,196)             =  1.62438  (p = 0.2040)
```

### `FirstStageResult`

**Stata:** `estat firststage`

First-stage diagnostics for an IV fit: one row per endogenous regressor plus the headline F. Fields: `endog` (names in reported order), `r2` and `adj_r2` (the endogenous regressor on ALL exogenous variables), `partial_r2` (the residualised endogenous regressor on the residualised excluded instruments), `f`, `df_num`, `df_denom`, `pvalue` (the joint test of the excluded instruments), and `shea`, `shea_adj` (only computed with more than one endogenous regressor, because with one they coincide with `partial_r2`).

**Signature**

```python
ws.FirstStageResult(*, endog: 'Sequence[str]', r2: 'Sequence[float]', adj_r2: 'Sequence[float]', partial_r2: 'Sequence[float]', f: 'Sequence[float]', df_num: 'Sequence[float]', df_denom: 'Sequence[float]', pvalue: 'Sequence[float]', shea: 'Sequence[float] | None' = None, shea_adj: 'Sequence[float] | None' = None, mineig: 'float | None' = None, mineigcv: 'Any' = None, show_all: 'bool' = False, force_nonrobust: 'bool' = False, robust_vce: 'bool' = False) -> 'None'
```

**Example**

```python
iv = ws.IV2SLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + [x2 ~ x3]', base).fit()
ws.estat_firststage(iv)
```

**Output**

```text

  First-stage regression summary statistics
  --------------------------------------------------------------------------
               |            Adjusted      Partial
      Variable |   R-sq.       R-sq.        R-sq.      F(1,197)   Prob > F
  -------------+------------------------------------------------------------
            x2 |  0.0008     -0.0093       0.0008      0.159849    0.6897
  --------------------------------------------------------------------------


  Minimum eigenvalue statistic = 0.159849    

  Critical Values                      # of endogenous regressors:    1
  H0: Instruments are weak             # of excluded instruments:     1
  ---------------------------------------------------------------------
                                     |    5%     10%     20%     30%
  2SLS relative bias                 |         (not available)
  -----------------------------------+---------------------------------
                                     |   10%     15%     20%     25%
  2SLS size of nominal 5% Wald test  |  16.38    8.96    6.66    5.53
  LIML size of nominal 5% Wald test  |  16.38    8.96    6.66    5.53
  ---------------------------------------------------------------------
```

### `OverIdResult`

**Stata:** `estat overid`

The overidentification restrictions test for an IV fit. Fields: `statistic`, `df` (the number of overidentifying restrictions) and `pvalue`; `basmann` and `basmann_pvalue`, the finite-sample variant `Sargan*(N-k)/(N-Sargan)`, which is `None` for GMM; and `test`, either `"Sargan"` (2SLS and LIML) or `"Hansen J"` (GMM). An exactly identified model has no restriction to test and is refused rather than reported as a statistic of zero.

**Signature**

```python
ws.OverIdResult(*, statistic: 'float', df: 'float', pvalue: 'float', test: 'str', basmann: 'float | None' = None, basmann_pvalue: 'float | None' = None) -> 'None'
```

**Example**

```python
iv = ws.IV2SLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + [x2 ~ x3 + y1]', base).fit()
ws.estat_overid(iv)
```

**Output**

```text

  Tests of overidentifying restrictions:

  Sargan (score) chi2(1) = .00297725  (p = 0.9565)
  Basmann chi2(1)        = .00291775  (p = 0.9569)
```

### `LRTestResult`

**Stata:** `lrtest unrestricted restricted`

A likelihood-ratio test between a restricted and an unrestricted model. Fields: `statistic` (`2 (lnL_unrestricted - lnL_restricted)`), `df` (the difference in parameter counts, always positive because two models with equal counts are refused as Stata's `r(498)`), `pvalue` (`P > chi2(df)`, exactly 1.0 for a negative statistic as Stata prints), and `ll_unrestricted`, `ll_restricted`, kept so the number can be audited without re-fitting.

**Signature**

```python
ws.LRTestResult(statistic: 'float', df: 'float', pvalue: 'float', ll_unrestricted: 'float', ll_restricted: 'float', restricted_name: 'str' = '', unrestricted_name: 'str' = '') -> None
```

**Example**

```python
u = ws.Logit.from_formula('yb ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
r = ws.Logit.from_formula('yb ~ x1', base).fit()
u.lrtest(r)
```

**Output**

```text
Likelihood-ratio test
Assumption: logit nested within logit
LR chi2(1) = 0.79
Prob > chi2 = 0.3731
```

### `HausmanResult`

**Stata:** `hausman fe re`

The Hausman specification test: `(b_c - b_e)' [V_c - V_e]^-1 (b_c - b_e)`. Fields: `statistic`, `df` (the number of coefficients actually compared, constants excluded), `pvalue` (`P > chi2(df)`), and `excluded` (parameters not present in both models, so not comparable -- a diagnostic this package adds, not an `r()` scalar). A rejection means the efficient estimator is inconsistent, so the consistent one is preferred.

**Signature**

```python
ws.HausmanResult(statistic: 'float', df: 'float', pvalue: 'float', excluded: 'list[str]' = <factory>, names: 'list[str]' = <factory>, consistent: 'np.ndarray | None' = None, efficient: 'np.ndarray | None' = None, standard_errors: 'np.ndarray | None' = None, consistent_label: 'str' = '', efficient_label: 'str' = '', consistent_command: 'str' = '', efficient_command: 'str' = '', tested_count: 'int | None' = None, not_positive_definite: 'bool' = False) -> None
```

**Example**

```python
fe = ws.PanelOLS.from_formula('ypanel ~ x1', base, entity='firm', time='year').fit()
re = ws.RandomEffects.from_formula('ypanel ~ x1', base, entity='firm', time='year').fit()
fe.hausman(re)
```

**Output**

```text

                 ---- Coefficients ----
             |      (b)          (B)            (b-B)     sqrt(diag(V_b-V_B))
             |       fe           re         Difference       Std. err.
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
          x1 |     .709774     .7287794       -.0190054        .0157648
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
                          b = Consistent under H0 and Ha; obtained from xtreg.
           B = Inconsistent under Ha, efficient under H0; obtained from xtreg.

Test of H0: Difference in coefficients not systematic

    chi2(1) = (b-B)'[(V_b-V_B)^(-1)](b-B)
            =   1.45
Prob > chi2 = 0.2280
```

**Notes:** The difference matrix `V_c - V_e` can be singular or have negative eigenvalues in finite samples; the test compares only the coefficients present in both models, which is what `df` counts.


## Other names

### `Areg`

**Stata:** `areg y x1 x2, absorb(firm)`

Linear regression absorbing categorical indicators -- Stata's `areg`. This is a DIFFERENT command from `reghdfe`, not a synonym: `absorb=` is required, the banner reads "Linear regression, absorbing indicators" with a second line naming the absorbed variable, `e(predict)` is `areg_p`, and -- the substantive part -- it REPORTS an intercept. `reghdfe` reports slopes only, so `Areg` appends `_cons`, the group-size-weighted mean of the absorbed effects. Estimation is the same within transform, so coefficients match `FEOLS`; the identity, the constant and the small-sample convention are what differ. `fit` accepts `level=` for the printed confidence intervals and understands `vce(hc2)`/`vce(hc3)` because it builds the full dummy design and can form the leverages `FEOLS` cannot reach.

**Signature**

```python
ws.Areg(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, absorb: 'Any' = None, weights: 'Any' = None, weight_type: 'str | None' = None, absorb_test: 'bool' = True, noabsorb: 'bool | None' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`Areg.from_formula`**

```python
Areg.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, absorb: 'Any' = None, weights: 'Any' = None, weight_type: 'str | None' = None, subset: 'Any' = None, absorb_test: 'bool' = True, noabsorb: 'bool | None' = None) -> "'Areg'"
```

**`Areg.fit`**

```python
Areg.fit(self, cov_type: 'str | None' = None, cov_kwds: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, ssc: 'SSC | None' = None, level: 'float | None' = None) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.Areg.from_formula('y ~ x1', base, absorb='firm').fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text
Linear regression, absorbing indicators            Number of obs     =     200
Absorbed variable: firm                            No. of categories =      40
                                                   F(1, 159)         =   86.12
                                                   Prob > F          =  0.0000
                                                   R-squared         =  0.4928
                                                   Adj R-squared     =  0.3652
                                                   Root MSE          =  1.1531

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
           y | Coefficient  Std. err.      t    P>|t|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
          x1 |   .8095565   .0872367     9.28   0.000     .6372645    .9818486
       _cons |   .9742472   .0816927    11.93   0.000     .8129043     1.13559
F test of absorbed indicators: F(39, 159) = 1.095       Prob > F = 0.340
```

**Notes:** `absorb=` is required -- without it this is just `regress`. `_cons` is the group-size-weighted average of the absorbed effects; an FE model does not identify a constant separately from them, so only a normalisation fixes it and this is areg's. The clustered covariance uses `K = k_reported + (k_absorb - 1)` in the CR1 factor, whereas `reghdfe` uses `k_reported`: the absorbed levels consume degrees of freedom in areg's convention. That difference is NOT uniform across cluster/absorb pairings, so the correction is applied only when `e(rank)` shows the dummies are not already counted. With multiway clustering the two commands' factors differ in structure rather than by a parameter count, so the covariance is rebuilt on the full design instead of scaled. `noabsorb=True` skips the absorbed-indicator F test (Stata's `noabsorbtest`), leaving `e(F_absorb)`/`e(p_absorb)` absent.

### `AucResult`

**Stata:** `estat auc after logit/probit`

The object `estat_auc` returns: the area under the ROC curve (`area`) and the number of observations it was computed on (`n`). Read the numbers with `to_dict()`; `str()` is not defined because Stata prints a one-line table rather than a block.

**Signature**

```python
ws.AucResult(*, area: 'float', n: 'int') -> 'None'
```

**Example**

```python
r = ws.estat_auc(ws.Logit.from_formula('yb ~ x1', base).fit())
print(round(r.area, 6))
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

### `BgTestResult`

**Stata:** `estat bgodfrey`

The object `estat_bgodfrey` returns, one row per tested lag order: `to_frame()` gives the lag, the chi-square statistic and its p-value for each row.

**Signature**

```python
ws.BgTestResult(rows: 'tuple[BgTestRow, ...]', nobs: 'int', n_gaps: 'int' = 0) -> None
```

**Example**

```python
r = ws.estat_bgodfrey(ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1', base).fit(), lags=[1, 2])
print(r.to_frame())
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

### `ContrastResult`

**Stata:** `contrast`

A list of `LincomResult` rows (one per comparison) that also carries the joint Wald test Stata prints in the ANOVA block: `term`, `fvalue`, `df`, `df_denom` and `pvalue`. `to_frame()` gives the per-comparison table.

**Signature**

```python
ws.ContrastResult(rows, *, term, fvalue, df, pvalue, df_denom)
```

**Example**

```python
fit = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + C(firm)', base.assign(firm=base.firm % 3)).fit()
r = ws.contrast(fit, 'firm')
print(len(r))
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

### `ContrastResults`

**Stata:** `statsmodels-style contrast result`

The statsmodels-shaped contrast object the statsmodels compatibility helpers build. It is NOT one of this package's estimation results: it carries `statistic`, `pvalue`, `df_num`, `df_resid`, `hypotheses` and `summary()`, and deliberately exposes no `params`/`bse` vector.

**Signature**

```python
ws.ContrastResults(*, statistic: 'float', pvalue: 'float', df_num: 'float', df_resid: 'float', use_t: 'bool', hypotheses: 'Sequence[str]', estimate: 'float', standard_error: 'float', param_names: 'Sequence[str]', distribution: 'str') -> 'None'
```

**Example**

```python
from westata.results.ecosystem import build_contrast
fit = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1', base).fit()
c = build_contrast(fit, 'x1 = 0')
print(round(c.pvalue, 6))
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

The object `estat_corrgram` returns: one row per lag with the autocorrelation, partial autocorrelation and their Q statistics. `to_frame()` gives the table; `to_dict()` gives one dict per row.

### `MarginsTable`

**Stata:** `margins (table)`

The multi-row container `margins` returns when the call produces more than one row. It is a `list` subclass, so it iterates and indexes like a list of `MarginsResult`; its `__str__` renders Stata's single table with one heading, one rule set and the rows beneath. A call that yields exactly one row returns a bare `MarginsResult` instead.

**Signature**

```python
ws.MarginsTable(iterable=(), /)
```

**Example**

```python
print(ws.margins(ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit(), dydx=['x1','x2']))
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

**Signature**

```python
ws.MoranResult(statistic: 'float', df: 'float', pvalue: 'float', moran_i: 'float', expected_i: 'float', z: 'float', errorlags: 'int' = 1) -> None
```

### `MultinomialLogit`

**Stata:** `mlogit depvar indepvars, base(#)`

Multinomial logistic regression. Stata stores one block of coefficients per outcome level and leaves the BASE outcome's block as zeros, still printing it; statsmodels estimates only `levels - 1` blocks, so the base row is inserted here rather than dropped -- dropping it shifts every later coefficient by a full block and still looks plausible. A binary outcome is refused with a pointer to `Logit`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.MultinomialLogit(endog: 'Any', exog: 'Any', *, param_names: 'list[str] | None' = None, freq_weights: 'Any' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`MultinomialLogit.from_formula`**

```python
MultinomialLogit.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, subset: 'Any' = None, freq_weights: 'str | None' = None) -> "'_DiscreteT'"
```

**`MultinomialLogit.fit`**

```python
MultinomialLogit.fit(self, cov_type: 'str | None' = None, cov_kwds: 'Any' = None, *, maxiter: 'int' = 200, strict: 'bool' = False) -> 'Any'
```

**Example**

```python
lv = (base['ycnt'] > base['ycnt'].median()).astype(int) + (base['ycnt'] > base['ycnt'].quantile(0.75)).astype(int)
f = ws.MultinomialLogit(lv.to_numpy(), np.column_stack([base['x1'], base['x2'], np.ones(len(base))]), param_names=['x1','x2','_cons']).fit()
np.round(f.e['ll'], 4)
```

**Output**

```text
-181.4397
```

**Stata:** `estat mundlak`

The Mundlak specification test result: which terms were tested, the chi-square statistic, its degrees of freedom and the p-value. A rejection means the random-effects estimator is inconsistent.

**Signature**

```python
ws.MundlakResult(statistic: 'float', df: 'float', pvalue: 'float', tested: 'list[str]') -> None
```

**Example**

```python

```

**Output**

```text
None
```

### `Newey`

**Stata:** `newey depvar indepvars, lag(#) [noconstant] [aw=...]`

Class form of `ws.newey` (R2.7): an OLS fit whose VCE is the Newey-West HAC sum. `lag` is a required MODEL option, so it belongs to construction, not to `fit()`. `fit()` takes no `cov_type` -- Stata's `newey` has no `vce()` option, the HAC variance IS the estimator. Use `Newey.from_formula(formula, data, lag=...)` for the standard argument order, or `Newey(data, formula, lag=...)` to match the function's positional order. `ws.newey(...)` remains as a thin wrapper over the same implementation.

**Signature**

```python
ws.Newey(data: 'pd.DataFrame', formula: 'str', *, lag: 'int', time: 'Any' = None, force: 'bool' = False, noconstant: 'bool' = False, weights: 'Any' = None, weight_type: 'str | None' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`Newey.from_formula`**

```python
Newey.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, lag: 'int', time: 'Any' = None, force: 'bool' = False, noconstant: 'bool' = False, weights: 'Any' = None, weight_type: 'str | None' = None) -> "'Newey'"
```

**`Newey.fit`**

```python
Newey.fit(self, *, lag: 'int | None' = None) -> 'NeweyWestResult'
```

**Example**

```python
r = ws.Newey.from_formula('y ~ x1', base, lag=2).fit()
print(r.cov_type, round(float(r.params[0]), 6))
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

**Stata:** `wntestq`

The portmanteau (Ljung-Box / Box-Pierce) result: the Q statistic, its degrees of freedom and p-value, for the lag order that was used.

**Signature**


### `Prais`

**Stata:** `prais depvar indepvars [, corc]`

Class form of `ws.prais` (R2.7): the Prais-Winsten / Cochrane-Orcutt AR(1) estimator. `corc` selects the Cochrane-Orcutt transform (drops the first observation instead of scaling it by sqrt(1-rho^2)) and is a MODEL option, so it belongs to construction. `fit()` takes no `cov_type` -- Stata's `prais` has no `vce()` option. NOTE THE ORDER: `Prais(data, formula)` keeps the function's positional order, which is the reverse of every other `from_formula`; use `Prais.from_formula(formula, data)` for the standard order.

**Signature**

```python
ws.Prais(data: 'pd.DataFrame', formula: 'str', *, maxiter: 'int' = 100, tolerance: 'float' = 1e-06, corc: 'bool' = False) -> 'None'
```

**`Prais.from_formula`**

```python
Prais.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, maxiter: 'int' = 100, tolerance: 'float' = 1e-06, corc: 'bool' = False) -> "'Prais'"
```

**`Prais.fit`**

```python
Prais.fit(self) -> 'PraisResult'
```

**Example**

```python
r = ws.Prais.from_formula('y ~ x1', base).fit()
print(round(float(r.rho), 6), round(float(r.params[0]), 6))
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

### `PraisResult`

**Stata:** `prais depvar indepvars`

Result of the Prais-Winsten AR(1) estimator. It exposes params/bse/pvalues/tvalues, rmse, both R-squareds, the Durbin-Watson statistics before and after the transformation, and records the estimated autocorrelation `rho`, the iteration count and whether the iteration converged, because the estimator is iterative and `maxiter`/`tolerance` control it.

**Signature**

```python
ws.PraisResult(**values: 'object') -> 'None'
```

**Example**

```python
r = ws.prais(base, 'y ~ x1')
print(round(r.rho, 6))
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

```text
None
```
