### `PwCompareTable`

**Stata:** `pwcompare`

A list of `LincomResult` rows, one per pairwise comparison, that also renders Stata's whole pairwise table. `to_frame()` gives one row per comparison.

**Signature**

```python
ws.PwCompareTable(rows: 'Sequence[LincomResult]', *, term: 'str' = '', margin_label: 'str' = 'asbalanced', comparison: 'str' = 'Unadjusted') -> 'None'
```

**Example**

```python
fit = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + C(firm)', base.assign(firm=base.firm % 3)).fit()
r = ws.pwcompare(fit, 'firm')
print(len(r))
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

### `RandomEffectsMLE`

**Stata:** `xtreg ypanel x1 x2, mle`

Random-effects regression by MAXIMUM LIKELIHOOD (Stata `xtreg, mle`). Unlike `RandomEffects`, which is the GLS `xtreg, re` that plugs in Swamy-Arora variance components, this maximises the same random-effects likelihood over the coefficients AND the two variance components at once, so `sigma_u` differs materially. Fitted with statsmodels' `MixedLM` using ML (not REML) and a Powell optimiser; standard errors come from the inverse of the observed information. Returns a `StataResults`; a robust or clustered VCE is refused because Stata's `mle` estimator does not accept one.

**Signature**

```python
ws.RandomEffectsMLE(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, entity: 'str', time: 'str', weights: 'Any' = None, weight_type: 'str | None' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`RandomEffectsMLE.from_formula`**

```python
RandomEffectsMLE.from_formula(formula: 'str', data: 'pd.DataFrame', *, entity: 'str', time: 'str', subset: 'Any' = None, weights: 'Any' = None, weight_type: 'str | None' = None) -> "'PanelOLS'"
```

**`RandomEffectsMLE.fit`**

```python
RandomEffectsMLE.fit(self, cov_type: 'str | None' = None, cov_kwds: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, ssc: 'SSC | None' = None, *, entity_effects: 'bool' = False, time_effects: 'bool' = False) -> 'StataResults'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.RandomEffectsMLE.from_formula('ypanel ~ x1 + x2', base, entity='firm', time='year').fit().summary(style='stata')
```

**Output**

```text

Random-effects ML regression                         Number of obs    =    200
Group variable: firm                                 Number of groups =     40

Random effects u_i ~ Gaussian                        Obs per group:
                                                                  min =      .
                                                                  avg =      .
                                                                  max =      .

                                                     LR chi2(2)       =  82.25
Log likelihood = -308.62872                          Prob > chi2      = 0.0000

------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      ypanel | Coefficient  Std. err.      z    P>|z|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
          x1 |   .7282049    .072845    10.00   0.000     .5854313    .8709784
          x2 |  -.1199841   .0780419    -1.54   0.124    -.2729434    .0329752
       _cons |   .8439937   .1404197     6.01   0.000     .5687762    1.119211
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
    /sigma_u |  .77026643
    /sigma_e |  .98414177
         rho |  .37987779
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
LR test of sigma_u=0: chibar2(01) = 39.08             Prob >= chibar2 = 0.0000
```

### `Rcm`

**Stata:** `rcm depvar predictors, unit() time() trunit() trperiod()`

Regression control method (HCW-style): fits a pre-treatment model for the treated unit, predicts the counterfactual, and reports `att` with the selected predictor set. `method` chooses the forward/backward search, `criterion` the information criterion. `fit()` accepts no `cov_type`: Stata's command has no `vce()` option, so the variance is part of the estimator. Construction goes through `from_variables(...)` rather than `from_formula(...)`, because Stata spells these commands with a variable list (R2.7, R3.2).

**Signature**

```python
ws.Rcm(response: 'Any', predictors: 'Any', unit: 'Any', time: 'Any', *, treated_unit: 'Any', treatment_period: 'float', method: 'str' = 'forward', criterion: 'str' = 'aicc', selected: 'Sequence[Any] | None' = None, max_predictors: 'int | None' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`Rcm.fit`**

```python
Rcm.fit(self) -> 'RcmResult'
```

**Example**

```python
print('Rcm 需要单位-时间面板与 treated_unit/treatment_period；见 GUIDE')
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

### `RcmResult`

**Stata:** `rcm`

The object `ws.rcm` returns: the `att`, the selected `predictors`, the fitted `coefficients`, the pre/post years, the observed and `predicted` paths and the per-period `effects`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.RcmResult(att: 'float', unit: 'str', treatment_period: 'float', predictors: 'tuple[str, ...]', coefficients: 'np.ndarray', pre_years: 'np.ndarray', post_years: 'np.ndarray', actual: 'np.ndarray', predicted: 'np.ndarray', method: 'str' = 'forward', criterion: 'str' = 'aicc') -> None
```

**Example**

```python
print('RcmResult is returned by ws.rcm; it needs a unit/time panel')
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

### `RdRobustResult`

**Stata:** `rdrobust`

The object `rdrobust` returns: the bias-corrected point estimate, its standard error, z statistic, p-value and confidence bounds, plus the bandwidth that produced them.

**Signature**

```python
ws.RdRobustResult(tau: 'float', cutoff: 'float', bandwidth: 'float', bias_bandwidth: 'float', nobs: 'int', nobs_left: 'int', nobs_right: 'int', nobs_effective_left: 'int', nobs_effective_right: 'int', order: 'int', bias_order: 'int', kernel: 'str', coef_covs: 'Any' = None, se: 'float' = nan, z: 'float' = nan, pvalue: 'float' = nan, ci_lower: 'float' = nan, ci_upper: 'float' = nan, variance_matches_stata: 'bool' = False) -> None
```

**Example**

```python
r = ws.rdrobust(base.y.to_numpy(), base.x1.to_numpy(), bandwidth=0.5)
print(round(r.tau, 6))
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

### `Rdrobust`

**Stata:** `rdrobust depvar running`

Local-polynomial regression-discontinuity estimate: `tau` is the bias-corrected jump at `cutoff`, `se` its robust standard error, plus the effective sample sizes per side. `bandwidth` may be given explicitly (MSE-optimal selection is not implemented and says so). `fit()` accepts no `cov_type`: Stata's command has no `vce()` option, so the variance is part of the estimator. Construction goes through `from_variables(...)` rather than `from_formula(...)`, because Stata spells these commands with a variable list (R2.7, R3.2).

**Signature**

```python
ws.Rdrobust(response: 'Any', running: 'Any', *, cutoff: 'float' = 0.0, bandwidth: 'float | None' = None, bias_bandwidth: 'float | None' = None, order: 'int' = 1, bias_order: 'int' = 2, kernel: 'str' = 'triangular', covs: 'Any' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`Rdrobust.fit`**

```python
Rdrobust.fit(self) -> 'RdRobustResult'
```

**Example**

```python
r = ws.Rdrobust.from_variables('y', 'x1', base, bandwidth=0.5).fit()
print(round(float(r.tau), 6))
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

### `SummarizeResult`

**Stata:** `estat summarize -- summarize of the estimation sample`

The `estat summarize` table: a per-variable summary of the ESTIMATION sample, not of the whole frame. Fields: `table` (a DataFrame indexed by variable with `Obs`, `Mean`, `Std. Dev.`, `Min`, `Max`), `n_obs` (the estimation sample size), `labels`, `clusters` and `noheader`. Because it summarises the rows the fit actually used, its `Obs` column can be smaller than `len(base)` after listwise deletion.

**Signature**

```python
ws.SummarizeResult(table: 'pd.DataFrame', n_obs: 'float', clusters: 'Mapping[str, Any] | None' = None, noheader: 'bool' = False, labels: 'Mapping[str, str] | None' = None) -> 'None'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.estat_summarize(ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()).to_frame()
```

**Output**

```text
            Obs      Mean  Std. Dev.       Min       Max
Variable                                                
y         200.0  1.020667   1.447346 -2.752204  4.881985
x1        200.0  0.057340   1.025868 -2.832134  3.278208
x2        200.0  0.063540   0.993631 -2.226171  3.259538
```

```python
ws.SummarizeTable(rows: 'list[tuple[str, float, float, float, float, float]]') -> None
```

### `Synth`

**Stata:** `synth depvar predictors, unit() time() trunit() trperiod()`

Synthetic control: builds a weighted donor pool matching the treated unit before treatment, then reports the post-treatment gap and `RMSPE`. The optional optimiser switches (`margin`, `sigf`, `bound`, `nested`, `allopt`) map onto `synth.ado`; graphics-only options raise instead of being silently ignored. `fit()` accepts no `cov_type`: Stata's command has no `vce()` option, so the variance is part of the estimator. Construction goes through `from_variables(...)` rather than `from_formula(...)`, because Stata spells these commands with a variable list (R2.7, R3.2).

**Signature**

```python
ws.Synth(data: 'Any', unit: 'str', time: 'str', depvar: 'str', predictors: 'Sequence[str]', trunit: 'Any', trperiod: 'float', counit: 'Sequence[Any] | None' = None, xperiod: 'Sequence[float] | None' = None, mspeperiod: 'Sequence[float] | None' = None, resultsperiod: 'Sequence[float] | None' = None, customV: 'Sequence[float] | None' = None, unitnames: 'str | None' = None, margin: 'float' = 0.005, maxiter: 'int' = 1000, sigf: 'int' = 12, bound: 'float' = 10, nested: 'bool' = False, allopt: 'bool' = False, technique: 'str | None' = None, iterate: 'int | None' = None, nrtolerance: 'float | None' = None, nonrtolerance: 'bool' = False, difficult: 'bool' = False, search: 'str | None' = None, trace: 'bool' = False, keep: 'str | None' = None, replace: 'bool' = False, figure: 'bool' = False, placebo: 'bool' = False) -> 'None'
```

**`Synth.fit`**

```python
Synth.fit(self) -> 'SynthResult'
```

**Example**

```python
panel = base.assign(unit=base.firm, period=base.year)
print('Synth 需要平衡面板与 trunit/trperiod；见 GUIDE 的完整示例')
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

### `Synth2`

**Stata:** `synth2 depvar predictors, unit() time() trunit() trperiod()`

Synthetic control with the placebo and leave-one-out machinery of `synth2` v2.1.0: the estimation itself is delegated to `synth`, and the extra options (`placebo`, `loo`, `ctrlunit`, `preperiod`, `postperiod`) drive the inference blocks. Frame/graphics options are refused explicitly, as documented in its design spec. `fit()` accepts no `cov_type`: Stata's command has no `vce()` option, so the variance is part of the estimator. Construction goes through `from_variables(...)` rather than `from_formula(...)`, because Stata spells these commands with a variable list (R2.7, R3.2).

**Signature**

```python
ws.Synth2(data: 'Any', unit: 'str', time: 'str', depvar: 'str', predictors: 'Sequence[str]', trunit: 'Any', trperiod: 'float', counit: 'Sequence[Any] | None' = None, ctrlunit: 'Sequence[Any] | None' = None, preperiod: 'Sequence[float] | None' = None, postperiod: 'Sequence[float] | None' = None, xperiod: 'Sequence[float] | None' = None, mspeperiod: 'Sequence[float] | None' = None, customV: 'Sequence[float] | None' = None, nested: 'bool' = False, allopt: 'bool' = False, margin: 'float' = 0.005, maxiter: 'int' = 1000, sigf: 'int' = 12, bound: 'float' = 10, placebo: 'str | None' = None, loo: 'bool' = False, figure: 'bool' = False, nofigure: 'bool' = False, savegraph: 'str | None' = None, symbol: 'int' = 1, frame: 'str | None' = None, show: 'int | None' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`Synth2.fit`**

```python
Synth2.fit(self) -> 'Synth2Result'
```

**Example**

```python
print('Synth2 需要平衡面板与 trunit/trperiod；见 GUIDE 的完整示例')
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

### `Synth2Result`

**Stata:** `ereturn list -- the e() surface synth2 stores (synth's e() is wiped)`

Frozen dataclass holding the outcome of `synth2`. Field names mirror Stata's `e()` verbatim: scalars `N/T/T0/T1/K/J/rmse/r2/att`, matrices `bal` (K x 6), `U_wt`, `V_wt`, `mspe` (in-space placebo only), `pval` (in-space placebo only), trajectories `Y_treated`/`Y_synthetic`/`time_index` with a `gap` property, placebo fields (`placebo_probability`, `placebo_removed`, `mspe_names`, `time_placebo`), LOO envelopes (`loo_min`/`loo_max`/`loo_effect_min`/`loo_effect_max`/`loo_n_runs`), and audit fields (`synth_result`, `method`, `numerically_inactive`). `summary()` renders a compact Stata-style report. It is NOT a `StataResults`: no `params`, `bse`, `cov_params` or `df_resid` are fabricated. The 6 `bal` columns are `Vweight, Treated, ValueSyntheticControl, BiasSyntheticControl, ValueAverageControl, BiasAverageControl`; `Bias% = (Value/Treated - 1)*100` (ado:181).

**Signature**

```python
ws.Synth2Result(N: 'int', T: 'int', T0: 'int', T1: 'int', K: 'int', J: 'int', rmse: 'float', r2: 'float', att: 'float', bal: 'np.ndarray', U_wt: 'np.ndarray', V_wt: 'np.ndarray', mspe: 'np.ndarray | None' = None, pval: 'np.ndarray | None' = None, Y_treated: 'np.ndarray | None' = None, Y_synthetic: 'np.ndarray | None' = None, time_index: 'np.ndarray | None' = None, placebo_probability: 'float | None' = None, placebo_removed: 'tuple[Any, ...]' = (), mspe_names: 'tuple[str, ...]' = (), time_placebo: 'dict[float, dict[str, np.ndarray]]' = <factory>, loo_min: 'np.ndarray | None' = None, loo_max: 'np.ndarray | None' = None, loo_effect_min: 'np.ndarray | None' = None, loo_effect_max: 'np.ndarray | None' = None, loo_n_runs: 'int' = 0, loo_units: 'tuple[Any, ...]' = (), synth_result: 'SynthResult | None' = None, method: 'str' = 'regression', numerically_inactive: 'tuple[str, ...]' = ()) -> None
```

**Example**

```python
units = [1, 2, 3, 4, 5]
years = np.arange(1997, 2006)
u = np.repeat(units, len(years))
t = np.tile(years, len(units))
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
x1 = 0.5 * u + rng.normal(size=len(u))
x2 = 0.2 * u + rng.normal(size=len(u))
y = 1.0 + 0.8 * x1 - 0.4 * x2 + 0.1 * (t - 1997) + 5.0 * (u == 1) * (t >= 2005) + rng.normal(size=len(u))
panel = pd.DataFrame({'unit': u, 'year': t, 'x1': x1, 'x2': x2, 'y': y})
res = ws.synth2(panel, unit='unit', time='year', depvar='y', predictors=['x1', 'x2'], trunit=1, trperiod=2005)
res.bal
```

**Output**

```text
[[  0.757511   0.851994   1.094422  28.454142   1.70489  100.105787]
 [  0.242489   0.870713   0.700712 -19.524351   0.678917 -22.02742 ]]
```

### `SynthResult`

**Stata:** `ereturn list -- the e() matrices synth stores`

Frozen dataclass holding the outcome of `synth`. Field names mirror Stata's `e()` matrices verbatim: `V_matrix` (K x K diagonal predictor weights -- note there is no plain `e(V)`), `W_weights`, `X_balance`, `RMSPE`, `Y_treated` and `Y_synthetic`, all reachable together through the `e` property. `gap` is the treatment-effect path `Y_treated - Y_synthetic`, `summary()` renders a compact Stata-style report, and `W_exact` keeps the unrounded QP solution for diagnostics. It is NOT a `StataResults`, so it carries no `params`, `bse` or `cov_params`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.SynthResult(V_matrix: 'np.ndarray', W_weights: 'np.ndarray', X_balance: 'np.ndarray', RMSPE: 'float', Y_treated: 'np.ndarray', Y_synthetic: 'np.ndarray', unit_names: 'tuple[Any, ...]', predictor_names: 'tuple[str, ...]', time_index: 'np.ndarray', treated_unit: 'Any', treatment_period: 'float', T0: 'int', W_exact: 'np.ndarray', missing_warnings: 'tuple[str, ...]' = (), method: 'str' = 'regression', optimizer_settings: 'dict[str, float | int] | None' = None, qp_solver: 'str' = 'SLSQP', unit_labels: 'tuple[str, ...]' = (), unit_codes: 'tuple[Any, ...]' = (), numerically_inactive: 'tuple[str, ...]' = (), stata_predictor_names: 'tuple[str, ...]' = ()) -> None
```

**Example**

```python
units = [1, 2, 3, 4, 5]
years = np.arange(1997, 2006)
u = np.repeat(units, len(years))
t = np.tile(years, len(units))
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
x1 = 0.5 * u + rng.normal(size=len(u))
x2 = 0.2 * u + rng.normal(size=len(u))
y = 1.0 + 0.8 * x1 - 0.4 * x2 + 0.1 * (t - 1997) + 5.0 * (u == 1) * (t >= 2005) + rng.normal(size=len(u))
panel = pd.DataFrame({'unit': u, 'year': t, 'x1': x1, 'x2': x2, 'y': y})
res = ws.synth(panel, unit='unit', time='year', depvar='y', predictors=['x1', 'x2'], trunit=1, trperiod=2005)
res.V_matrix
```

**Output**

```text
[[0.757511 0.      ]
 [0.       0.242489]]
```

### `TeffectsNnmatch`

**Stata:** `teffects nnmatch (depvar) (treatment) (covariates)`

Nearest-neighbour matching on Mahalanobis distance, with an Abadie-Imbens style bias correction. `fit()` accepts no `cov_type`: Stata's command has no `vce()` option, so the variance is part of the estimator. Construction goes through `from_variables(...)` rather than `from_formula(...)`, because Stata spells these commands with a variable list (R2.7, R3.2).

**Signature**

```python
ws.TeffectsNnmatch(response: 'Any', treatment: 'Any', covariates: 'Any', *, nneighbor: 'int' = 1, caliper: 'float | None' = None, tie_tolerance: 'float' = 1e-10) -> 'None'
```


### `TeffectsPsmatch`

**Stata:** `teffects psmatch (depvar) (treatment) (covariates)`

Propensity-score matching (ATET): the propensity comes from a logit of the treatment on the covariates with an intercept. Ties at the k-th smallest distance are ALL included, so the realised match count can exceed `nneighbor`. `fit()` accepts no `cov_type`: Stata's command has no `vce()` option, so the variance is part of the estimator. Construction goes through `from_variables(...)` rather than `from_formula(...)`, because Stata spells these commands with a variable list (R2.7, R3.2).

**Signature**

```python
ws.TeffectsPsmatch(response: 'Any', treatment: 'Any', covariates: 'Any', *, nneighbor: 'int' = 1, tie_tolerance: 'float' = 1e-09, outcome: 'str' = '', caliper: 'float | None' = None) -> 'None'
```

**`TeffectsPsmatch.fit`**

```python
TeffectsPsmatch.fit(self) -> 'PsmatchResult'
```

**Example**

```python
r = ws.TeffectsPsmatch.from_variables('y', 'd', base.assign(d=(base.y > 0).astype(int)), covariates=['x1']).fit()
print(round(float(r.ate), 6))
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

### `TeffectsRa`

**Stata:** `teffects ra (depvar covariates) (treatment)`

Regression-adjustment treatment effects: one outcome regression per arm, counterfactuals averaged over the sample. Returns `ate`/`atet`, the potential-outcome means (`po_0`, `po_1`), and standard errors; `estimand="atet"` switches to the treated-population effect. `fit()` accepts no `cov_type`: Stata's command has no `vce()` option, so the variance is part of the estimator. Construction goes through `from_variables(...)` rather than `from_formula(...)`, because Stata spells these commands with a variable list (R2.7, R3.2).

**Signature**

```python
ws.TeffectsRa(response: 'Any', treatment: 'Any', covariates: 'Any', *, estimand: 'str' = 'ate', add_constant: 'bool' = True) -> 'None'
```

**`TeffectsRa.fit`**

```python
TeffectsRa.fit(self) -> 'TefectsRaResult'
```

**Example**

```python
r = ws.TeffectsRa.from_variables('y', 'd', base.assign(d=(base.y > 0).astype(int)), covariates=['x1']).fit()
print(round(float(r.ate), 6))
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

### `VARResults`

**Stata:** `var`

The fitted VAR: `params` is Stata's equation-by-equation coefficient TABLE (a DataFrame, not a flat vector), `bse`, `sigma_u`, `cov_params()` and `e`, plus `irf()`, `fevd()` and `granger_causality()`. It is not a `StataResults`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.VARResults(_result: 'Any', names: 'list[str]', lags: 'int', trend: 'str', ml: 'bool' = True) -> None
```

**Example**

```python
r = ws.VAR(base[['y', 'x1']].to_numpy(), lags=1).fit()
print(r.params.shape)
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

### `VECMResults`

**Stata:** `vec`

The fitted VECM: `alpha` and `beta` blocks, `cov_params()`, `conf_int()`, `summary()` and the underlying statsmodels result. The reported drift differs from Stata's printed `_cons`; see the `VECM` entry.

**Signature**

```python
ws.VECMResults(raw: 'Any', names: 'list[str]', lags: 'int', coint_rank: 'int', deterministic: 'str', endog: 'Any' = None) -> None
```

**Example**

```python
r = ws.VECM(base[['y', 'x1']].to_numpy(), coint_rank=1, lags=2).fit()
print(r.conf_int().shape)
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

### `VarGrangerResult`

**Stata:** `vargranger`

The Granger-causality table: one row per (caused, causing) pair with the Wald chi-square, its degrees of freedom and p-value. `str()` prints Stata's boxed table.

**Signature**

```python
ws.VarGrangerResult(rows: 'list[_Row]', equations: 'tuple[str, ...]') -> None
```

**Example**

```python
r = ws.vargranger(ws.VAR(base[['y', 'x1']].to_numpy(), lags=1).fit())
print(len(r.rows))
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

### `VarSocResult`

**Stata:** `varsoc`

The lag-order selection table: one row per candidate lag with the AIC, HQIC, SBIC and FPE that Stata prints. `to_frame()` gives the numbers.

**Signature**

```python
ws.VarSocResult(rows: 'tuple[VarSocRow, ...]', nobs: 'int', equations: 'int', sample_start: 'int', sample_end: 'int', names: 'tuple[str, ...]' = ()) -> None
```

```python
r = ws.varsoc(base[['y', 'x1']].to_numpy(), maxlag=3)
print(r.to_frame())
```

```text
None
```

```python
r = ws.varstable(ws.VAR(base[['y', 'x1']].to_numpy(), lags=1).fit())
print(r.to_frame())
```

```text
None
```

### `WeakRobustResult`

**Stata:** `estat weakrobust`

The weak-instrument-robust inference result: the Anderson-Rubin `statistic`, its `df`, the F form and the p-value.

**Signature**

```python
ws.WeakRobustResult(test: 'str', statistic: 'float', f_statistic: 'float', df: 'float', df_denom: 'float', pvalue: 'float') -> None
```

**Example**

```python
print('WeakRobustResult is returned by estat_weakrobust; see its entry')
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

### `XtSerialResult`

**Stata:** `xtserial`

The Wooldridge panel serial-correlation test. The five `r()` scalars are `corr` (the differenced residual's lag coefficient, about -0.5 under the null), `f` with `df`/`df_r` for the Wald test that it equals -0.5, and `p`. Also `nobs` (the first-stage sample Stata prints), `n_panels`, and `first_stage` -- the differenced regression itself as a `StataResults` -- plus `first_stage_table()` for its rendered table. `str()` reproduces Stata's three-line test block.

**Signature**

```python
ws.XtSerialResult(corr: 'float', f: 'float', df: 'int', df_r: 'int', p: 'float', nobs: 'int', n_panels: 'int', first_stage: 'Any' = None) -> None
```

**Example**

```python
d = base.assign(pid=(np.arange(len(base)) % 12) + 1, period=(np.arange(len(base)) // 12) + 1)
r = ws.xtserial(d, 'y ~ x1 + x2', entity='pid', time='period')
print(round(r.f, 6), r.df_r)
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

### `XtTest0Result`

**Stata:** `xttest0`

The Breusch-Pagan LM test for random effects: `chibar2`, its p-value, and the variance components `var_u`, `var_e`, `var_y`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.XtTest0Result(chibar2: 'float', pvalue: 'float', var_u: 'float', var_e: 'float', var_y: 'float', nobs: 'int', entities: 'int', periods: 'int', depvar: 'str' = '', entity_name: 'str' = '', time_name: 'str' = '') -> None
```

**Example**

```python
fit = ws.RandomEffects.from_formula('y ~ x1', base, entity='firm', time='year').fit()
r = ws.xttest0(fit)
print(round(r.chibar2, 6))
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

**Stata:** `(no Stata equivalent -- the mtest() adjustment arithmetic)`

Adjust a vector of p-values for multiple testing, using one of the methods Stata's `mtest()` offers. Returns an array of the same length; `MTEST_METHODS` lists the accepted spellings.

```python
ws.adjust_pvalues(pvalues: 'Sequence[float]', method: 'str') -> 'np.ndarray'
```



### `dfgls`

**Stata:** `dfgls varname`

DF-GLS (Elliott-Rothenberg-Stock) unit-root test: a GLS-detrended Dickey-Fuller, which has better power than the ordinary ADF near the unit root. The reported statistic and critical values come from the ERS tables, not the Dickey-Fuller ones.

**Signature**

```python
ws.dfgls(series: 'Any', *, maxlag: 'int | None' = None, trend: 'bool' = True, name: 'str' = '', **legacy: 'Any') -> 'DFGLSResult'
```

**Example**

```python
print(ws.dfgls(base['y'].to_numpy()))
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

### `dfuller`

**Stata:** `dfuller y, lags(2)`

Augmented Dickey-Fuller unit-root test, spelled the way Stata spells it. A thin alias for `unitroot`: `dfuller(y, trend=True)` is `unitroot(y, test='adf', regression='ct')`, no option gives `regression='c'`, and `regression=` overrides `trend`. `lags=` maps to Stata's `lags(#)` (zero lags by default). Returns a `UnitRootResult` whose printed form is Stata's Dickey-Fuller table, with the statistic under `r(Zt)` and the MacKinnon p-value under `r(p)`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.dfuller(endog: 'Any', *, trend: 'bool' = False, lags: 'int | None' = None, autolag: 'str | None' = None, regression: 'str | None' = None) -> 'UnitRootResult'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.dfuller(base['y'], lags=2)
```

**Output**

```text

Augmented Dickey–Fuller test for unit root
Variable: y                               Number of obs  = 197
                                          Number of lags =   2

H0: Random walk without drift, d = 0

                                       Dickey–Fuller
                   Test      -------- critical value ---------
              statistic           1%           5%          10%
--------------------------------------------------------------
 Z(t)            -6.706       -3.478       -2.884       -2.574
--------------------------------------------------------------
MacKinnon approximate p-value for Z(t) = 0.0000.
```

The regression-based robust score test for endogeneity, built from an IV fit's structure. It is what `estat endogenous` runs under a robust VCE; `estat_endogenous(result)` is the command-level entry point.

**Signature**

```python
ws.endog_regf_test(structure: 'dict[str, Any]', response: 'np.ndarray', *, cov_type: 'str' = 'nonrobust', cluster_labels: 'Sequence[Any] | None' = None, tested: 'Sequence[str] | None' = None) -> 'RegFResult'
```

**Example**

```python
print('endog_regf_test takes an iv_structure dict; use estat_endogenous on a fitted result')
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

The robust score test for endogeneity computed from the residuals. Like `endog_regf_test`, this is the internals of `estat endogenous`; call that command on a fitted IV result instead.

```python
ws.endog_score_test(structure: 'dict[str, Any]', resid: 'np.ndarray', response: 'np.ndarray', *, hac: 'bool' = False, lags: 'int' = 0, tested: 'Sequence[str] | None' = None) -> 'EndogScoreResult'
```

**Example**

```python
print('endog_score_test takes an iv_structure dict; use estat_endogenous on a fitted result')
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

The parallel-trends test for a `didregress`/`xtdidregress` fit: it compares the pre-treatment trend of the treated group with the controls and returns the statistic, its degrees of freedom and the p-value.

### `etable_as_stata`

**Stata:** `esttab m1 m2, se r2 mtitle`

`etable` rendered the way Stata's `esttab` prints it. A convenience wrapper over `render_esttab` that forwards `model_names`, `digits`, `se`, `stars` and `stats`; `etable` itself returns a DataFrame because that is more useful to a program, while this returns the exact text a comparison against Stata needs. Both are kept deliberately -- neither substitutes for the other.

**Signature**

```python
ws.etable_as_stata(results: 'Sequence[Any]', *, model_names: 'Sequence[str] | None' = None, digits: 'int' = 3, se: 'bool' = True, stars: 'bool' = True, stats: 'Sequence[tuple[str, str]] | None' = None) -> 'str'
```

**Example**

```python
m1 = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1', base).fit()
m2 = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
ws.etable_as_stata([m1, m2], model_names=['m1', 'm2'], stats=[('N', 'nobs'), ('R-sq', 'r2')])
```

**Output**

```text

--------------------------------------------
                      (1)             (2)   
                       m1              m2   
                        y               y   
--------------------------------------------
x1                  0.843***        0.841***
                 (0.0804)        (0.0725)   

x2                                 -0.512***
                                 (0.0748)   

_cons               0.972***        1.005***
                 (0.0824)        (0.0744)   
--------------------------------------------
N                     200             200   
R-sq                0.357           0.480   
--------------------------------------------
Standard errors in parentheses
* p<0.05, ** p<0.01, *** p<0.001
```

### `format_ereturn_list`

**Stata:** `ereturn list`

The four-block dump Stata's `ereturn list` prints: scalars, macros, matrices, functions -- in that fixed order, each followed by a blank line. Scalars and macros use DIFFERENT separators (` =  ` with two spaces versus a single space and a colon), and the split between the two blocks is by TYPE: strings become macros, numbers become scalars, so a macro added later cannot be misfiled. Scalar values print up to 16 significant digits with no leading zero and no trailing zeros.

**Signature**

```python
ws.format_ereturn_list(result: 'Any') -> 'str'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.format_ereturn_list(ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1', base).fit())
```

**Output**

```text

scalars:
                  e(N) =  200
              e(sum_w) =  200
               e(df_m) =  1
               e(df_r) =  198
                  e(F) =  109.7490736414687
                 e(r2) =  .3566186969885982
               e(rmse) =  1.163859236421547
                e(mss) =  148.6626185459396
                e(rss) =  268.2045277963416
               e(r2_a) =  .3533692964683386
                 e(ll) =  -313.1309552730224
               e(ll_0) =  -357.2327276994888
               e(rank) =  2

macros:
              e(title) : "Linear regression"
          e(marginsok) : "XB default"
                e(vce) : "ols"
             e(depvar) : "y"
                e(cmd) : "regress"
         e(properties) : "b V"
            e(predict) : "regres_p"
              e(model) : "ols"
          e(estat_cmd) : "regress_estat"

matrices:
                  e(b) :  1 x 2
                  e(V) :  2 x 2
               e(beta) :  1 x 1

functions:
             e(sample)
```

### `kpss`

**Stata:** `kpss varname`

KPSS test for stationarity -- the null is the OPPOSITE of the unit-root tests beside it: `H0: the series is (trend) stationary`. `trend=True` tests around a deterministic trend, `False` around a constant. Read the p-value as evidence AGAINST stationarity, which trips people up next to `dfuller`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.kpss(values: 'Any', *, trend: 'bool' = True, maxlag: 'int | None' = None, name: 'str' = '') -> 'KpssResult'
```

**Example**

```python
print(ws.kpss(base['y'].to_numpy()))
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

### `lincom_as_stata`

**Stata:** `lincom`

The function behind `result.lincom(expression)`: form a linear combination of the coefficients, its standard error, the test statistic and the confidence interval. Use it directly when the expression is built at runtime rather than typed.

**Signature**

```python
ws.lincom_as_stata(result: 'Any', expression: 'str', *, level: 'float' = 0.95, eform: 'bool' = False) -> 'Any'
```

**Example**

```python
fit = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
r = ws.lincom_as_stata(fit, 'x1 + x2')
print(round(r.to_dict()['estimate'], 6))
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

### `pperron`

**Stata:** `pperron y, trend`

Phillips-Perron unit-root test, spelled the way Stata spells it. A thin alias for `unitroot`: `pperron(y, trend=True)` is `unitroot(y, test='pp', regression='ct')`, with the same option mapping as `dfuller`. Returns a `UnitRootResult`; the Newey-West lag truncation is chosen by the same default as `dfuller` when `lags=None`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.pperron(endog: 'Any', *, trend: 'bool' = False, lags: 'int | None' = None, autolag: 'str | None' = None, regression: 'str | None' = None) -> 'UnitRootResult'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.pperron(base['y'], trend=True)
```

**Output**

```text

Augmented Dickey–Fuller test for unit root
Variable: y                               Number of obs  = 199
                                          Number of lags =   4

H0: Random walk with or without drift

                                       Dickey–Fuller
                   Test      -------- critical value ---------
              statistic           1%           5%          10%
--------------------------------------------------------------
 Z(t)           -14.483       -4.007       -3.437       -3.137
--------------------------------------------------------------
MacKinnon approximate p-value for Z(t) = 0.0000.
```

### `prais`

**Stata:** `prais depvar indepvars`

Prais-Winsten AR(1) regression: GLS after transforming the data by the estimated autocorrelation, iterated until `rho` settles. The transformation keeps the first observation (that is what distinguishes Prais-Winsten from Cochrane-Orcutt, which drops it), so the sample size is the full one.

**Signature**

```python
ws.prais(data: 'pd.DataFrame', formula: 'str', *, maxiter: 'int' = 100, tolerance: 'float' = 1e-06, corc: 'bool' = False) -> 'PraisResult'
```

**Example**

```python
print(str(ws.prais(base, 'y ~ x1'))[:180])
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

### `prais_winsten`

**Stata:** `(the estimator behind prais; no separate Stata command)`

The Prais-Winsten / Cochrane-Orcutt AR(1) solver on raw arrays: returns the coefficients, the estimated rho, the iteration count and the rho trace. `ws.prais` is the formula-level wrapper around it.

**Signature**

```python
ws.prais_winsten(exog: 'np.ndarray', endog: 'np.ndarray', *, maxiter: 'int' = 100, tolerance: 'float' = 1e-06, rho: 'float | None' = None, corc: 'bool' = False) -> 'tuple[np.ndarray, float, int, list[tuple[int, float]]]'
```

**Example**

```python
coef, rho, iters, trace = ws.prais_winsten(base.x1.to_numpy()[:, None], base.y.to_numpy())
print(round(rho, 6), iters)
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

### `rcm`

**Stata:** `rcm depvar indepvars, trunit(#) trperiod(#)`

Regression control method for a single treated unit: fit the treated unit's pre-period outcome on control units' outcomes, then average the post-period gap. `selected=` names the control units directly and reproduces Stata exactly when the same set is chosen. The FORWARD SEARCH is not the same algorithm Stata uses -- pass `selected=` for a reproducible result.

**Signature**

```python
ws.rcm(response: 'Any', predictors: 'Any', unit: 'Any', time: 'Any', *, treated_unit: 'Any', treatment_period: 'float', method: 'str' = 'forward', criterion: 'str' = 'aicc', selected: 'Sequence[Any] | None' = None, max_predictors: 'int | None' = None) -> 'RcmResult'
```

**Example**

```python
d = base.assign(unit=np.repeat(np.arange(20), 10), t=np.tile(np.arange(10), 20))
r = ws.rcm(d['y'], d[['x1']], d['unit'], d['t'], treated_unit=0, treatment_period=6, selected=[1, 2])
round(r.att, 6)
```

**Output**

```text
-0.596186
```

**Notes:** The automatic model search is NOT aligned with Stata. Stata's own choice fits the pre-period far worse than the exhaustive optimum, because its regression setup differs from any reconstruction attempted here; a test asserts the mismatch so the two paths are not mistaken for equivalent.

### `rdrobust`

**Stata:** `rdrobust depvar runvar, c(#)`

Sharp regression-discontinuity estimate: two local polynomial fits, one each side of the cutoff, differenced at the cutoff. The POINT ESTIMATE is exact (verified on real data to nine significant digits). `bandwidth` must be supplied -- Stata's MSE-optimal selector is not implemented, so `None` raises rather than silently choosing one.

**Signature**

```python
ws.rdrobust(response: 'Any', running: 'Any', *, cutoff: 'float' = 0.0, bandwidth: 'float | None' = None, bias_bandwidth: 'float | None' = None, order: 'int' = 1, bias_order: 'int' = 2, kernel: 'str' = 'triangular', covs: 'Any' = None) -> 'RdRobustResult'
```

**Example**

```python
x = base['x1'].to_numpy()
y = 2.0 * (x > 0) + 0.5 * x + base['x2'].to_numpy() * 0.1
round(ws.rdrobust(y, x, cutoff=0.0, bandwidth=0.8).tau, 4)
```

**Output**

```text
1.9415
```

**Notes:** The robust (CCT) standard error is not reproduced: `rdrobust_vce` exists only in the compiled lrdrobust.mlib, so se/z/ci are NaN. The assembly step around it IS verified against Stata's own e(V_cl_l)/e(V_cl_r).

### `relabel_cov_type`

**Stata:** `(no Stata equivalent -- an internal bookkeeping method)`

Record the covariance type actually used after the matrix has been replaced. `Areg` fits with a placeholder and then builds the real HC2/HC3 matrix on the full design, so it relabels the result here; a caller reading `result.cov_type` must see what was computed, not the placeholder.

**Signature**

```python
ws.relabel_cov_type(self, cov_type: 'str') -> 'None'
```

**Example**

```python
from westata.protocols import SSC
fit = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1', base).fit(cov_type='HC1')
print(fit.cov_type)
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

### `render_estimates_table`

**Stata:** `estimates table m1 m2 ..., b se`

Stacks any number of fitted models side by side in Stata's `estimates table` layout, a different shape from `esttab`: no column numbers, no dependent-variable row, and a `Legend: b/se` line closes the table. Coefficients sit on one row and their standard errors on the row beneath, per model column.

**Signature**

```python
ws.render_estimates_table(results: 'Sequence[Any]', *, model_names: 'Sequence[str] | None' = None, se: 'bool' = True, star: 'Sequence[float] | None' = None, mtitle: 'bool' = False, stats: 'Sequence[str] | None' = None, pvalues: 'bool' = False) -> 'str'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.render_estimates_table([ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1', base).fit(), ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()], model_names=['a', 'b'])
```

**Output**

```text

----------------------------------------
    Variable |     a            b       
-------------+--------------------------
          x1 |  .84252495    .84064454  
             |  .08042338    .07247108  
          x2 |              -.51208701  
             |                .0748223  
       _cons |  .97235675    1.0050025  
             |  .08242638    .07442849  
----------------------------------------
                            Legend: b/se
```

**Notes:** Geometry is derived, not tabulated: a 14-character gutter then 13 columns per model, so the rule is 14 + 13*n characters and '+' sits at index 13. Names are centred and abbreviated past ten characters. Values use eight significant figures at or above 1 and eight decimals below. NOTE the VCE convention: Stata's `regress, r` is HC1 while `ivregress ..., r` is HC0, so a table mixing OLS and IV needs cov_type='HC1' for the OLS fits and 'HC0' for the IV ones.

### `render_esttab`

**Stata:** `esttab m1 m2, se r2 mtitle`

Render one or more fitted results as the TEXT Stata's `esttab` prints: a `(1) (2)` numbering row, an `mtitle` row when `model_names` is given, coefficient rows with standard errors in parentheses and significance stars, plus a footer of statistics. `stats` is a sequence of `(label, source)` pairs and defaults to `N` from `nobs`; `stats_digits` controls the footer's decimals. Unlike `etable`, which returns a DataFrame for programs, this returns the printable string. At most two models fit the measured 44-column layout.

**Signature**

```python
ws.render_esttab(results: 'Sequence[Any]', *, model_names: 'Sequence[str] | None', digits: 'int' = 3, se: 'bool' = True, stars: 'bool' = True, stats: 'Sequence[tuple[str, Any]] | None' = None, stats_digits: 'int' = 3) -> 'str'
```

**Example**

```python
m1 = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1', base).fit()
m2 = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
ws.render_esttab([m1, m2], model_names=['m1', 'm2'], stats=[('N', 'nobs'), ('R-sq', 'r2')])
```

**Output**

```text

--------------------------------------------
                      (1)             (2)   
                       m1              m2   
                        y               y   
--------------------------------------------
x1                  0.843***        0.841***
                 (0.0804)        (0.0725)   

x2                                 -0.512***
                                 (0.0748)   

_cons               0.972***        1.005***
                 (0.0824)        (0.0744)   
--------------------------------------------
N                     200             200   
R-sq                0.357           0.480   
--------------------------------------------
Standard errors in parentheses
* p<0.05, ** p<0.01, *** p<0.001
```

### `summarize`

**Stata:** `summarize varlist`

Stata's plain `summarize` table: for each variable, the non-missing count, mean, standard deviation, minimum and maximum in one 71-character row. This is the SIX-column form; `estat_summarize` is the estimation-sample variant with no Obs column and a different header, so the two are not interchangeable. Values print with `%9.0g`, which is why `106.2666` and `29.61636` carry different decimal counts in the same column. `weights` switches to frequency weights, in which case the Obs cell reports the weight sum as Stata does.

**Signature**

```python
ws.summarize(data: 'Any', variables: 'Sequence[str] | None' = None, *, weights: 'Any' = None, detail: 'bool' = False) -> 'Any'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.summarize(base, ['y', 'x1'])
```

**Output**

```text

    Variable |        Obs        Mean    Std. dev.       Min        Max
-------------+---------------------------------------------------------
           y |        200    1.020667    1.447346  -2.752204   4.881985
          x1 |        200    .0573402    1.025868  -2.832134   3.278208
```

### `supported_types`

**Stata:** `(the predict type list a model accepts)`

The tuples of `predict` kinds a given model supports, e.g. `supported_types('regress')`. Asking for a type outside this list raises rather than silently returning another quantity.

**Signature**

```python
ws.supported_types(model_name: 'str') -> 'tuple[str, ...]'
```

**Example**

```python
print(ws.supported_types('regress'))
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

### `synth`

**Stata:** `synth y x1 x2, trunit(1) trperiod(2005)`

Synthetic control estimation (Stata `synth`, default regression-based V path). Builds a weighted combination of the control units that matches the treated unit on pre-intervention predictor means, then uses it as the counterfactual. `unit`/`time`/`depvar` name the panel columns, `predictors` the matching variables, `trunit`/`trperiod` the treated unit and intervention period. Returns a `SynthResult` frozen dataclass; because `synth` has no coefficient vector, no `e(V)` and no degrees of freedom, those statsmodels-style attributes are deliberately absent. The example builds a small balanced 5-unit x 9-period panel inline, so it needs no external .dta file.

**Signature**

```python
ws.synth(data: 'Any', *, unit: 'str', time: 'str', depvar: 'str', predictors: 'Sequence[str]', trunit: 'Any', trperiod: 'float', counit: 'Sequence[Any] | None' = None, xperiod: 'Sequence[float] | None' = None, mspeperiod: 'Sequence[float] | None' = None, resultsperiod: 'Sequence[float] | None' = None, customV: 'Sequence[float] | None' = None, unitnames: 'str | None' = None, margin: 'float' = 0.005, maxiter: 'int' = 1000, sigf: 'int' = 12, bound: 'float' = 10, nested: 'bool' = False, allopt: 'bool' = False, technique: 'str | None' = None, iterate: 'int | None' = None, nrtolerance: 'float | None' = None, nonrtolerance: 'bool' = False, difficult: 'bool' = False, search: 'str | None' = None, trace: 'bool' = False, keep: 'str | None' = None, replace: 'bool' = False, figure: 'bool' = False, placebo: 'bool' = False, **unsupported: 'Any') -> 'SynthResult'
```

**Example**

```python
units = [1, 2, 3, 4, 5]
years = np.arange(1997, 2006)
u = np.repeat(units, len(years))
t = np.tile(years, len(units))
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
x1 = 0.5 * u + rng.normal(size=len(u))
x2 = 0.2 * u + rng.normal(size=len(u))
y = 1.0 + 0.8 * x1 - 0.4 * x2 + 0.1 * (t - 1997) + 5.0 * (u == 1) * (t >= 2005) + rng.normal(size=len(u))
panel = pd.DataFrame({'unit': u, 'year': t, 'x1': x1, 'x2': x2, 'y': y})
ws.synth(panel, unit='unit', time='year', depvar='y', predictors=['x1', 'x2'], trunit=1, trperiod=2005).RMSPE
```

**Output**

```text
1.3651043054505119
```

**Notes:** Only the DEFAULT path is implemented: `nested`/`allopt`, `figure`, `keep()`, `placebo` and inferential quantities are refused with `UnsupportedError`. `customV` supplies diagonal predictor weights; `xperiod`/`mspeperiod`/`resultsperiod` override the pre-period windows. Reported `W_weights` are rounded to 3 decimals as Stata does, while `RMSPE` uses the exact QP weights.

### `synth2`

**Stata:** `synth2 y x1 x2, trunit(1) trperiod(2005)`

Stata `synth2`'s NON-GRAPHICS feature set. `synth2` is a wrapper: all optimisation is delegated to `ws.synth`, and it adds fit statistics (`rmse`/`r2`/`att`), a 6-column covariate balance table (`bal`), non-zero unit weights (`U_wt`), the V diagonal (`V_wt`), in-space placebo (fake treatment units -> `mspe` + pointwise two/right/left p-values `pval`, with optional `cut()`), in-time placebo (`placebo('period(2001)')`, re-estimation only, no inference), and leave-one-out min/max envelopes (`loo=True`). Options map to Stata's: `counit`, `preperiod`, `postperiod`, `xperiod`, `mspeperiod`, `customV`, `nested`, `allopt`, and the four optimiser settings. Returns a `Synth2Result` frozen dataclass. Graphics-only options (`figure`/`nofigure`/`savegraph`/`show`) and Stata `frame()`/`symbol()` are deliberately refused/omitted; all results are attributes of the returned object. Evidence grade E2 by quantity: the formula layer (bal/U_wt/V_wt) reaches machine precision, while the re-fit chain inherits `ws.synth`'s rank-deficient-QP differences.

**Signature**

```python
ws.synth2(data: 'Any', *, unit: 'str', time: 'str', depvar: 'str', predictors: 'Sequence[str]', trunit: 'Any', trperiod: 'float', counit: 'Sequence[Any] | None' = None, ctrlunit: 'Sequence[Any] | None' = None, preperiod: 'Sequence[float] | None' = None, postperiod: 'Sequence[float] | None' = None, xperiod: 'Sequence[float] | None' = None, mspeperiod: 'Sequence[float] | None' = None, customV: 'Sequence[float] | None' = None, nested: 'bool' = False, allopt: 'bool' = False, margin: 'float' = 0.005, maxiter: 'int' = 1000, sigf: 'int' = 12, bound: 'float' = 10, placebo: 'str | None' = None, loo: 'bool' = False, figure: 'bool' = False, nofigure: 'bool' = False, savegraph: 'str | None' = None, symbol: 'int' = 1, frame: 'str | None' = None, show: 'int | None' = None, **unsupported: 'Any') -> 'Synth2Result'
```

**Example**

```python
units = [1, 2, 3, 4, 5]
years = np.arange(1997, 2006)
u = np.repeat(units, len(years))
t = np.tile(years, len(units))
rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
x1 = 0.5 * u + rng.normal(size=len(u))
x2 = 0.2 * u + rng.normal(size=len(u))
y = 1.0 + 0.8 * x1 - 0.4 * x2 + 0.1 * (t - 1997) + 5.0 * (u == 1) * (t >= 2005) + rng.normal(size=len(u))
panel = pd.DataFrame({'unit': u, 'year': t, 'x1': x1, 'x2': x2, 'y': y})
ws.synth2(panel, unit='unit', time='year', depvar='y', predictors=['x1', 'x2'], trunit=1, trperiod=2005).rmse
```

**Output**

```text
1.3651043054505119
```

**Notes:** `synth2.ado:330` calls `ereturn clear` and wipes `synth`'s e(); the Python result surface is synth2's own, plus `synth_result` for the raw fit. `R2` uses the SYNTHETIC outcome's pretreatment deviations as denominator (ado:647, non-standard). Aggregate probability and pointwise p-values INCLUDE the treated unit in the denominator with >= comparisons (ado:441/940/947-949). In-time placebo produces NO mspe/pval. `frame()`/`symbol()` are Stata-specific result-dataset mechanisms, replaced by Synth2Result attributes.

### `tebalance_summarize`

**Stata:** `tebalance summarize`

Covariate balance after a matching fit: each covariate's standardized difference and variance ratio, raw versus matched, plus the four observation counts. The formulas come from `_tebalance_summarize.ado`: standardized difference is `(m1 - m0) / sqrt((v1 + v0)/2)` and the variance ratio is `v1 / v0`, with the matched columns reweighting the control side by the matching weights. Pass the treated/control weights as `match_weights`.

**Signature**

```python
ws.tebalance_summarize(treatment: 'Any', covariates: 'Any', *, match_weights: 'Any' = None, names: 'list[str] | None' = None, exact: 'tuple[str, ...]' = (), normalize_counts: 'bool' = False) -> 'BalanceSummary'
```

**Example**

```python
ps = np.asarray(ws.Logit.from_formula('treat ~ x1 + x2', base).fit().predict())
c = np.flatnonzero(base['treat'].to_numpy() == 0)
w = np.zeros(len(base))
[w.__setitem__(c[np.abs(ps[i] - ps[c]).argmin()], 1.0) for i in np.flatnonzero(base['treat'].to_numpy() == 1)]
ws.tebalance_summarize(base['treat'].to_numpy(), base[['x1','x2']].to_numpy(), match_weights=w, names=['x1','x2'])
```

**Output**

```text
Covariate balance summary

                         Raw      Matched
-----------------------------------------
Number of obs =          200          179
Treated obs   =          128          128
Control obs   =           72           51
-----------------------------------------

-----------------------------------------------------------------
                |Standardized differences          Variance ratio
                |        Raw     Matched           Raw    Matched
----------------+------------------------------------------------
             x1 |  -.1960176   -.0278721      1.036638   1.192021
             x2 |  -.3042354   -.2882414      .7707397   .8606013
-----------------------------------------------------------------
```

**Notes:** On the reference nsw_dw fit, seven of eight covariates reproduce Stata to every printed digit; the eighth (`age`) differs because the tie weights are distributed slightly differently, and that residual is documented rather than hidden.

### `teffects_nnmatch`

**Stata:** `teffects nnmatch ..., atet`

ATET by nearest-neighbour matching on the Mahalanobis distance (Stata's default metric, `e(metric)` reads "mahalanobis"), using the inverse sample covariate covariance. The same tie rule as `teffects_psmatch` applies: all controls at the k-th smallest distance are used and averaged. `caliper` caps the distance; a treated unit with no control inside it raises rather than borrowing a distant one, which is Stata's "no matches" condition.

**Signature**

```python
ws.teffects_nnmatch(response: 'Any', treatment: 'Any', covariates: 'Any', *, nneighbor: 'int' = 1, caliper: 'float | None' = None, tie_tolerance: 'float' = 1e-10) -> 'NnmatchResult'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.teffects_nnmatch(base['ytreat'], base['treat'], base[['x1','x2','x3']].to_numpy(), nneighbor=4)
```

**Output**

```text
NnmatchResult(ate=1.4150728330730742, nobs=200, treated=128, controls=72, nneighbor=4, matches_min=4, matches_max=4, metric='mahalanobis', caliper=nan, se=nan, z=nan, pvalue=nan, ci_lower=nan, ci_upper=nan)
```

### `teffects_psmatch`

**Stata:** `teffects psmatch ..., atet`

ATET by propensity-score nearest-neighbour matching. The propensity score comes from a logit of the treatment on the covariates (Stata's default treatment model). Ties matter: every control at the k-th smallest distance is a match, not just one, which is what makes the number match Stata. On the reference data the n(1) tie count reaches 8 and the n(4) count reaches 11, and those counts are reported.

**Signature**

```python
ws.teffects_psmatch(response: 'Any', treatment: 'Any', covariates: 'Any', *, nneighbor: 'int' = 1, tie_tolerance: 'float' = 1e-09, outcome: 'str' = '', caliper: 'float | None' = None) -> 'PsmatchResult'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.teffects_psmatch(base['ytreat'], base['treat'], base[['x1','x2','x3']].to_numpy(), nneighbor=1)
```

**Output**

```text

Treatment-effects estimation                   Number of obs      =        200
Estimator      : propensity-score matching     Matches: requested =          1
Outcome model  : matching                                     min =          1
Treatment model: logit                                        max =          1
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
             |              AI robust
             | Coefficient  std. err.      z    P>|z|     [95% conf. interval]
-------------+----------------------------------------------------------------
ATET         |
       treat |
   (1 vs 0)  |      1.852          .        .       .            .           .
------------------------------------------------------------------------------
```

**Notes:** The Abadie-Imbens AI-robust standard error is not reproduced, so se/z/p/ci are NaN rather than a plausible guess.

### `teffects_ra`

**Stata:** `teffects ra (depvar covars) (treat)`

Regression-adjustment treatment effect: fit the outcome model separately in the treated and control groups, then average the predicted difference over the estimand's population (`ate` over everyone, `atet` over the treated). The point estimate is exact; the standard error is not reproduced because the sandwich is computed inside Stata's builtin `gmm`, so se/z/p/ci are NaN.

**Signature**

```python
ws.teffects_ra(response: 'Any', treatment: 'Any', covariates: 'Any', *, estimand: 'str' = 'ate', add_constant: 'bool' = True) -> 'TefectsRaResult'
```

**Example**

```python
print(ws.teffects_ra(base['y'] + base['yb'], base['yb'], base[['x1','x2']].to_numpy()).ate)
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

### `test_as_stata`

**Stata:** `test`

The function behind `result.test(...)`: run one or more Wald restrictions and return Stata's `test` output. Each argument is one restriction specification; a comma inside a single string is not a separator.

**Signature**

```python
ws.test_as_stata(result: 'Any', *specs: 'Any', coef: 'bool' = False) -> 'WaldTest | dict[str, Any]'
```

**Example**

```python
fit = ws.OLS.from_formula('y ~ x1 + x2', base).fit()
r = ws.test_as_stata(fit, 'x1', 'x2')
print(r)
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

### `vargranger`

**Stata:** `vargranger`

Granger causality Wald tests for a fitted VAR, as the boxed table Stata prints: one row per excluded variable under each equation's heading, then an ALL row that re-tests them jointly. Every row is 70 characters. The statistics come from the same Wald test `granger_causality` performs; note that `granger_causality(a, b)` reports the test in equation `a` excluding `b` -- the parameter is NAMED `causing` but holds the equation, and swapping them silently exchanges the two chi2 values.

**Signature**

```python
ws.vargranger(fitted: 'Any') -> 'VarGrangerResult'
```

**Example**

```python
ws.vargranger(ws.VAR(base[['y1','y2']].to_numpy(), lags=1, names=['y1','y2']).fit())
```

**Output**

```text
   Granger causality Wald tests
  +------------------------------------------------------------------+
  |          Equation           Excluded |   chi2     df Prob > chi2 |
  |--------------------------------------+---------------------------|
  |                y1                 y2 | .025404     1    0.873    |
  |                y1                ALL | .025404     1    0.873    |
  |--------------------------------------+---------------------------|
  |                y2                 y1 |  .55296     1    0.457    |
  |                y2                ALL |  .55296     1    0.457    |
  +------------------------------------------------------------------+
```

**Notes:** Each equation block ends with an ALL row even when only one variable is excluded; Stata prints it there and it repeats the single row exactly.

### `varlmar`

**Stata:** `varlmar`

Lagrange-multiplier test for residual autocorrelation in a fitted VAR: for lags 1 to `mlag`, whether the residuals are autocorrelated at that order. The null is no autocorrelation at the given lag.

**Signature**

```python
ws.varlmar(result: 'Any', *, mlag: 'int' = 2) -> 'LmarResult'
```

**Example**

```python
print(ws.varlmar(ws.VAR(base[['y1','y2']].to_numpy(), lags=1, names=['y1','y2']).fit(), mlag=1))
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

### `varsoc`

**Stata:** `varsoc varlist, maxlag(#)`

Lag-order selection criteria for a VAR: for each candidate order, the final prediction error, AIC, HQIC and SBIC, with the minimising order starred per criterion. The four criteria often disagree, and Stata stars each independently rather than picking one for you.

**Signature**

```python
ws.varsoc(data: 'np.ndarray | pd.DataFrame', *, maxlag: 'int' = 4, trend: 'str' = 'c', names: 'list[str] | None' = None) -> 'VarSocResult'
```

**Example**

```python
print(ws.varsoc(base[['y1','y2']].to_numpy(), maxlag=2))
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

### `varstable`

**Stata:** `varstable`

Eigenvalue stability condition for a fitted VAR: the companion matrix's moduli beside their eigenvalues. The process is stable when every modulus lies strictly inside the unit circle, and Stata flags any that do not.

**Signature**

```python
ws.varstable(result: 'Any') -> 'VarStableResult'
```

**Example**

```python
print(ws.varstable(ws.VAR(base[['y1','y2']].to_numpy(), lags=1, names=['y1','y2']).fit()))
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

### `varwle`

**Stata:** `varwle`

Wald lag-exclusion tests for a VAR: for each equation, whether each lag's coefficients are jointly zero, plus an ALL row. The statistic is a Wald chi2 built from the coefficient covariance, so it needs the full lag block rather than one coefficient at a time.

**Signature**

```python
ws.varwle(result: 'Any') -> 'VarWleResult'
```

**Example**

```python
print(ws.varwle(ws.VAR(base[['y1','y2']].to_numpy(), lags=1, names=['y1','y2']).fit()))
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

### `veclmar`

**Stata:** `veclmar`

Lagrange-multiplier test for residual autocorrelation in a fitted VECM, the VECM counterpart of `varlmar`. Run on the differenced residuals, so the lag numbering refers to differences, not levels.

**Signature**

```python
ws.veclmar(result: 'Any', *, mlag: 'int' = 2) -> 'LmarResult'
```

**Example**

```python
print(ws.veclmar(ws.VECM(base[['y1','y2']].to_numpy(), coint_rank=1, lags=2, names=['y1','y2']).fit(), mlag=1))
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

### `vecrank`

**Stata:** `vecrank varlist, lags(#)`

Johansen cointegration rank test, with the trace and maximum-eigenvalue forms and their 5% critical values. `lags` counts the VAR lag order as Stata does; `lags(1)` is handled separately because at one lag there are no lagged differences to regress out, so both sides of the reduced-rank regression are residualised on a constant alone.

**Signature**

```python
ws.vecrank(data: 'Any', *, lags: 'int' = 2, trend: 'str' = 'constant', show_max: 'bool' = False) -> 'VecRankResult'
```

**Example**

```python
print(ws.vecrank(base[['y1','y2']].to_numpy(), lags=1))
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

**Notes:** `trend` accepts only "constant"; the other Stata trend specifications are refused rather than approximated.

### `vecstable`

**Stata:** `vecstable`

Eigenvalue stability condition for a fitted VECM. Note the VECM must actually have a short-run block to check: with `lags=1` there is none and the check raises rather than returning an empty table.

**Signature**

```python
ws.vecstable(result: 'Any') -> 'VecStableResult'
```

**Example**

```python
print(ws.vecstable(ws.VECM(base[['y1','y2']].to_numpy(), coint_rank=1, lags=2, names=['y1','y2']).fit()))
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

### `xtserial`

**Stata:** `xtserial (user-written, Drukker)`

Wooldridge's test for serial correlation in linear panel-data models, the user-written `xtserial` of that name. It differences the response and every regressor (no constant, clustered on the panel), regresses those residuals on their own lag the same way, and tests that the lag coefficient equals -0.5 -- because differencing an AR(1) error leaves `rho - 1`, which is -0.5 when there is no serial correlation. `entity` and `time` play the role of `xtset`. The lag runs on the (panel, time) axis, so a missing period makes the difference missing on both sides of the gap. The first-stage regression -- what Stata prints under `, output` -- is returned on `.first_stage` as an ordinary `StataResults` rather than discarded; `keep_first_stage=False` drops it. Measured on lin_1992.dta: `corr = -0.192578493`, `F = 12.511374217`, `df_r = 27`, `p = 0.001484513`, and the first stage gives N = 420, `r2 = 0.5797`, `F = 902.61`, against StataNow 19.5.

**Signature**

```python
ws.xtserial(data: 'pd.DataFrame', formula: 'str', *, entity: 'str', time: 'str', keep_first_stage: 'bool' = True) -> 'XtSerialResult'
```

**Example**

```python
d = base.assign(pid=(np.arange(len(base)) % 12) + 1, period=(np.arange(len(base)) // 12) + 1)
print(ws.xtserial(d, 'y ~ x1 + x2', entity='pid', time='period'))
```

**Output**

```text
None
```

### `xttest0`

**Stata:** `xttest0 (after xtreg, re)`

Breusch-Pagan Lagrange-multiplier test for random effects: whether the panel variance component is zero. It is a test of `sigma_u = 0`, which is why it follows a RANDOM-effects fit -- a fixed-effects fit has already absorbed `u`, so the test does not apply.

**Signature**

```python
ws.xttest0(result: 'Any', *, entity: 'Any' = None) -> 'XtTest0Result'
```

**Example**

```python
d = base.assign(firm=np.repeat(np.arange(20), 10), t=np.tile(np.arange(10), 20))
f = ws.RandomEffects.from_formula('y ~ x1', d, entity='firm', time='t').fit()
print(ws.xttest0(f, entity=d['firm']))
```

**Output**

```text
None
```
